The model details (spatial structure, binary choices, group-based punishment) are informative for mapping to similar experimental designs.

- source: Chen, T., Hu, X. Z., Wang, Y. J., & Wang, L. (2018). The effect of heterogeneous defectors on the evolution of public cooperation. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment mechanisms in PGGs with heterogeneous defectors can significantly increase the sum of contributions (i.e., promote cooperation), especially when the punishment inflicted on defectors is at least as large as the cost to punishers. The effect is strongest when the loss to defectors is double the cost to punishers. If the punishment is too costly for punishers relative to the loss inflicted, the mechanism fails to promote cooperation. The network structure also matters: small-world and scale-free networks allow cooperation to emerge more easily with lower punishment costs. The main outcome is the sum of contributions, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase group contributions in PGG-like environments, especially when the punishment is effective (defector loss >= punisher cost) and when the network structure facilitates information flow. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or total group payoff, its findings should be used as indirect support for the prediction task. The results suggest that, for a given control efficiency, enabling punishment with sufficient effectiveness (high punishment impact per cost) is likely to increase group contributions, and thus may increase efficiency, especially in networked settings. The effect is sensitive to the relative cost and impact of punishment.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Fu, M. J. (2020). A punishment mechanism in the spatial public goods game with continuous strategies. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game with continuous strategies, punishing the minority of lowest contributors (i.e., when the number of punished individuals is less than half the group) increases the average contribution level, while punishing the majority decreases it. Increasing the punishment fine increases cooperation when the punished are a minority, but decreases it when the punished are a majority. The main reported outcome is the average contribution (cooperation level), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment design (number punished, fine size, cost structure) affects average contribution in a spatial continuous PGG. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that punishment can backfire if the cost to punishers is too high relative to the fine, and that targeting a minority of low contributors is more effective for increasing cooperation. These insights may inform the qualitative direction of efficiency effects in similar designs, but quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Benjamin, C., & Sarkar, S. (2019). Triggers for cooperative behavior in the thermodynamic limit: A case study in Public goods game. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the thermodynamic limit of the two-player PGG, the prevalence of cooperation (as measured by game magnetization) is strongly affected by the reward for providing, the cost of the public good, and the presence and magnitude of punishment for defection. Specifically, increasing punishment shifts the critical point for cooperation to lower reward values, making cooperation more prevalent. Increasing cost shifts the critical point to higher reward values, making defection more prevalent. The model predicts phase transitions in the dominant strategy as these parameters vary. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that punishment increases the fraction of cooperators, which would generally be associated with higher efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in the thermodynamic limit of a two-player PGG, the presence and magnitude of punishment increases the prevalence of cooperation (i.e., more players choose to provide), while higher cost suppresses cooperation. Although the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the increase in cooperation due to punishment suggests that, in standard PGGs, enabling punishment would increase efficiency, especially when the cost of the public good is not too high. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with two players, continuous contribution choices, and no communication, and may be less directly transferable to finite or empirical settings. The results are useful for predicting the qualitative effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, on efficiency, but do not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Akiyama, E., & Toriumi, F. (2021). Cooperation in spatial public good games depends on the locality effects of game, adaptation, and punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that localizing either the game interaction or the punishment scope in a spatial PGG model leads to robust cooperation, while globalizing both undermines cooperation. The adaptation (learning) process also interacts with these effects: global adaptation can help spread cooperation if the regime is already cooperative, but can also help defectors invade if cooperation is fragile. The model uses expected payoffs to update strategies, but does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are most directly about cooperation rates and the stability of cooperative regimes under different locality conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that local punishment and local game interactions are important for sustaining cooperation in spatial PGGs, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are useful for understanding which game design features (local vs global interaction, punishment scope) are likely to support high cooperation, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but the lack of explicit payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the efficiency task.

- source: Nakamura, G. M., Contesini, G. S., & Martinez, A. S. (2019). Cooperation risk and Nash equilibrium: Quantitative description for realistic players. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment into public goods games reduces the risk associated with cooperation, which can promote cooperative behavior among players. However, punishment also reduces the absolute payoffs for all players, and its effect on cooperation depends on the cost-to-benefit ratio and the punishment parameter. There are critical thresholds (phase transitions) where small changes in parameters can shift the system from a cooperative to a non-cooperative regime. The main reported outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment can alter cooperation rates in public goods games, especially through the lens of cooperation risk and phase transitions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff, which are the primary outcomes for the downstream prediction task. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation rates under certain parameter regimes, but may also reduce payoffs and, in some cases, accelerate defection. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic insight rather than quantitative payoff outcomes.

- source: Allgaier, K., Scigala, K. A., Trautwein, U., Hilbig, B. E., & Zettler, I. (2020). Honesty-humility and dictator and ultimatum game-giving in children. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game with punishment (both pool and peer), time-dependent effects such as oscillations in the enhancement factor (r) and delays in punishment delivery reduce the prevalence of cooperation and increase defection. Peer punishment is more effective than pool punishment at promoting cooperation, but both are less effective when r oscillates with high amplitude or when punishment is delayed. The main outcomes are the frequencies of cooperators, defectors, and punishers, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that instability in game parameters and delays in sanctioning undermine the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It confirms that punishment can promote cooperation in public goods games, especially when punishment is immediate and the enhancement factor is stable. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that in environments with time-varying productivity or delayed punishment, the positive effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, likely on efficiency) is weakened. For prediction, this paper supports the idea that punishment is less effective in unstable or delayed settings, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Han, D., Yan, S. T., & Li, D. D. (2019). The evolutionary public goods game model with punishment mechanism in an activity-driven network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that increasing the maximal fine of punishment has a greater impact on defectors than on cooperators, but as the fine increases, the final density of cooperators decreases. The maximal cost of punishment mainly affects the prevalence of punishing strategies, with higher costs reducing the number of punishing cooperators. The initial density of cooperators has a counterintuitive effect: a higher initial density leads to a lower final density of cooperators, and vice versa. The results are reported in terms of the densities of strategy types, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies under different parameter settings. While the model structure and parameterization (e.g., punishment cost, fine, network dynamics) are informative for understanding mechanisms, the lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes means this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. It may be useful for understanding how punishment parameters affect cooperation rates, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., Wu, Y. E., Yan, M., & Xie, Y. Y. (2018). Tolerance-based punishment and cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that tolerance-based punishment, where cooperators only punish defectors after a threshold of tolerated defections, can significantly promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. The mechanism reduces the frequency of punishment and allows for higher levels of cooperation under adverse conditions. The effects of punishment fine (\alpha) and tolerance threshold (M) are systematically explored, showing that higher fines and lower tolerance thresholds promote cooperation. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment mechanisms with a tolerance threshold can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games. However, because the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, under similar spatial and parameter conditions, enabling punishment (especially with moderate tolerance) is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if the cost of punishment is not too high. However, the paper does not quantify efficiency or net group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible from this study alone.

- source: Liu, J. Z., Meng, H. R., Wang, W., Li, T., & Yu, Y. (2018). Synergy punishment promotes cooperation in spatial public good game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment into a spatial public goods game increases the fraction of cooperators, and that further reducing the cost of punishment through a synergistic effect (i.e., when multiple punishers share the cost) leads to even higher cooperation rates. The effect of synergy is most pronounced when the punishment fine is low; at high fines, the additional benefit of synergy is minimal. The main outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The paper qualitatively links higher cooperation to higher group payoffs, but does not report efficiency or payoff data directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both punishment and reduced punishment cost (via synergy) increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting how design dimensions like punishment cost and punishment effectiveness affect cooperation rates, and by extension may suggest (but not demonstrate) positive effects on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect support for the idea that lower punishment cost and higher punishment effectiveness (via synergy) should increase efficiency, but this is inferred from cooperation rates, not measured payoffs.

- source: Zhu, P. C., Guo, H., Zhang, H. L., Han, Y., Wang, Z., & Chu, C. (2020). The role of punishment in the spatial public goods game. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment leads to higher levels of cooperation than pool punishment in spatial public goods games. When the threshold for switching to pool punishment is high (i.e., more peer punishment), cooperation is more prevalent. The coexistence of both punishment types is explored, and the transition between regimes is mapped. The main findings are based on the frequencies of cooperators, defectors, and punishers, not on efficiency or group payoff. The ratio of fine to cost is higher for peer punishment, which is suggested as a reason for its greater effectiveness in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that peer punishment is more effective than pool punishment in increasing the prevalence of cooperation in spatial PGGs. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model and parameterization are clearly described, so the paper can inform mechanistic expectations about how punishment type and its implementation affect cooperation, but not directly about efficiency or welfare outcomes.

- source: Moreno, S. F. G. Y., & Gutierrez-Garcia, J. O. (2018). Collective action in organizational structures. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of hierarchical organizations and the design of punishment mechanisms significantly affect collective action, as measured by aggregate contribution levels. Peer-based (collegial) punishment mechanisms lead to higher contributions than supervisor-based punishment. Organizations with more hierarchical levels achieve higher contributions when supervisor-based punishment is used. Smaller organizational units (lower span of control) also foster higher contributions, especially when only the most egoistic agent is punished. The presence of any punishment mechanism increases contributions compared to no punishment. However, all results are reported in terms of contribution rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms (peer vs. supervisor), the number of hierarchical levels, and the span of control can substantially affect contribution rates in repeated public goods games. However, because the outcomes are reported as contribution levels rather than efficiency or group payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency is limited. The findings suggest that, in similar agent-based or organizational settings, enabling peer punishment and structuring organizations into smaller units with more levels may increase contributions, which could correlate with higher efficiency if punishment costs are not excessive. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the net efficiency impact, especially considering the costs of punishment.

- source: Adami, C., & Hintze, A. (2018). Thermodynamics of evolutionary games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a one-dimensional PGG with three players per group, cooperation only emerges when the synergy parameter r exceeds the group size (r > 3). Introducing punishment (with a fine for defectors) shifts the critical point, allowing cooperation to emerge at lower r. The effect of punishment depends on the fine imposed on defectors, not on the cost to punishers. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results are derived analytically and confirmed by agent-based simulations, but neither approach reports group efficiency or welfare directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment increases the fraction of cooperators in a PGG-like environment and lowers the threshold for cooperation. However, it does not report or analyze group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The findings are useful for predicting when cooperation will emerge as a function of game parameters (e.g., punishment fine, group size, synergy), but do not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that, under the studied conditions, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may be associated with higher efficiency in standard PGGs, but this link is not explicitly demonstrated or quantified in the paper.

- source: Battu, B. (2021). Evolution of altruistic punishments among heterogeneous conditional cooperators. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with a population of heterogeneous conditional cooperators, the evolution of altruistic punishment (where more willing cooperators are also more willing to punish) can stabilize high levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends on the inflicted cost of punishment and the frequency with which punishment opportunities arise. When punishment is frequent and the inflicted cost is high, the population evolves toward more cooperative and punitive types, and donation rates are high. When punishment is infrequent or the inflicted cost is low, cooperation collapses. The model also shows that it is not necessary to punish all free riders equally; targeting persistent (selfish) free riders is sufficient. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher donation rates are interpreted as indicative of higher group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in repeated public goods games with conditional cooperators can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is sufficiently costly to the target and opportunities to punish are frequent. However, because the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its quantitative value for predicting efficiency is limited. The main actionable insight is that punishment is most effective when it is frequent and sufficiently severe, and that selective (generous) punishment targeting persistent free riders is sufficient to stabilize cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with high inflicted cost and frequent opportunities) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, but the magnitude cannot be directly inferred from this paper.

  findings: The study shows that egoistic punishment can evolve and promote high levels of cooperation in both peer and pool punishment modes, outperforming altruistic punishment in a wider range of parameter settings (especially at higher punishment costs and lower fines). The mechanism is robust to different strategy updating rules and population structures. However, the paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of cooperation and the survival of different strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of the punishment mechanism (egoistic vs. altruistic) and the parameterization of punishment cost and fine can substantially affect cooperation rates in public goods games. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that egoistic punishment mechanisms may be more effective at sustaining cooperation under a broader set of conditions, which could indirectly imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly measured or reported.

- source: Quan, J., Tang, C. X., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). Reputation evaluation with tolerance and reputation-dependent imitation on cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  findings: The study finds that, in a spatial public goods game with both punishment and exclusion available, making exclusion the primary sanctioning mechanism (with punishers acting as pure cooperators when excluders are present) leads to a larger parameter region where cooperation dominates, compared to the reverse. The average frequency of cooperation is higher and the threshold for the emergence of cooperation is lower when exclusion leads. The results are robust across variations in the punishment fine, exclusion cost, and synergy factor. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are based on behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a spatial PGG with both punishment and exclusion, exclusion as the leading sanctioning mechanism is more effective at promoting cooperation than punishment as the leading mechanism, across a wide range of parameter values. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that game designs enabling exclusion (rather than punishment) as the primary sanction may yield higher cooperation rates, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The findings are most relevant for settings with spatial structure, multiple sanctioning options, and evolutionary updating.

- source: Niu, H., Chen, Y. Y., Ye, H., Zhang, H., Li, Y., & Chen, S. (2020). Distinguishing punishing costly signals from nonpunishing costly signals can facilitate the emergence of altruistic punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game, if costly signaling is effective and/or if altruistic punishment is treated as a distinctive and more valuable signal than nonpunishing cooperation, then altruistic punishment can emerge and support cooperation. When punishment is not distinguished as a special costly signal, or when the efficacy of costly signaling is low, cooperation and punishment do not emerge. The results are robust across a range of parameters, including different initial population compositions and values of the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). However, the study does not report direct effects on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary stability and frequency of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by clarifying that the emergence and stability of punishment (and thus, by implication, higher efficiency) in public goods games depends on whether punishment is socially recognized as a distinctive and valuable costly signal. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is not socially salient or is not treated as a special signal, enabling punishment may not improve cooperation or efficiency. However, if punishment is socially valued, it can support cooperation even when other mechanisms are weak. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency, so its value for direct efficiency prediction is limited, but it is informative about the conditions under which punishment is likely to be effective.

- source: van Dijk, E., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Experimental Games and Social Decision Making. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 72*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The review highlights that the inclusion of punishment and reward mechanisms in repeated public goods games and other social dilemmas enables partner control, which can promote cooperation and the development of group norms. It notes that punishment is typically costly to the punisher but more costly to the target, and that such mechanisms can shape future cooperation through reputation and indirect reciprocity. The review also discusses moderators such as group heterogeneity, intergroup competition, and cross-cultural variation in norm enforcement. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative synthesis on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in public goods games and related environments, especially in repeated interactions. It identifies key design features (e.g., repeated play, sanctioning options, partner selection) that are relevant to predicting the impact of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to calibrate the magnitude of punishment's effect on efficiency. Its value for prediction is mainly in identifying which game dimensions are theoretically important moderators.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social norms, particularly conditional cooperation norms, are central to explaining cooperation in public goods games. Punishment opportunities increase cooperation rates, but the welfare (efficiency) effects are often limited or negative due to the costs of punishment and the occurrence of antisocial punishment. Normative constraints on punishment—such as those enabled by institutional or group-level norm formation—can reduce antisocial punishment and make punishment more effective, leading to higher cooperation with less collateral cost. The review emphasizes that the presence of punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the structure and normative context of punishment matter greatly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and integrative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff unless punishment is normatively constrained to reduce antisocial punishment and collateral costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of normative or institutional constraints, the prevalence of antisocial punishment, and the cultural context. Game design dimensions that affect the cost, targeting, and normative context of punishment are likely to be critical moderators of efficiency outcomes. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes, but it cautions against assuming that increased cooperation from punishment always translates into higher efficiency.

- source: Akdeniz, A., & van Veelen, M. (2021). The evolution of morality and the role of commitment. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that commitment is a central mechanism for the evolution of cooperation, punishment, and other moral behaviors in humans. It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that commitment to punishment can sustain cooperation in public goods games and prisoners' dilemmas, even when punishment is costly. The authors contend that standard models based on population structure or repetition do not fully explain observed human behavior, and that commitment (and the ability to recognize commitment in others) is necessary for the evolution of costly punishment and conditional cooperation. The paper discusses the implications for efficiency and group welfare in a theoretical sense but does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical results on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment can sustain cooperation in public goods games and similar environments, emphasizing the role of commitment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that the presence of punishment (and the ability to commit to punishment) can change the strategic environment and potentially increase cooperation, but it does not allow for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of commitment and recognizability of commitment for punishment to be effective.

- source: Wang, S. X., Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Incentive strategies for the evolution of cooperation: Analysis and optimization. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment, reward, exclusion, or adaptive combinations thereof can stabilize or increase cooperation in public goods games, but the implementation of these strategies is costly and can create second-order dilemmas. Theoretical models show that optimal (time-varying) incentive protocols can minimize the cumulative cost required to reach a desired cooperation level. Punishment is generally more cost-effective than reward for achieving high cooperation. Adaptive hybrid strategies (first carrot, then stick) can outperform pure punishment or reward. The paper also highlights that the structure of the population and the design of the incentive protocol (e.g., thresholds for switching between punishment and exclusion) are important moderators. However, the main outcomes are cooperation rates and incentive costs, not group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how different incentive strategies (punishment, reward, exclusion, and hybrids) affect cooperation rates and the cost of achieving cooperation in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment can promote cooperation, and for predicting how design features (such as the presence of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the use of hybrid strategies) might influence cooperation rates. However, because the primary outcomes are cooperation rates and incentive costs, not group efficiency or total payoff, the paper is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment can increase cooperation and that cost-effective protocols exist, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency changes.

- source: Barrett, J. (2020). Punishment and disagreement in the state of nature. *Economics and Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that experimental public goods games with punishment show that punishment can stabilize cooperation when individuals agree on norms and punishment costs are low. However, when there is moral disagreement or opportunities for counter-punishment, cooperation breaks down and conflict can escalate. The analysis is theoretical and interpretive, using published experimental results as illustrative evidence. The paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public goods games, especially when punishment is low-cost and there is agreement on norms. However, it also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment is undermined by moral disagreement and counter-punishment, which can lead to conflict and breakdown of cooperation. For the prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment is likely to increase cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) in standard public goods games under conditions of agreement and low punishment cost, but that this effect is fragile to disagreement and retaliation dynamics. The paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value is mainly in highlighting moderators and mechanisms.

- source: Schunk, D., & Wagner, V. (2021). What determines the willingness to sanction violations of newly introduced social norms: Personality traits or economic preferences? evidence from the COVID-19 crisis. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews the literature on punishment in public goods games, emphasizing that while punishment can sustain cooperation, the presence of antisocial punishment (punishment of cooperators) can undermine this effect. It highlights the importance of reputation, group size, and social context in moderating the effectiveness of punishment. The paper does not present new data or direct analyses of efficiency or group payoff, but provides a conceptual overview of when and why punishment may or may not improve cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual background on the mechanisms by which punishment can affect cooperation in public goods games, including the role of antisocial punishment and reputation. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions affect the efficiency impact of punishment. Its value for prediction is mainly in highlighting potential moderators (e.g., group size, reputation systems, presence of antisocial punishment), but it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes from design parameters.

- source: Henrich, J., & Muthukrishna, M. (2021). The Origins and Psychology of Human Cooperation. *Annual Review of Psychology, Vol 72*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The review argues that mechanisms such as punishment, reputation, and signaling are crucial for sustaining cooperation in large-scale human societies, especially where kinship and direct reciprocity are insufficient. It highlights that the willingness to punish norm violations (including in public goods and related games) varies dramatically across societies and is associated with community size and institutional context. The review synthesizes evidence that punishment can sustain cooperation, but also that the form and prevalence of punishment are culturally variable. It also notes that mechanisms supporting cooperation can stabilize both prosocial and maladaptive costly norms. However, the review does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can sustain cooperation in public goods game-like environments, especially in large, anonymous societies. It also highlights that the effectiveness and prevalence of punishment depend on cultural, institutional, and demographic factors such as community size. However, because the review does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in identifying which dimensions (e.g., community size, presence of punishment, cultural context) are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, and in providing context for interpreting empirical findings from other studies.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2021). Friendly-rivalry solution to the iterated n-person public-goods game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the CAPRI-n strategy achieves full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the iterated n-person public-goods game, even with implementation errors. When all players use CAPRI-n, the group achieves the cooperative payoff in the limit of small error probability. The strategy is defensible (cannot be exploited), efficient (achieves full cooperation), and distinguishable (can exploit unconditional cooperators). Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI-n dominates the population across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios and population sizes, outperforming other memory-one strategies. The results are robust for n=2 and 3, and supported by Monte Carlo simulation for n=4.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated public-goods games with error, a well-designed punishment/reciprocity strategy (CAPRI-n) can achieve near-maximal efficiency, even as group size increases. The findings suggest that, when the game allows for memory-based contingent punishment and recovery, enabling such mechanisms can dramatically increase efficiency relative to baseline. The results are most directly applicable to settings with continuous contributions, no explicit punishment cost, and no external punishment/reward mechanism, but where strategies can implement contingent retaliation. The paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated PGGs with endogenous, strategy-based punishment/reciprocity, especially as group size increases.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2020). Evolutionary game dynamics in multiagent systems with prosocial and antisocial exclusion strategies. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in finite well-mixed populations playing a public goods game, prosocial pool exclusion (where cooperators can pay to exclude defectors from benefits) is more effective at promoting cooperation and group efficiency than costly punishment. Even when antisocial exclusion (where defectors exclude cooperators) is introduced, prosocial pool excluders maintain their evolutionary advantage and can dominate the population, especially when second-order sanctioning is allowed. When both exclusion and punishment (prosocial and antisocial) are available, prosocial pool excluders prevail and maximize group payoff/efficiency. Antisocial exclusion alone leads to defection and low efficiency, but does not undermine the effectiveness of prosocial exclusion when both are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with the option for prosocial exclusion, enabling such exclusion mechanisms leads to higher group efficiency and welfare than enabling costly punishment, even in the presence of antisocial sanctioning. The results are robust to the inclusion of second-order sanctioning and to moderate changes in the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). For prediction tasks, the presence of prosocial exclusion as a punishment-like mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, and to outperform standard costly punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to games with continuous contributions, no communication, and fixed group size and rounds, as modeled here.

- source: Quan, J., Qin, Y., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). How to evaluate one's behavior toward 'bad' individuals? Exploring good social norms in promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games with reputation-based social norms, rewarding cooperation and punishing defection toward 'bad' individuals (low-reputation) can promote cooperation, especially when the reputation threshold for being considered 'good' is high. However, increasing the reward intensity for cooperating with 'bad' individuals does not further improve cooperation, while increasing punishment intensity for defecting against 'bad' individuals only helps in high-threshold environments. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperators; average payoff per strategy is reported but not the main focus. The model is robust to both lattice and well-mixed population structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment and reward mechanisms on cooperation in PGG-like environments, especially under varying reputation thresholds. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The reported average payoffs per strategy suggest that cooperators tend to have higher payoffs, but group-level efficiency (relative to full cooperation) is not analyzed. The model's manipulation of punishment and reward intensity, and the reporting of average payoffs, may inform qualitative expectations about the direction of efficiency effects, but quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Quan, J., Li, X., & Wang, X. J. (2019). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game with conditional peer exclusion. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of conditional peer exclusion in a spatial public goods game model can greatly promote the emergence and stability of cooperation, especially when the tolerance for defectors is low (i.e., excluders act against defectors even when there are few of them), the cost of exclusion is low, and the probability of exclusion success is high. Even with moderate or low exclusion success probability, conditional exclusion can still promote cooperation if the synergy factor (equivalent to MPCR) is not too low. The main reported outcome is the cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include a direct control (no-exclusion) efficiency comparison, but does reference the threshold for cooperation in the standard SPGG. The findings suggest that exclusion-based punishment can be a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional exclusion (a form of punishment) can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially under low tolerance, low cost, and high effectiveness. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting the effect of punishment on cooperation rates, and only indirectly suggest that efficiency may increase when cooperation is high. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency from design dimensions plus control efficiency), this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic support, not quantitative or direct evidence.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). The effect of increasing returns to scale in public goods investment on threshold values of cooperation under social exclusion mechanism. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of an exclusion-type punishment mechanism (social exclusion) in a PGG with increasing returns to scale can promote the emergence of cooperation, depending on the cost and effectiveness of exclusion, the returns to scale, and the public goods multiplier. Increasing the effectiveness of exclusion, lowering its cost, or increasing the public goods multiplier can shift the system from all-defection to coexistence of cooperation and exclusion. However, simply increasing returns to scale cannot eliminate defection entirely in the presence of exclusion; it only expands the parameter region where cooperation and exclusion coexist. In structured populations, increasing returns to scale can more directly promote cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about the prevalence of cooperative strategies rather than direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that the presence and effectiveness of an exclusion-based punishment mechanism can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in PGG-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and its effectiveness is high. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, under certain parameter regimes, enabling punishment (via exclusion) is likely to increase efficiency, but this is inferred from the prevalence of cooperation rather than direct payoff data. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as social exclusion rather than direct fines.

  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in social dilemmas, as studied in public goods games and related models, is just one aspect of a broader class of moral behaviors. It reviews how statistical physics and network science have been used to study cooperation, including mechanisms like punishment and reward, and suggests that these methods can be extended to study other forms of moral behavior. The paper references prior work showing that mechanisms such as punishment, reward, and network structure can affect the evolution of cooperation, but does not provide new empirical or simulation results. Efficiency is mentioned as a moral preference, but not as a measured outcome.

It is useful for contextualizing which mechanisms and dimensions (e.g., punishment, reward, network structure) are considered important in the literature, and for mapping which game features have been studied in prior simulation and empirical work. However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative effects of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it provide parameter-level evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Chen, Q., & Chen, T. (2020). Group rewarding can promote cooperation and save costs in public goods games. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that group rewarding, when combined with a blame/shame feedback mechanism, can promote high levels of cooperation and total contributions in PGGs, often at a much lower cost than traditional individual rewards. The effectiveness and cost-efficiency of the mechanism depend on the synergy factor (mpcr equivalent), the cost of being an initiator, and the reward adjustment coefficient. There is an optimal region for the reward parameter where contributions are maximized and costs minimized. The model demonstrates that, in large populations, group rewarding can be a scalable and cost-effective way to promote cooperation. The presence of the blame/shame mechanism is critical for maximizing the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that group-based reward mechanisms, especially when combined with non-monetary social feedback (blame/shame), can increase efficiency in PGG-like environments. While it does not directly study punishment, the findings suggest that soft social sanctions can substitute for costly punishment in promoting cooperation. For prediction tasks, the results indicate that enabling group reward (with social feedback) in a PGG with known control efficiency will likely increase efficiency, especially if the reward parameters are tuned to an optimal intermediate value. The model's focus on cost-effectiveness is particularly relevant for predicting the net efficiency impact of interventions. However, direct predictions about monetary punishment are not possible from this paper.

- source: Zhang, X. Y., Chen, T., Chen, Q., & Li, X. Y. (2020). Will you cooperate in case the payoff can be guaranteed?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing an insurance-like incentive mechanism in a PGG leads to higher cooperation rates and increased group efficiency (net profit, NP), provided that the extra-charge (insurance premium) and incentive rate (compensation multiplier) are set at moderate levels. Too high an extra-charge or too low an incentive rate reduces the effectiveness of the mechanism. The model demonstrates that the mechanism can eliminate second-order free riders and allows organizers to save on incentive costs compared to standard reward mechanisms. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that insurance-like incentive mechanisms can increase efficiency in PGGs, especially when the parameters are set appropriately. While it does not study punishment directly, it offers insight into how alternative incentive mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing a well-calibrated incentive mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) can increase group efficiency relative to a control without such mechanisms. However, the findings are based on simulation, not empirical data, and may not transfer directly to real-world or experimental PGGs with punishment.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Benefits of intervention in spatial public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional intervention, modeled as a central authority forcing some defectors to contribute, can increase the efficiency (average total payoff) of a spatial PGG, especially when the synergy factor is high and the intervention cost is controlled. Weak intervention can paradoxically reduce cooperation and efficiency, but moderate intervention can lead to higher group incomes than the standard PGG. Excessive intervention may reduce cooperation. The effect of intervention is distinct from peer punishment, as the cost is shared by all contributors and there is no direct punisher. The findings are based on simulation results and analysis of average payoffs and cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in spatial PGGs, the introduction of an institutional intervention mechanism (analogous to centralized punishment) can increase group efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate intervention degree/intensity and high synergy factor (mpcr). The effect is non-monotonic: weak or excessive intervention can reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency, one should consider the strength and cost of the intervention and the underlying mpcr. The results are most applicable to settings with spatial structure, no peer punishment, and institutional (not individual) enforcement.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2018). Seven rules to avoid the tragedy of the commons. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games with three players and implementation error, it is possible to construct deterministic memory-3 strategies that achieve mutual cooperation (efficiency) and defensibility (no player can be exploited), as well as distinguishability (outperforming unconditional cooperators). For n-person games, memory length at least n is required for such strategies. The strategies do not involve explicit punishment actions or costs, but use conditional cooperation and defection rules that can serve similar functions. The findings are theoretical and do not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment mechanisms on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated public goods games, it is possible to achieve high efficiency through carefully designed deterministic strategies with sufficient memory, even in the presence of errors. However, it does not study the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (such as costly punishment or sanctions) on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare games with and without punishment. Therefore, while the results are highly relevant for understanding the potential for cooperation and efficiency in repeated PGGs, they do not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in experimental or empirical settings. The paper is most useful for understanding the theoretical limits of strategy design rather than the causal effect of punishment interventions.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2021). Coevolution of actions, personal norms and beliefs about others in social dilemmas. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, in repeated public goods and related games, the long-term average group action and payoff converge to the Nash equilibrium based on material payoffs when there is no external authority. Social and cognitive factors (such as conformity, cognitive dissonance, and beliefs about others) affect the distribution and variance of actions and beliefs, but do not shift the group average away from the material-payoff optimum in the absence of authority. When an external authority promotes a particular action, group behavior can shift toward the promoted standard, sometimes increasing or decreasing efficiency depending on the alignment of the promoted action with the material optimum. The model does not include explicit peer punishment or sanctioning as a treatment variable, so it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical baseline for predicting efficiency in public goods and related games without explicit punishment mechanisms. It shows that, absent external authority or explicit sanctions, efficiency is determined by material payoffs, and social/cognitive factors mainly affect variance and convergence speed. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, unless punishment is modeled as a material cost for non-conformity (as in the 'disapproval by peers' term), enabling punishment would require additional modeling or empirical evidence. The model is most useful for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency and for understanding the role of soft normative pressures, but does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling explicit punishment on efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2019). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in the public goods game with pool exclusion strategies. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper shows that introducing prosocial pool exclusion into a PGG leads to higher average payoffs and can stabilize cooperation through periodic oscillations or fixation to full cooperation, depending on whether second-order exclusion is present. Antisocial exclusion can destabilize cooperation but does not fully prevent the effectiveness of prosocial exclusion. Compared to punishment, prosocial pool exclusion is a more robust and effective mechanism for sustaining high efficiency in the population, even in the presence of antisocial strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in infinite well-mixed populations, enabling a prosocial pool exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a fixed cost to exclude defectors from the public good) increases group efficiency relative to the standard PGG without exclusion. The effect is robust to the presence of antisocial exclusion, and even more pronounced when second-order exclusion is allowed. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a pool exclusion mechanism should be expected to yield higher efficiency than punishment, especially when exclusion is institutionalized and second-order exclusion is possible. However, the results are derived from deterministic replicator dynamics and may not directly translate to finite or experimental settings.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation in costly institutions exhibits Red Queen and Black Queen dynamics in heterogeneous public goods. *Communications Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that introducing a costly institution (with a participation fee) alongside a free institution in a public goods game can increase cooperation and group efficiency. For low enhancement factors (low profitability), only the costly institution supports cooperation, and average payoffs remain close to the entrance cost (i.e., not much higher than the baseline). For higher enhancement factors, cooperation emerges in both institutions, and group payoffs increase above zero. The costly institution is more likely to be populated by cooperators, as defectors avoid the entrance cost unless there are enough cooperators to exploit. The model also shows that the effect of the entrance cost is context-dependent: it is beneficial for cooperation and efficiency at low profitability, but can be detrimental at high profitability if the cost is too high, as it reduces participation. The results hold in both well-mixed and structured populations, though spatial structure removes some bistability and further supports cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that introducing a costly institution (entrance fee) in a public goods game can increase efficiency and group payoff, especially when the baseline profitability (mpcr/enhancement factor) is low. The entrance cost acts as a filter, keeping defectors out and allowing cooperators to achieve higher payoffs. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly institution (or, by analogy, a punishment mechanism with a cost to participate) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such cost, particularly when the marginal per-capita return is low. However, if the cost is too high and the game is already highly profitable, the effect may reverse. The model does not include explicit punishment, but the costly institution's effect is adjacent to punishment in that it deters free-riding through a cost barrier. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where institutional participation costs or costly precommitment mechanisms are present, rather than direct peer punishment.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in two-round public goods games, both reward-based and assortative mechanisms can promote the evolution of cooperation and the emergence of consistent cooperative or defective personalities. In the 'reward dilemma' scenario, only first-round cooperators are allowed to play a second PGG, which can incentivize cooperation if the second game's enhancement factor is high enough. In the 'assortative' scenario, both cooperators and defectors play a second PGG in separate groups, and competition between these groups can further promote cooperation and personality consistency. The model shows that cooperation is maximized at intermediate values of the second-round enhancement factor, and that consistent personalities (cooperating or defecting in both rounds) naturally evolve. However, the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how reward and assortment mechanisms (and the possibility of repeated or conditional interaction) can promote cooperation and personality consistency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or their efficiency effects. The findings may be indirectly informative for prediction tasks involving reward or assortment, but not for punishment. The model's outcomes are primarily behavioral (cooperation rates, personality consistency), not efficiency or group payoff, so it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2019). Cleverly handling the donation information can promote cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list partially (i.e., only for those who contribute above a threshold) increases total contributions to the public good compared to publishing all or none of the list. The optimal threshold for publication can be set dynamically as the mean of recent contributions, which is both effective and convenient. The model shows that this approach increases the sum of contributions and reduces the variance in contributions, indicating more uniform and higher cooperation. The effect is robust to changes in network connectivity and the window of historical data used. Gossip and self-assessment of face act as indirect social sanctions, motivating higher contributions. There is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism with costs and impact parameters as in standard PGG punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that indirect social sanctions (via partial publication of contribution information and gossip) can increase total group contributions in a PGG-like environment. While it does not study explicit costly punishment, it suggests that reputational mechanisms can substitute for or complement formal punishment in promoting cooperation and group welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that introducing a reputational/gossip-based sanction (by publicizing contributions above a threshold) can increase group payoff, especially if the threshold is set adaptively. However, the absence of explicit punishment cost/impact parameters means the results are not directly transferable to standard punishment treatments. The sum of contributions is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

- source: Yang, R., Chen, T., & Chen, Q. (2020). Promoting cooperation by reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism in public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism, which redistributes payoffs from low- to high-reputation players, can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. The effect depends on the parameters controlling the proportion of players who lose or gain payoffs (transfer ratios) and the strength of the transfer. When both the proportion of punished (transferred) and rewarded (accepted) players are moderate and balanced, cooperation can quickly dominate the population. The mechanism is most effective when all players are either punished or rewarded (transfer ratio = 1). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms combining endogenous punishment and reward based on reputation can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when the redistribution is balanced and covers the whole population. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings may be useful for predicting cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2020). A belief in rewards accelerates cooperation on consumer-generated media. *Journal of Computational Social Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a public goods game with only rewards and meta-rewards (no punishment), cooperation can be sustained if players can observe and respond to the individual meta-rewarding rates of others. When players base their reward-giving on the observed likelihood of receiving a meta-reward from a specific cooperator, stable cooperation emerges. If players cannot condition on this information, cooperation collapses. The benefit-to-cost ratio of rewards and meta-rewards must be sufficiently high for cooperation to be maintained. The model does not include punishment, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the role of reward and meta-reward mechanisms, and highlights the importance of observability and benefit-to-cost ratios for sustaining cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, and efficiency or group payoff is not a reported outcome, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding adjacent mechanisms (reward-based), but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Du, C. P., Jia, D. Y., Jin, L. B., & Shi, L. (2018). The impact of neutral reward on cooperation in public good game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
