  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a neutral reward mechanism, where the majority strategy in a group is rewarded by the minority, promotes the evolution of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. As the reward factor increases, cooperation becomes more prevalent, and for sufficiently high reward, the system can reach full cooperation. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperators and the spatial distribution of strategies. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reward mechanisms (even when not perfectly targeted) can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the outcomes are limited to cooperation rates and do not include efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The game design dimensions are described in the model, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment or reward. The results may suggest that reward mechanisms can increase efficiency if cooperation translates to higher group payoff, but this is not demonstrated in the paper.

- source: Sutter, M., & Untertrifaller, A. (2020). Children's heterogeneity in cooperation and parental background: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that allocating taxes and rewards based on memory reputation generally increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games, especially in environments with high taxes or high synergy factors. In low-tax, low-synergy environments (favorable to defectors), equal allocation of rewards and taxes is more effective at sustaining cooperation. The model shows that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms depends on the balance between tax and reward costs, memory length, and the synergy factor. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the structure of incentive mechanisms (tax-funded rewards, reputation-based allocation) can affect cooperation rates, and that the effectiveness of such mechanisms depends on environmental parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive incentive mechanisms interact with game design dimensions.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2020). Public Goods Games on Coevolving Social Network Models. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to rewire their social connections when unsatisfied (i.e., when their payoff is less than their contribution) leads to higher average contributions in public goods games on networks, compared to static networks. The transition from low to high contribution occurs at lower enhancement factors when rewiring is allowed, and higher rewiring probabilities further increase average contributions. These effects are robust across both synthetic and real social network structures. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward options, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling a form of costless partner switching (exit) can increase average contributions in networked public goods games, which may be relevant for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms that allow players to avoid or sanction defectors. However, because the model does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for understanding the impact of dynamic network structure (partner choice) rather than punishment per se.

- source: Chen, T., Wu, Z. H., & Wang, L. (2018). Disseminators or silencers: The effect of information diffusion intensity on cooperation in public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and type of information dissemination strongly affect the speed at which full cooperation is achieved in a spatial public goods game with reputation and conformity. Negative disseminators (who spread information about defectors) most often lead to the fastest convergence to full cooperation, followed by neutral and positive disseminators. Silencers (no dissemination) are least effective. The effect of dissemination type is most pronounced when conformity synergy, link-neighbor degree, population size, or environmental noise are high, or when the synergy factor is low. However, all models eventually reach full cooperation, so there is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare differences between models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of information diffusion and reputation in accelerating the achievement of cooperation. The findings suggest that mechanisms which amplify reputational consequences for defectors (akin to negative gossip or public shaming) can speed up the transition to full cooperation, especially in certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address whether the final payoffs or efficiency differ between conditions.

- source: Zheng, J. J., Ren, T. Y., Ma, G., & Dong, J. H. (2021). The emergence and implementation of pool exclusion in spatial public goods game with heterogeneous ability-to-pay. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing pool exclusion, where excluders pay a cost to exclude defectors from the public good, can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of exclusion depends on parameters such as the enhancement factor (r), the probability and effectiveness of exclusion, and the contribution rate to the exclusion pool. Cooperation is more likely to prevail when exclusion is effective and not overinvested. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators and excluders, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusionary sanctions (pool exclusion) can increase cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because the main outcome is cooperation level (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the sanction is exclusion rather than classic costly punishment, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that effective, not overly costly exclusion can help sustain cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, W. M., & Zhao, J. H. (2020). The evolution of cooperation within the multigame environment based on the Particle Swarm Optimization algorithm. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that increasing the threshold required to initiate a public goods game (i.e., requiring more cooperators for the game to proceed) leads to higher stable levels of cooperation in a spatially structured population. Introducing a reputation adjustment factor, where defectors lose reputation and cooperators gain reputation, further increases cooperation rates. The model shows that even small reputation effects can shift the population toward cooperation. The effect of noise in strategy updating is complex: higher noise can promote cooperation under high thresholds. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; all main results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which increase the cost of defection (here, via reputation loss) or require a threshold of cooperation to initiate public goods production can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include explicit punishment (costly sanctions) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding how threshold and reputation mechanisms affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Wang, M. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2020). The interplay of behaviors and attitudes in public goods game considering environmental investment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of an alliance strategy, where players pay an aid fee to help exploited allies, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperative behavior (C + A) in spatial PGGs. Alliance clusters are robust to defector invasion and can expand to dominate the population, especially for intermediate values of the synergy factor (r) and aid intensity (beta). The cooperation level is a non-monotonic function of both r and beta, with optimal intervals for each. Average payoffs per strategy are reported, showing that alliance players eventually outperform defectors and cooperators. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total welfare as a function of the alliance mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to mutual aid or alliance (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation and increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare, it is only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency effects of punishment. The findings suggest that payoff redistribution among cooperators can be highly effective in suppressing defection, but the results cannot be directly transferred to settings with explicit punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding the effects of mutual aid or alliance-type mechanisms, not punishment.

- source: Liu, Q., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2018). Evolutionary Public Goods Game on Evolving Random Networks. *Journal of the Korean Physical Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing informed cooperators to rewire their network connections at a cost can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in public goods games on random networks. Higher rates of network rewiring (by informed cooperators) lower the threshold for cooperation to emerge, especially under adverse conditions (low synergy factor). The system exhibits distinct phases (full cooperation, full defection, coexistence, and segregated sub-networks), and the transition between these phases depends on the synergy factor and rewiring rate. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not implement or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Instead, it studies the effect of costly information and network rewiring on cooperation rates. While the findings suggest that network adaptation can promote cooperation, they do not provide evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effect of punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, though it may be contextually relevant for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Morsky, B., & Akçay, E. (2021). False beliefs can bootstrap cooperative communities through social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that inaccurate, overly optimistic beliefs about the level of cooperation can bootstrap and sustain high cooperation in communities governed by conditional cooperation norms, even in anonymous settings. The model shows that the dynamics of joining, learning, and leaving the community can create bistability, abrupt transitions, or cycles in cooperation rates and community size. High cooperation can be achieved and maintained if naive (optimistic) individuals are present in sufficient numbers, and if learning the true (lower) level of cooperation is slow or if disillusioned individuals can easily leave. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but discusses how mechanisms that increase transparency or allow for punishment may interact with these dynamics. The findings are relevant for understanding how social norms and belief management can foster cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how belief management and social norm dynamics can affect cooperation rates in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. It suggests that mechanisms which slow learning about true cooperation levels or allow for easy exit can paradoxically increase cooperation by maintaining a pool of optimistic, conditionally cooperative individuals. However, because the model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual and highlights possible trade-offs between transparency (which may reduce cooperation by revealing low compliance) and incentive mechanisms (which may increase or decrease cooperation depending on beliefs). For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of belief dynamics and social norms as moderators, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that persistent cooperation mechanisms in continuous public goods games can substantially increase the average cooperation level, especially when agents participate in multiple groups and allocate investments preferentially to more cooperative groups. Multi-group games outperform single-group games, and non-even investment with preference yields the highest cooperation levels. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, but higher average cooperation levels are interpreted as beneficial for the group. The mechanisms are presented as alternatives to punishment and reward, which are not modeled or compared directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions as a treatment or variable. Instead, it analyzes alternative mechanisms (persistent cooperation) that promote cooperation without punishment. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how non-punitive mechanisms can enhance cooperation, but they do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, except as a conceptual contrast to punishment-based interventions.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews the development of experimental economics in the context of public goods and prosocial behavior, highlighting Andreoni's work on impure altruism, crowding out, and the rationality of giving. It discusses how early experiments showed voluntary contributions to public goods, the decline of contributions over repeated rounds, and the theoretical and empirical challenges to the 'free rider' hypothesis. The review emphasizes the importance of motives (e.g., warm-glow giving) and the incomplete crowding out of voluntary contributions by government intervention. It also highlights the rationality of prosocial choices as demonstrated by Andreoni and Miller (2002). However, the paper does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or data for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding the historical context and theoretical motivations behind public goods experiments, especially regarding voluntary contributions and prosocial motives. However, it does not analyze or report on punishment mechanisms, their design dimensions, or their effects on efficiency or payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers only background context and no actionable evidence.

The effect is stronger when the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is higher. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. - source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Yang, R. (2019). Using rewards reasonably: The effects of stratified-rewards in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that stratified-rewards can promote, maintain, or even inhibit efficiency (measured as available funds for public goods) depending on the scope and distribution of rewards. If the number of people who receive rewards is small and the number who get high-level rewards is controlled, available funds can increase. If too many people get high rewards, available funds can fall below the baseline, even if all players cooperate. The sum of contributions and the standard deviation of contributions are also affected by reward parameters. The fraction of cooperators is always high with rewards, but this does not guarantee high efficiency. The optimal reward design balances the number of recipients and the cost of rewards to maximize available funds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of reward mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like environments is highly sensitive to the design of the reward scheme. It shows that simply enabling rewards does not guarantee higher efficiency; the number of rewarded players, the proportion of high-level rewards, and the cost of rewards all interact to determine the net group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a reward mechanism can have positive, neutral, or negative effects on efficiency, depending on these design details. Since the paper does not study punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment, but it does highlight the importance of cost structures and incentive distribution in predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Alvarez-Rodriguez, U., Battiston, F., de Arruda, G. F., Moreno, Y., Perc, M., & Latora, V. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of higher-order interactions in social networks. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in PGGs with higher-order interactions (hypergraphs), the critical synergy factor required for cooperation decreases as group size increases in uniform random hypergraphs, meaning larger groups can foster cooperation more easily (at a given synergy factor). Heterogeneity in group membership (hyperdegree) and group size can either facilitate or hinder cooperation, depending on the structure: scale-free (hub-dominated) hypergraphs inhibit cooperation, while uniform or less heterogeneous structures do not. The framework is validated with empirical data from scientific collaborations, showing that the synergy factor (group benefit) is a non-monotonic function of group size, with an optimal group size for maximum benefit. The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency and payoff outcomes in PGG-like environments with varying group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. It is directly relevant for understanding how group size and network heterogeneity affect the critical conditions for cooperation and group payoff, but cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. Use this paper to inform baseline efficiency predictions in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but not for treatment effects of punishment.

- source: Wang, S., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2018). Non-Fixed Investment in Voluntary Public Goods Games. *Journal of the Korean Physical Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing non-fixed investment in voluntary PGGs leads to complex rock-scissors-paper dynamics among cooperators, defectors, and loners. Increasing the maximum possible investment ratio can initially promote cooperation and increase average payoff, but further increases may reduce cooperation due to increased temptation to defect. Higher interest rates (MPCR) and lower loner payoffs promote both cooperation and higher average payoffs. The memory length (sensitivity to past payoffs) also moderates outcomes: moderate memory promotes higher average payoffs and cooperation, while very short or very long memory is detrimental. The system always converges to a stable equilibrium regardless of initial conditions. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or discussed as interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the model or analysis. It is therefore not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how other design dimensions (investment flexibility, MPCR, loner payoff, memory) affect efficiency and cooperation in voluntary PGGs without punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Chen, W. M. (2019). The evolution of cooperation in public good game with deposit. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a deposit mechanism into the PGG can promote cooperation under certain parameter regimes. The equilibrium frequencies of cooperation, deposit cooperation, and defection depend on the deposit size, discount rate, benefit-sharing rule (weak vs. strong altruism), and the threshold for public good provision. Higher thresholds and weak altruism (where contributors also benefit) increase the equilibrium level of cooperation. Lower deposit and discount rates promote deposit cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for expected payoffs and equilibrium frequencies, but does not address punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as it does not include any punishment, sanction, or peer enforcement mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how deposit-based commitment mechanisms and benefit-sharing rules affect cooperation and payoffs in PGGs without punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Cooperation and competition between pair and multi-player social games in spatial populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary competition between 'alone' (pairwise) and 'together' (group) attitudes leads to complex phase behavior. At low public goods multiplication factors, pairwise interactions dominate; at intermediate values, both attitudes coexist but this can paradoxically lower average income; at high multiplication factors, group interactions dominate and cooperation can be sustained. The presence of the 'together' attitude at low multiplication factors can actually reduce average income, contrary to intuition. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding how the structure of interaction (pairwise vs. group) and the public goods multiplication factor affect efficiency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Stochastic dynamics and stable equilibrium of evolutionary optional public goods game in finite populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in an optional public goods game (with strategies: cooperate, defect, or opt out), the probability of the group reaching full cooperation, full defection, or universal non-participation depends on the investment return multiplier (r), the fixed income for non-participants (sigma), and the group size (N). Optional participation can prevent the spread of defection and, for certain parameter ranges (large r and sigma), can make full cooperation the most likely outcome. However, for small sigma or r, non-participation or defection may dominate. The model provides quantitative relationships between parameters and the likelihood of each stable state, which directly map to group efficiency. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly informative for predicting efficiency in optional public goods games without punishment or reward. It provides quantitative relationships between group size, number of rounds (implicitly infinite in the Markov process), MPCR (via r and N), and the probability of full cooperation (i.e., efficiency). However, it does not address the effect of punishment, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. It is most useful as a control or baseline for optional PGGs without sanctions.

- source: Tu, J. (2018). Contribution inequality in the spatial public goods game: Should the rich contribute more?. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when the rich contribute more (higher w), the effect on average wealth (group payoff) and cooperation rate depends on the enhancement factor (mpcr, or r/(z+1)). For low enhancement factors, higher w reduces both cooperation and average wealth. For intermediate enhancement, higher w can increase average wealth even as cooperation rate falls, because the extra contributions from the rich compensate for more defectors. For high enhancement, higher w increases both cooperation and average wealth, and reduces wealth inequality. The relationship between cooperation rate and group payoff is not always positive: higher cooperation does not always mean higher group wealth. The results are robust to population size but sensitive to network degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. However, it provides strong evidence that group payoff (efficiency) can be decoupled from cooperation rate, especially when contribution rules are heterogeneous. The findings suggest that interventions that change contribution rules (e.g., progressive contributions) can have complex effects on efficiency, depending on the enhancement factor. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant to punishment, but it is highly relevant to understanding how efficiency responds to other game design dimensions, especially mpcr and contribution heterogeneity.

- source: Li, X. Y., Chen, T., Chen, Q., & Zhang, X. Y. (2020). The impact of retention time of donation list on cooperation in public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that publishing a donation list with a moderate retention time and listing a moderate proportion of players can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators and the total sum of contributions in a public goods game. Instant or very frequent list updates are less effective than lists with longer retention times (but not excessively long). Listing too many or too few players is also suboptimal; an intermediate proportion maximizes contributions. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the sum of contributions is used as a proxy for public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it does provide informative evidence about the effects of reputation-based mechanisms (public listing, retention time, and partial disclosure) on cooperation and total contributions. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of non-punitive social incentives. The sum of contributions (S_c) is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

- source: Battu, B., & Srinivasan, N. (2020). Evolution of conditional cooperation in public good games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that high and stable cooperation (measured by donation rates) can be achieved in repeated public good games when agents' social learning is sufficiently biased toward prestige (social value) or when both prestige and payoff are weighted but with some noise. When imitation is driven mainly by payoff, cooperation collapses. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how social learning mechanisms (prestige vs. payoff) affect the evolution of cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that cooperation can be sustained by prestige-biased social learning even in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable quantitative or qualitative evidence about the efficiency impact of punishment or about moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Spatial public goods game with continuous contributions based on Particle Swarm Optimization learning and the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) learning rule can promote higher average cooperation levels in spatial public goods games with continuous contributions, compared to the Fermi rule and Genetic Algorithm learning. The network structure matters: a nearest-neighbor coupled network (1-D) is more conducive to cooperation than a square lattice (2-D). The memory/imitation parameter (c9) has a complex, non-linear effect on cooperation, depending on the investment enhancement factor (r). However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show how network structure and learning rules affect average cooperation levels in spatial continuous PGGs. It may inform expectations about baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment, but not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2021). Computational behavioral models in public goods games with migration between groups. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing unsatisfied players to migrate between groups increases average contribution in public goods games, especially for lower enhancement factors (r). The effect of group size and update timing (synchronous vs asynchronous) is minor. Heterogeneity in migration propensity has little effect on average contribution compared to a homogeneous population. The amount of information available to players (own payoff/contribution vs also knowing the number of contributors in the group) does not substantially change the results. However, the paper does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on migration as an alternative to punishment may be contextually informative for understanding other forms of exit or mobility, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes are average contribution rates, not efficiency or payoff, and thus are only adjacent to the prediction task.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Morales, A. J., & Walker, J. M. (2018). Varying experimental instructions to improve comprehension: Punishment in public goods games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, mechanisms that allow individuals to preferentially imitate high-reputation neighbors (high reputation reasoning ability) promote the emergence and stability of cooperation. However, when reputation is updated using second-order rules (taking into account both the individual's and the worst neighbor's actions), cooperation is inhibited compared to first-order rules, especially when the magnitude of reputation change is large. Heterogeneity in reasoning ability (both endogenous and exogenous) can reduce cooperation, particularly when the variance or degree of heterogeneity is high. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate; average payoffs are discussed in relation to strategy and reputation clusters but not as a primary outcome. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation-based social learning and information structures affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution, and some average payoffs by type) are not directly translatable to group efficiency as defined in the prediction task. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive social mechanisms (like reputation) affect cooperation.

- source: Kang, H. W., Zhou, X. J., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). Influencer propagation model promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing influencers with extra gain (parameter alpha) and varying their proportion (theta) can significantly promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the synergy factor (r) is low. The model shows that cooperative influencer groups can resist invasion by defectors and spread cooperation through the network. However, as r increases, the effect of influencers on promoting cooperation diminishes, and high values of alpha and theta can eventually stabilize both cooperative and betrayal groups. The main outcome is the proportion of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how leadership, influencer effects, and heterogeneity can promote cooperation, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings may be useful for adjacent questions about mechanisms that promote cooperation, but not for the specific prediction task of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency.

- source: Chen, Y. S., Yang, H. X., Guo, W. Z., & Liu, G. G. (2018). Promotion of cooperation based on swarm intelligence in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of swarm intelligence (where players update their strategies based on both their own best past strategy and the best current strategy in their neighborhood) into a continuous spatial public goods game leads to a strong promotion of cooperation. The level of cooperation depends on the multiplication factor (r) and the weight given to personal vs. neighborhood best strategies (c1). For low c1 (neighborhood-focused), cooperation can dominate at high r, but is vulnerable at low r. For high c1 (personal best-focused), cooperation is more stable even at low r, but the system is more diverse in strategies. The main reported outcome is the average cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how alternative strategy updating rules (swarm intelligence) affect cooperation rates, not payoffs or efficiency. It does, however, confirm that cooperation can be promoted by non-punitive mechanisms, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

- source: Gao, B., Liu, X., Lan, Z. Z., Hong, J., & Zhang, W. G. (2021). The evolution of cooperation with preferential selection in voluntary public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that when players preferentially imitate neighbors with higher payoffs (higher α), cooperation is promoted and loners become less prevalent. When α is low or negative (preference for low-payoff neighbors), loners dominate. The enhancement factor r (analogous to MPCR) also modulates the prevalence of cooperation, with higher r supporting more cooperation. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may provide some indirect context on how participation and imitation dynamics affect cooperation rates, but is not relevant for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Chang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., Li, Y., Wu, Y. E., & Xie, Y. Y. (2018). Investment preference promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially invest in groups with more cooperators (higher alpha) and to use longer memory of past group composition increases the fraction of cooperators in both well-mixed and structured populations. The mechanism leads to the formation of larger and more robust cooperation clusters, which can dominate the population under favorable conditions. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that mechanisms which allow for adaptive investment based on group composition can promote cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only contextual or adjacent evidence, not direct input.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Freedom to choose between public resources promotes cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing individuals to choose between multiple public goods games (resources) with different enhancement factors can promote cooperation. Defectors tend to cluster in the highest quality resource, while cooperators can 'shelter' in lower quality resources, avoiding exploitation. Cooperation is maximized when the two highest quality resources are similar in quality. The model is analyzed both analytically (replicator-mutator dynamics) and via simulation, showing robust results across parameter ranges. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how choice between public resources affects cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the impact of resource choice and group structure on cooperation.

- source: Liu, J. Z., Peng, M., Peng, Y. C., Li, Y., Chu, C., Li, X. Y., & Liu, Q. (2021). Effects of inequality on a spatial evolutionary public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that when individual investment ability and enhancement factor are positively correlated (aligned inequality), cooperation is strongly promoted, with the system reaching full cooperation at lower enhancement factors than in the traditional PGG. When these are negatively correlated (misaligned inequality), cooperation is suppressed and may disappear entirely. The mechanism is that high-contribution cooperators form the core of cooperative clusters, attracting and sustaining cooperation among lower-contribution individuals. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in endowments and productivity affects cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or the impact of punishment. It should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, M., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2019). Dynamic scale return coefficient with environmental feedback promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of a dynamic scale return coefficient, governed by the difference between a group's average reputation and the population average, promotes the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. Higher reputation amplitude (the parameter controlling the impact of reputation differences) lowers the threshold for the enhancement factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the stable level of cooperation. The effect is robust across lattice and small-world network structures. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is focused on a different cooperation-promoting mechanism (reputation-based environmental feedback) and does not include or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Quan, J., Zhou, Y. W., Zhang, M., Tang, C. X., & Wang, X. J. (2019). The impact of heterogeneous scale return coefficient between groups on the emergence of cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient between groups (i.e., making the returns to investment nonlinear and group-specific) generally increases the average cooperation level compared to a homogeneous (linear) setting. The effect depends on the type of heterogeneity (uniform, exponential, power-law) and its amplitude. Uniform heterogeneity is most conducive to high and stable cooperation in most parameter ranges, but exponential heterogeneity can outperform in some cases. Stronger heterogeneity (higher amplitude) generally promotes higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency-related outcomes. Its findings are limited to how heterogeneity in the scale return coefficient affects average cooperation (contribution) levels in a spatial, continuous-contribution PGG. It is not relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be contextually useful for understanding how nonlinearities and heterogeneity in returns affect cooperation rates.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Pan, Q. H., Ju, X. X., & He, M. F. (2021). Public goods game with the interdependence of different cooperative strategies. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games on coevolving networks where agents disconnect from neighbors with similar payoff and connect to those with different payoff, the maintenance of cooperation depends on the balance between these two tendencies. Cooperation is best sustained when the rates of disconnection and connection are moderate and well-matched. Too much network time-dependence (frequent rewiring) generally undermines cooperation compared to the classic static network case. The emergence and stability of cooperation are associated with changes in network cohesion, but the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how network coevolution (rewiring based on payoff similarity/difference) affects cooperation rates, not efficiency. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Zhang, L., Xie, Y., Huang, C. W., Li, H. H., & Dai, Q. L. (2020). Heterogeneous investments induced by historical payoffs promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in investment, where individuals allocate more to groups that have yielded higher payoffs in the recent past, significantly increases the fraction of cooperators in spatial PGGs. Shorter memory (small M) and lower weight on earlier payoffs (small or negative omega) further enhance cooperation. Stronger heterogeneity (higher alpha) also promotes cooperation. These effects are robust to changes in network topology (square lattice vs. ER random networks). However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that certain forms of heterogeneity in investment can promote cooperation rates, but the translation to efficiency or payoff outcomes is not addressed. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only adjacent, not direct, evidence.

- source: Huang, C. W., Han, W. C., Li, H. H., Cheng, H. Y., Dai, Q. L., & Yang, J. Z. (2019). Public cooperation in two-layer networks with asymmetric interaction and learning environments. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the structure of the interaction and learning networks (specifically, their overlap) can significantly affect the steady-state level of cooperation in a spatial PGG. The results are robust to changes in network topology and node degree. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how network structure (specifically, the overlap between interaction and learning networks) affects cooperation rates in PGGs without punishment. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Wu, Z. H., Huang, H., & Liao, Q. H. (2021). The study on the role of dedicators on promoting cooperation in public goods game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The presence of dedicators in a PGG model can strongly promote and stabilize cooperation, leading to higher total contributions. Dedicators' willingness to donate is more important than the amount they donate. Emotional effects—whereby others are inspired to cooperate by dedicator actions—further enhance cooperation. The system is robust to several parameter changes, but a too-high budget requirement can collapse cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled or analyzed. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in PGG-like environments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide indirect evidence that mechanisms other than punishment (such as the presence of prosocial 'dedicators' and emotional contagion) can promote cooperation and higher contributions. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency outcomes in the required sense.

- source: Gao, B., Li, X., Hou, S. X., Jia, D. Y., & Du, M. J. (2019). Resolving public goods dilemma by giving the poor more support. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that narrowing the income gap among group members (negative alpha, i.e., redistributive policies favoring the poor) increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial public goods game, while increasing income inequality (positive alpha) reduces cooperation. The model is simulated on a lattice, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators under different redistribution regimes. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about redistribution and cooperation rates may be of indirect interest for understanding how inequality and redistribution affect cooperation, but it is not relevant for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Naar, N. (2020). Gaming Anthropology: The Problem of External Validity and the Challenge of Interpreting Experimental Games. *American Anthropologist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that experimental economic games, including public goods games, show substantial variability in behavioral outcomes (such as cooperation and prosociality) across populations, subject pools, settings, and contextual frames. There is no reliable generalizability of results, and evidence for parallelism between game and real-world behavior is mixed. The review highlights the importance of context and framing in interpreting game results and cautions against assuming that game behavior reflects real-world preferences or norms. The paper does not address punishment or efficiency outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It is useful for understanding the limitations of generalizing behavioral results from experimental games, especially across different populations and contexts. However, it does not discuss punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and thus offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is as a caution about external validity and the importance of context, not as a source of quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or payoff effects.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Lv, S. J. (2019). The roles of particle swarm intelligence in the prisoner's dilemma based on continuous and mixed strategy systems on scale-free networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The authors propose a method to predict the strategies of players whose current actions are hidden, based on the similarity of their past behavior to that of observed players. The method is tested on both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods games on a spatial lattice. The prediction method achieves high accuracy (success rate up to 0.9) in identifying hidden strategies, with performance depending on the length of historical data and game parameters (temptation to defect in PDG, enhancement factor in PGG). The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Its findings are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Acemoglu, D., & Wolitzky, A. (2020). SUSTAINING COOPERATION: COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT VERSUS SPECIALIZED ENFORCEMENT. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal way to sustain cooperation in repeated public-goods-like games depends on the effectiveness of the punishment technology. If specialized enforcers can punish at low cost and high impact, then pure enforcer punishment (with immediate return to cooperation) maximizes efficiency. If specialized enforcement is less effective, then combining enforcer punishment with community enforcement (e.g., temporary withdrawal of cooperation or ostracism) is optimal. The paper provides explicit characterizations of the maximum sustainable cooperation (efficiency) in each regime, and shows that the presence of effective specialized enforcement can increase group welfare compared to pure community enforcement. However, if the punishment technology is very weak, community enforcement alone is nearly optimal. The results generalize to both small and large groups, and to settings with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of introducing punishment in public-goods-like games, conditional on the effectiveness of the punishment technology and other game design parameters. It shows that, when punishment is highly effective (i.e., the cost to punishers is low relative to the harm inflicted), enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency by supporting higher cooperation. However, if punishment is costly or ineffective, the efficiency gain is limited, and community enforcement may be nearly as good. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency as a function of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and discount factor. The results also highlight that the structure of punishment (one-time vs. repeated, enforcer vs. peer) and the possibility of ostracism or repentance are important moderators.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Liang, J. L. (2020). The effects of resource limitation and consensual incentives on cooperation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a nonlinear multiplayer public goods game with a threshold and resource limitation, the introduction of consensual incentives (punishment or reward, decided collectively) can promote cooperation and increase group payoff (efficiency), provided that the population's preference for incentives is sufficiently high. Without incentives, the system always evolves to full defection (zero efficiency). The effect of incentives is not monotonic in resource abundance: there is an intermediate resource level where the positive effect of incentives is minimized, and both scarcity and abundance of resources can promote cooperation when incentives are present. The model also shows that a lower consensus threshold for employing incentives and a higher preference for incentives both increase the basin of attraction for full cooperation. Consensual reward can be more effective than punishment in promoting cooperation when preferences for incentives are high.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment (or reward) in a nonlinear, threshold-based public goods game can shift the system from full defection (zero efficiency) to full cooperation (high efficiency), provided that the population's preference for incentives is above a critical value. The effect is sensitive to the consensus threshold for employing incentives, the abundance of resources, and the type of incentive. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially if it is collectively decided and the population is willing to use it) is likely to increase efficiency dramatically compared to the control (no punishment) case. However, the effect may be minimized at intermediate resource levels. The model is analytic and does not provide empirical effect sizes, but the qualitative direction is clear: enabling punishment (or reward) increases efficiency in this class of games.

- source: Kol'veková, G., Raisová, M., Zoricak, M., & Gazda, V. (2021). Endogenous Shared Punishment Model in Threshold Public Goods Games. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The endogenous shared punishment model, where agents vote on the level and cost of punishment, achieves higher mean payoffs (efficiency) than both the baseline (no punishment) and the cooperation (full punishment) models. In the standard scenario (group size 4, MPCR 0.5, threshold 60, endowment 30), the endogenous shared punishment model yields a weighted mean reward of 40.09, compared to 33.42 for the baseline and 35.04 for the cooperation model. The provision rate is 73.8% for the endogenous shared punishment model, 24.7% for the baseline, and 98.2% for the cooperation model. The endogenous shared punishment model achieves this efficiency at a very low cost of punishment, as pro-punishment votes and punishment costs decline over time as cooperation stabilizes. Adding 'activists' (altruistic contributors) further increases provision rates and mean payoffs. The model's results are robust to variations in group size, threshold, MPCR, and learning parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a low-cost, endogenous, shared punishment mechanism in threshold public goods games can substantially increase efficiency (mean group payoff) compared to both no-punishment and full-punishment regimes. The efficiency gains are achieved even when the provision rate is not maximized, due to the low cost of punishment and the stabilization of cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in TPGG-like environments, especially when punishment is endogenous, low-cost, and shared. The results are most applicable to games with group size 4, MPCR 0.5, and threshold 60, but the model explores a range of parameters. The paper's agent-based approach and focus on payoff outcomes make it highly informative for the prediction task.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2018). Punishment and inspection for governing the commons in a feedback-evolving game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Punishment and inspection mechanisms can promote cooperation and prevent overexploitation in a common-pool resource game, but their ability to improve group efficiency (resource sustainability) depends critically on the intrinsic growth rate of the resource. If the resource renews too slowly, even perfect cooperation enforced by strong punishment leads to resource depletion and low efficiency. At moderate growth rates, strong punishment and inspection can achieve both high cooperation and sustainable resource levels, but weak institutions result in mixed strategy populations and depleted resources. At high growth rates, the resource is always sustainable regardless of punishment, and punishment only affects the fraction of cooperators versus defectors. Thus, the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and can be absent or even negative if resource dynamics are unfavorable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like common-pool resource games is highly contingent on the ecological context, especially the resource's intrinsic growth rate. When predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions (such as punishment cost and effectiveness) but also the underlying dynamics of the resource. In settings where the resource cannot recover quickly, punishment may not improve efficiency even if it increases cooperation. In more favorable ecological conditions, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently effective (high detection probability and/or fine). Thus, the paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universal and must be interpreted in light of resource dynamics.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Shrestha, M. D. (2021). Evolving institutions for collective action by selective imitation and self-interested design. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of collective action with institutionalized punishment by leaders, both selective imitation and self-interested design (foresight) in leaders can lead to the emergence of effective institutions that increase cooperation, group production, and payoffs (efficiency). The presence of leaders who monitor and punish free-riders increases group effort and payoffs compared to groups without leaders or punishment. The effect is strongest when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high, punishment is not too costly, and leaders value future payoffs. The model also shows that the details of the updating mechanism (imitation vs. foresight) matter most when the benefit-to-cost ratio is intermediate and leaders heavily discount the future. In 'us vs. nature' games, increased monitoring and production lead to higher payoffs for all; in 'us vs. them' games, overproduction can reduce payoffs for subordinates. The model provides direct evidence that institutionalized punishment can increase efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling institutionalized punishment by leaders in public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and punishment is not too costly. The effect is robust to variations in group size, punishment cost, and the leader's share of rewards. For prediction tasks, the presence of leader-administered punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), with the magnitude depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio, punishment cost, and whether leaders value future payoffs. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is less positive or even negative if punishment is very costly or if leaders do not value future payoffs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with institutionalized punishment, especially when the control efficiency is low due to free-riding.

- source: Wang, M., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). The role of alliance cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with probabilistic punishment of defectors, increasing the monitoring probability and punishment severity generally increases the proportion of cooperators and can help maintain higher resource levels (i.e., higher efficiency/welfare). However, if the initial resource level is too low or the resource's maximum growth rate is insufficient, the system will experience resource depletion (tragedy of the commons) regardless of punishment strength. The model demonstrates bistability: depending on initial conditions, the system may converge to full cooperation with resource depletion, or to a mixed/cooperative state with sustainable resources. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but only when ecological conditions allow for resource regeneration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource environments is conditional: punishment (higher probability and severity) can increase efficiency and resource sustainability, but only if the resource's regenerative capacity and initial abundance are sufficient. In settings where the resource cannot recover (low growth rate or low initial stock), punishment does not prevent collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by ecological parameters (resource growth, initial abundance) in addition to standard game design dimensions. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the efficiency outcome is directly modeled.

- source: Góis, A. R., Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2019). Reward and punishment in climate change dilemmas. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (threshold public goods games with risk of group failure), both reward and punishment policies improve group achievement (η_G) compared to no policy. Reward is particularly effective at initiating cooperation when the risk of failure is low, while punishment is more effective at sustaining high levels of cooperation, especially when the risk is high or the threshold for group success is stringent. The optimal policy is to use reward when the fraction of cooperators is low and switch to punishment when cooperation is sufficiently high. The model provides explicit formulas for the optimal switching point and shows that combining both policies synergistically yields the highest group achievement.
