The results are robust across a range of parameter values, including group size, population size, and policy efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (and/or reward) in threshold public goods games will increase group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to a control without such mechanisms. The model allows for direct mapping of game design dimensions such as player count, group size, number of rounds (in evolutionary time), presence and efficiency of punishment and reward, and the structure of the public goods dilemma (threshold, risk). The findings suggest that punishment is most effective at maintaining cooperation once it is established, while reward is more important for initiating cooperation. The optimal policy is a dynamic combination of both. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that adding punishment (especially with sufficient budget and efficiency) will generally increase efficiency, but the effect size and optimality depend on the risk, threshold, and whether reward is also available. The model is theoretical, so empirical calibration may be needed for quantitative predictions.

- source: Bolle, F. (2021). Deterrence by Collective Punishment May Work against Criminals but Never against Freedom Fighters. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that collective punishment (CP) is ineffective or counterproductive in threshold public good games where resistance is individually costly but can produce a public good (e.g., protests, strikes). In these cases, increasing the threat or severity of CP does not increase compliance and may even increase resistance. In contrast, in threshold public bad games (e.g., pollution, crime), CP can sometimes increase compliance, but only when the individual benefit from misbehavior is small compared to the social cost. The paper provides formal equilibrium analysis and discusses equilibrium selection, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for predicting the behavioral effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding when punishment is likely to deter undesirable behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on when collective punishment is likely to be effective in public-goods-like games. It suggests that in threshold public good games (where cooperation is costly and produces a group benefit), punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or compliance, and may even be counterproductive. In threshold public bad games (where defection is individually profitable but socially costly), punishment can increase compliance and, by implication, efficiency, but only when the individual incentive to defect is not too large. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions for efficiency, but its qualitative results can inform predictions about the likely direction of punishment effects given the structure of the game (public good vs. public bad, cost/benefit ratios). However, because the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, its support for efficiency prediction is indirect and should be supplemented with empirical or simulation evidence.

- source: Sun, W. W., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2021). Combination of institutional incentives for cooperative governance of risky commons. *Iscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk social dilemma games, institutional pure reward strategies (where executors only reward cooperators) are more effective than pure punishment or hybrid strategies in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target). This result holds for both fixed and flexible incentive schemes, and is robust to changes in risk level, mutation rate, and enhancement factor. Local sanctioning schemes are more effective than global ones. The main outcome is group achievement, not efficiency or total group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that, in collective-risk social dilemmas, institutional punishment alone is less effective than reward or hybrid schemes in promoting group achievement (a proxy for cooperation). However, because the main outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather the fraction of groups reaching a behavioral target, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction. The game structure (collective-risk, institutional incentives, evolutionary dynamics) is close but not identical to standard PGGs. The results suggest that institutional punishment may not increase efficiency as much as reward, but do not provide quantitative efficiency estimates.

- source: Perry, L., Shrestha, M. D., Vose, M. D., & Gavrilets, S. (2018). Collective Action Problem in Heterogeneous Groups with Punishment and Foresight. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing punishment in heterogeneous groups increases cooperation and group effort, especially when individuals have foresight and can anticipate the effects of their actions on others' future behavior. Foresight enables punishment to become utility-increasing, overcoming the second-order free-rider problem. However, the costs of punishment can be substantial, so higher cooperation does not always translate into higher group payoffs or efficiency. The model predicts a division of labor, with dominant individuals specializing in punishment and subordinates in contribution. The effect of punishment on group efficiency is context-dependent: it can be positive, negative, or neutral depending on parameter values (e.g., group size, benefit/cost ratio, hierarchy steepness, precision, and foresight weight).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the interplay between group heterogeneity, foresight, and the costs of punishment. If punishment is cheap and foresight is present, enabling punishment is more likely to increase efficiency by raising cooperation. If punishment is costly, the net effect on efficiency may be negative, as the costs of punishment can outweigh the gains from increased contributions. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is sensitive to group size (larger groups make cooperation harder), the benefit/cost ratio, and the degree of hierarchy. For prediction, the presence of foresight and the relative costs of punishment are key moderators. The model does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers qualitative guidance on when punishment will or will not improve efficiency.

- source: Lee, J. H., Iwasa, Y., Dieckmann, U., & Sigmund, K. (2019). Social evolution leads to persistent corruption. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This theoretical study shows that while institutional punishment can, in principle, sustain cooperation and high payoffs in public-goods-like settings, the possibility of corruption (i.e., punishment institutions accepting bribes) leads to cycles or stable states of reduced cooperation and lower average payoffs. The model demonstrates that transparency and monitoring are necessary to maintain honest punishment, but these are costly and thus not always sustained. As a result, the efficiency gains from punishment are not robust: corruption can cause efficiency to fall well below the cooperative optimum, and the system may oscillate between periods of high and low efficiency depending on the prevalence of corruption and monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: enabling punishment in a public-goods-like game does not guarantee increased efficiency if the punishment institution can be corrupted. The efficiency benefit of punishment depends critically on the integrity of the institution and the cost and prevalence of monitoring. In prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether corruption is possible and how transparency is maintained. If the game design allows for institutional corruption or lacks mechanisms for transparency and monitoring, enabling punishment may not improve—and may even reduce—efficiency relative to the control. The model also highlights the importance of considering dynamic and cyclical effects, not just static outcomes.

- source: Currie, T. E., Campenni, M., Flitton, A., Njagi, T., Ontiri, E., Perret, C., & Walker, L. (2021). The cultural evolution and ecology of institutions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that institutions, including punishment mechanisms, can alter the payoffs in collective action problems such as public goods games, making cooperation more stable and frequent. The agent-based model demonstrates that when agents can collectively set punishment levels, cooperation can emerge and be maintained, with higher payoffs for cooperators and a reduction in free-riders. The model is illustrative and not based on empirical data. Macro-scale analyses show that institutional complexity and inclusivity are associated with higher economic development, but these are not tested in controlled PGG-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment mechanisms in public goods games can increase cooperation and, in model terms, improve payoffs for cooperators. However, it does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in controlled experimental settings. The agent-based model suggests that the ability to set and adjust punishment can help overcome collective action problems, but the findings are illustrative and not directly transferable to quantitative prediction tasks. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing the role of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Couto, M. C., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2020). Governance of risky public goods under graduated punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Graduated institutional punishment, where the severity and cost of punishment increase with the number of defectors, is more effective than strict (fixed) punishment in promoting cooperation and public good achievement in collective-risk dilemmas. This effect is especially pronounced at low risk levels and when both fines and costs are graduated. The model shows that graduated punishment reduces the time populations spend in all-defector states and increases the prevalence of cooperation and sustainable institutions. The approach also helps solve the second-order free-rider problem associated with institutional punishment. The findings are robust across a range of parameters and extend to linear public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in threshold public goods games with risk and institutional punishment, making punishment and its costs graduated (i.e., increasing with the number of defectors) leads to higher group achievement and overall welfare than strict, fixed punishment. While the main outcomes are not reported as efficiency ratios, the close relationship between group achievement, average fitness, and efficiency means the results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure (threshold, risk, institutional punishment), enabling graduated punishment will likely increase efficiency relative to strict punishment, especially at low risk and when punishment costs are also graduated. The results are robust to changes in group size, threshold, and other parameters.

- source: Li, Q. R., Li, Y. Q., Li, K., Chen, L., Zheng, Q., & Chen, K. (2020). Study on the influence of subjective well-being on travel mode selection. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing both punishment and reward mechanisms, based on subjective well-being thresholds, greatly increases the proportion of travelers choosing the cooperative (low-carbon) option. Punishment is found to be more effective than reward in stabilizing high levels of cooperation. The ease of triggering incentives (by narrowing the threshold interval) further boosts cooperation. However, the study does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (and to a lesser extent, reward) mechanisms can substantially increase cooperation rates in a PGG-like setting. However, because the model and results focus on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The model structure and parameterization (e.g., cost and effectiveness of punishment/reward) are informative for understanding how design dimensions might affect cooperation, but not directly efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Continuous spatial public goods game with self and peer punishment based on particle swarm optimization. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both self-punishment (modeled as a fitness penalty for contributing less than peers) and peer punishment (costly punishment of low contributors by others) substantially increase the average cooperation level in a continuous spatial PGG. Lower tolerance for low contributions and higher punishment intensity both promote higher cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of memory and imitation parameters. However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the increased cooperation translates into higher group efficiency, especially given the costs of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both self-punishment and peer punishment mechanisms can increase average cooperation rates in continuous spatial PGGs, especially when tolerance is low and punishment intensity is high. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The cost of punishment is modeled and subtracted from individual fitness, but the aggregate effect on group payoff or efficiency is not reported. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that punishment increases cooperation, it does not directly inform whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency in these settings.

- source: He, N. R., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Central governance based on monitoring and reporting solves the collective-risk social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of central governance based on monitoring and reporting, with top-down punishment of defectors, can dramatically increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial collective-risk social dilemma. There are threshold effects: small increases in monitoring probability, punishment fine, or decreases in reporting cost can cause a sudden jump from low to high cooperation. The effect is stronger under harsh conditions (low synergy factor). However, the study focuses on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that top-down punishment based on monitoring and reporting can increase cooperation rates in spatial collective-risk dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar spatial PGG-like environments, enabling punishment via monitoring/reporting is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if contributions are productive, but the paper does not quantify this. The model's details (e.g., spatial structure, overlapping groups, no explicit cost of punishment to individuals) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Nhim, T., Richter, A., & Zhu, X. Q. (2019). The resilience of social norms of cooperation under resource scarcity and inequality - An agent-based model on sharing water over two harvesting seasons. *Ecological Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that strong punishment (high punishment strength) and high social capital (frequent encounters) support the emergence and resilience of cooperation in a common-pool resource dilemma. However, resource scarcity, high variability in resource inflow, and increased inequality (in land or skill) undermine cooperation, making collapse of social norms more likely. The system tends toward either full cooperation or full defection, with little evidence for stable mixed equilibria. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the collapse of cooperation is interpreted as a breakdown of socially optimal resource use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like resource dilemmas, but its effectiveness is strongly moderated by resource scarcity, variability, and inequality. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally similar, with a focus on seasonal water allocation and peer punishment. The main outcome is the presence or absence of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so the paper is most useful for predicting the conditions under which punishment will be effective at sustaining cooperation, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency gains. The findings suggest that in environments with high scarcity, variability, or inequality, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency (if any) is likely to be weaker or absent.

- source: Korman, A., & Vacus, R. (2021). On the role of hypocrisy in escaping the tragedy of the commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in large networked public goods games with negligible MPCR, cooperation can emerge from a population of mostly defectors if mild but appropriately calibrated punishment (social-pressure) is applied, especially when an intermediate behavioral type (hypocrisy) is present. The emergence of cooperation is highly sensitive to the level of punishment against hypocritical players: too little or too much punishment leads to persistent defection or hypocrisy, while an intermediate level enables a transition to widespread cooperation. The model is theoretical and supported by numerical simulations, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the conditions under which punishment can promote cooperation in large public goods games with negligible MPCR. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the calibration of punishment levels, especially toward intermediate types like hypocrites. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental or empirical settings. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment calibration and the role of intermediate behavioral types in the dynamics of cooperation.

- source: Solferino, N., Solferino, V., & Taurino, S. F. (2018). The economics analysis of a Q-learning model of cooperation with punishment and risk taking preferences. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public-goods-like game modeled with Q-learning, punishment can induce a stable cooperative equilibrium, but only if individuals have high enough exploration rates (i.e., are willing to try new strategies rather than always exploiting the best-known one). The required punishment must be proportional to the temptation to free-ride and increases with the exploration rate. If individuals are highly exploitative (low exploration), punishment is less effective unless intrinsic motivation for cooperation is high. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher cooperation rates would generally be associated with higher efficiency in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public-goods-like environments, but its effect depends on agents' willingness to explore new strategies (exploration rate/risk preference). The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in settings where learning and exploration are important. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it provides only indirect evidence for the prediction task. The model suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when exploration rates are high, but does not quantify efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ringsmuth, A. K., Lade, S. J., & Schlüter, M. (2019). Cross-scale cooperation enables sustainable use of a common-pool resource. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that even weak social sanctioning (punishment) between communities can prevent the collapse of cooperation that would otherwise occur due to biophysical coupling (resource sharing). When only biophysical coupling is present and communities do not communicate or sanction each other, increased resource access can undermine cooperation in one community. However, if communities can sanction defectors in both communities, or if they communicate when setting norms, cooperation is more robust. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation or defection collapses, depending on the strength of biophysical and social coupling and on whether communities collaborate in norm setting. The findings highlight the importance of cross-community sanctioning and communication for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (social sanctioning) in public-goods-like environments can prevent the collapse of cooperation that may occur due to resource sharing or biophysical coupling. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect. The results suggest that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms, as well as opportunities for communication and norm setting, are important moderators of cooperation stability. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will have a more positive effect on cooperation (and likely efficiency) when social coupling is present, especially if communities can communicate and set norms collaboratively. However, quantitative predictions about efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Redhead, D., Dhaliwal, N., & Cheng, J. T. (2021). Taking charge and stepping in: Individuals who punish are rewarded with prestige and dominance. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that high-status individuals (prestige or dominance) are more likely to engage in costly punishment in collective action settings, such as public goods games, because their status lowers the net cost and risk of punishment. Punishment can further increase their prestige or dominance through reputational rewards. The review highlights that punishment is not always altruistic and may be motivated by self-interest, especially when reputational gains are possible. The paper also notes that the net payoff of punishment depends on the balance of reputational gains and costs (including risk of retaliation), and that these dynamics are context-dependent. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual background on the social and reputational mechanisms that may moderate the effects of punishment in public goods and collective action games. It suggests that the effectiveness and cost-benefit of punishment may depend on the status of the punisher, the risk of retaliation, and the cultural or institutional context. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions. Thus, while it is useful for understanding potential moderators and mechanisms, it offers limited direct support for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters.

- source: Kosfeld, M. (2020). The role of leaders in inducing and maintaining cooperation: The CC strategy. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Drawing on a range of lab and field experiments, the paper finds that: (1) Groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators achieve better efficiency and group outcomes; (2) Punishment of non-cooperation (especially when administered by leaders with prosocial motives) increases group efficiency and cooperation, as shown in field data from Ethiopian forest user groups and lab public goods games; (3) Antisocial punishment by leaders is associated with significantly worse group outcomes; (4) Trust by leaders (as opposed to control) can increase efficiency in principal-agent settings; (5) Self-selection mechanisms can help attract motivated types, further improving group efficiency. The paper provides strong evidence that punishment, when properly targeted and motivated, is a key mechanism for sustaining high efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games will increase group efficiency, especially when punishment is prosocially motivated and not antisocial. The evidence is drawn from both lab and field settings, including real-world resource management and controlled experiments. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the motives of the punisher (leader or group member), the presence of motivated types, and the structure of the group. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, particularly when the punishment technology is effective and antisocial punishment is minimized. The paper also highlights the importance of leader behavior and group composition, which may interact with the game design dimensions.

- source: Bhui, R., Chudek, M., & Henrich, J. (2019). How exploitation launched human cooperation. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative indirect reciprocity (NIR)—whereby individuals are allowed to exploit those with bad reputations—can suppress exploitation and support the emergence and maintenance of cooperation and public goods provision. The model shows that cooperation is most stable when exploitation is inefficient (harm to victim > gain to exploiter), and that as the frequency of opportunities for reputation improvement increases, the minimum benefit required for stable cooperation also increases. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable, and shows that NIR can serve as a foundation for more complex forms of cooperation. The overall effect of NIR is to increase group efficiency and welfare by reducing exploitation and supporting norm adherence, especially in early or cognitively simple societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, tolerated exploitation of ill-reputed individuals) can increase group efficiency and public goods provision, especially when exploitation is inefficient and when opportunities for norm adherence are not too frequent. The model gives explicit payoff-based conditions for when cooperation is stable, which can inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when the cost to the victim of exploitation is high relative to the benefit to the exploiter, and when errors in reputation assignment are low. The model also highlights that as norm adherence becomes more mandatory and frequent, the requirements for stable cooperation become stricter, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency in some parameter regimes.

- source: Zhou, K., & Ren, T. Y. (2021). Low-carbon technology collaborative innovation in industrial cluster with social exclusion: An evolutionary game theory perspective. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a social exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a cost to expel defectors from the public goods game) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a networked public goods environment. The effect is stronger when the cost of exclusion is low and the probability of successful exclusion is high. Sparse network structures (low average degree) are more conducive to sustaining cooperation. Voluntary participation (allowing loners) also helps prevent defection from dominating. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, social exclusion) can increase cooperation rates in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and the network is sparse. However, because the model and results focus on behavioral outcomes (cooperation level) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The model's structure and parameter explorations may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more likely to be effective, but do not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes for the prediction task.

- source: Song, Q., Cao, Z. H., Tao, R., Jiang, W., Liu, C., & Liu, J. Z. (2020). Conditional neutral punishment promotes cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that language and symbolic communication fundamentally alter the possibilities for collective action by simplifying coordination, reducing the costs of monitoring and punishing free riders, and amplifying reputation and signaling effects. It reviews theoretical models and empirical studies (from both ethnography and experiments) showing that communication (including chat, gossip, and feedback) increases cooperation rates and the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemmas. However, it does not report direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling communication (chat, language, gossip) in public goods games and collective action settings increases cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment, which should, in principle, improve group outcomes and efficiency. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of communication and norm establishment as moderators of punishment effectiveness.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Power, E. A. (2021). No good deed goes unpunished: the social costs of prosocial behaviour. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that prosocial acts can incur social costs, especially when observers infer self-interested motives or feel disadvantaged by others' generosity. It reviews evidence that in public goods games and similar environments, cooperators can be punished (antisocial punishment) or evicted, and that overtly generous individuals may be derogated or disliked. The review suggests that these social costs can limit the emergence and scale of cooperation, and that reputation management strategies (such as modesty or anonymity) are often used to avoid such costs. The paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, but highlights the importance of context, observer motives, and cultural variation in shaping responses to prosocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for understanding when punishment or sanctions may have counterproductive effects in public goods games and related environments, particularly through antisocial punishment of cooperators. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in a specific game design. It does suggest that the presence of antisocial punishment and reputational risks may reduce the positive impact of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, especially in contexts where observers feel threatened or infer strategic motives. For prediction tasks, this review supports the idea that punishment does not always increase efficiency and that context and social perception are important moderators.

- source: Skarzhinskaya, Y. M., & Tsurikov, V. I. (2021). Organizational change: from community to firm. *Rossiiskii Zhurnal Menedzhmenta-russian Management Journal*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper develops mathematical models of a self-governed collective where individual effort generates aggregate income, distributed by pre-negotiated shares. In the baseline, self-interested behavior leads to an inefficient Nash equilibrium. When a coalition forms and coordinates effort, the coalition's and its members' payoffs increase. If the coalition offers contracts to non-members (who then forego their share in exchange for a reward per effort), the organization transforms into a hierarchical structure, and under certain conditions, all agents' payoffs increase, reaching the communal optimum. The paper provides theorems showing that expanding the coalition always increases the sum of payoffs for coalition members and the new joiner, and can also increase payoffs for non-coalition members. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with no empirical or experimental results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms enabling coordination, selective incentives, or contracts (analogous to institutionalized punishment or reward) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not model explicit punishment, it analyzes how selective incentives and contracts can overcome inefficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing mechanisms that align individual and group incentives (such as contracts or selective rewards) can increase group efficiency, even in the absence of explicit punishment. The findings are most relevant for settings where coalition formation or contracting is possible, and less directly informative for standard PGGs with simple punishment options.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2019). Three-player repeated games with an opt-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a three-player repeated public goods game with an opt-out option (no explicit punishment), both tolerant and intolerant cooperators can survive, but their coexistence is not an evolutionary outcome. The equilibrium frequency of cooperators (and thus average payoff) increases with the benefit-to-cost ratio and decreases with the probability of group dissolution. The model shows that (partially) cooperative stable equilibria can exist, but the all-defector equilibrium is always asymptotically stable, making the initial evolution of cooperation difficult without additional mechanisms. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding the effect of voluntary partner refusal (opt-out) on cooperation and efficiency in small-group public goods games, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. It provides analytic results on how group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and group dissolution probability affect equilibrium cooperation and payoffs. However, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present. Its results may be useful as a baseline for comparison to punishment-enabled games, especially regarding the efficiency achievable through partner choice alone.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Strategy intervention in spatial voluntary public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This simulation study finds that a centralized intervention mechanism (distinct from peer or pool punishment) can increase the average total payoff of the group and reduce income inequality, provided the intervention is sufficiently strong (i.e., the product of intervention degree and intensity exceeds a threshold). Weak intervention can actually reduce cooperation and group payoff compared to no intervention. The model provides explicit parameter ranges for when full cooperation and maximum efficiency are achieved. The intervention cost is shared by all, and the mechanism is implemented by a central authority, not by individuals punishing each other.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that centralized, institutionally-enforced interventions (akin to pool punishment but implemented as forced contributions rather than direct payoff reduction) can increase efficiency in spatial voluntary PGGs, but only if the intervention is strong enough. The effect is non-monotonic: weak intervention can be counterproductive. The findings are most relevant for settings with voluntary participation, spatial structure, and centralized enforcement. The explicit mapping between intervention parameters and efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of institutional punishment-like mechanisms, especially when the control (no intervention) efficiency is low. However, the mechanism is not standard punishment, so direct transfer to peer punishment settings should be cautious.

- source: Nyborg, K. (2018). Reciprocal climate negotiators. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that reciprocal preferences can, under certain conditions (strong and widespread reciprocity), stabilize the grand coalition in a public-goods-like coalition game, leading to high group welfare (efficiency). If only a minority of countries are reciprocal, or if reciprocity is weak, the only stable coalitions are small and provide little welfare improvement over the non-cooperative baseline. The model demonstrates that the presence of reciprocal preferences can shift the game from a pure Prisoner's Dilemma to a coordination game in utilities, but this does not guarantee high efficiency unless reciprocity is strong and common. The paper does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning institutions, but reciprocity can act as a form of endogenous social sanctioning. The welfare/efficiency effects of reciprocity are thus mixed: potentially large if conditions are right, but often minimal in realistic parameter ranges.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that social preferences (reciprocity) can, in principle, increase efficiency in public-goods-like coalition games, but only if they are strong and widespread. In the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms, reciprocity alone is not a reliable path to high efficiency unless the population is highly reciprocal. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling social preference mechanisms (like reciprocity) may only improve efficiency if the relevant parameters (strength and prevalence of reciprocity) are high. The model does not address explicit punishment, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled games is limited, but it does show that endogenous social preferences can sometimes substitute for formal sanctions in supporting cooperation and efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented via social preferences rather than institutionalized mechanisms.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). Benefits of asynchronous exclusion for the evolution of cooperation in stochastic evolutionary optional public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asynchronous exclusion (where excluders act sequentially and avoid redundant costs) is more effective at promoting cooperation than synchronous exclusion (where all excluders act simultaneously and pay redundant costs). This effect is strongest when the probability of exclusion success is high and the public goods multiplier is low (well-mixed) or intermediate (structured). The main reported outcomes are the probability of the system reaching a cooperative state and the frequency of different strategies at equilibrium. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effectiveness of exclusion (a sanctioning mechanism) in promoting cooperation in optional public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the increased probability of reaching cooperative states under asynchronous exclusion suggests that, under some parameter regimes, more effective or less costly sanctioning can increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not measured, and because exclusion differs from standard punishment (it removes defectors from benefit sharing rather than imposing a direct cost), the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs. The results are most relevant for settings where exclusion is the main sanction and where the cost structure of sanctioning can be varied.

- source: Min, Y., Du, Y. C., & Jin, C. (2018). The effect of link rewiring on a coevolutionary common pool resource game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to rewire their links away from defectors (link rewiring) in a CPR game can prevent the collapse of cooperation and resource depletion, especially in sparsely connected networks and when resource inflow is high. The model uses a costless social ostracism mechanism (reputation-based exclusion) rather than costly punishment. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperators and resource abundance, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that adaptive network structure (partner switching) can be a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but the effects depend on network density and ecological parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of costly punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games. Instead, it analyzes a costless ostracism/reputation mechanism and the impact of adaptive network rewiring. While the findings are relevant for understanding how social structure and reputation can support cooperation, they do not inform predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The model and results are adjacent to the prediction task, offering indirect insight into the role of network adaptation and costless sanctions, but not the direct impact of costly punishment on efficiency.

- source: Pedroso, M. (2021). Blind Cooperation: The Evolution of Redundancy via Ignorance. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that redundancy in group cooperation can evolve without targeted punishment or coercion if individuals are ignorant of others' strategies and/or face harsh environments. In a threshold public goods game, when players do not know what others will do, they cooperate with an intermediate probability, leading to more cooperators than strictly necessary (redundancy). Harsher environments (higher group-level cost of failure) increase the probability of cooperation and thus redundancy. In contrast, if individuals are well-informed about others' strategies, redundancy does not evolve without punishment. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, but discusses these concepts in relation to the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how information availability and environmental harshness affect cooperation and redundancy in threshold public goods games. It suggests that punishment is not strictly necessary for redundancy or intermediate cooperation rates if players are ignorant of others' actions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of information and environmental context as moderators, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Senthilnathan, A., & Gavrilets, S. (2021). The dynamics of cooperation, power, and inequality in a group-structured society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and efficiency in group-structured societies depend strongly on group size, the degree of rivalrousness of goods, and the strength of institutional checks and balances (modeled by the incumbency parameter ε). Stronger checks and balances (lower ε) promote cooperation and reduce inequality. Larger groups tend to be more powerful and successful in between-group cooperation, except when goods are highly rivalrous, in which case smaller groups may outperform larger ones (Olson's paradox). The model predicts multiple stable equilibria and non-equilibrium dynamics depending on parameter values. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; these are only discussed as possible future extensions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group size, benefit parameters, and rivalrousness affect efficiency and payoff outcomes in nested public-goods-like environments. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the role of structural and institutional moderators, but not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Hu, L. W., He, N. R., Weng, Q. F., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2020). Rewarding endowments lead to a win-win in the evolution of public cooperation and the accumulation of common resources. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: Rewarding endowments in a collective-risk social dilemma (a PGG variant with environmental feedback) can create a win-win for cooperation and resource accumulation, but only in a specific parameter region: low basic endowment and intermediate reward intensity. High reward intensity or high basic endowment can lead to resource exhaustion, even if cooperation is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctions. The main outcomes are the amount of common resources (group welfare) and a composite index of cooperation and resource accumulation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it studies only reward mechanisms and does not include punishment or sanctions. However, it provides evidence that reward mechanisms can increase both cooperation and group welfare, but only under certain parameter regimes. The findings suggest that the design of incentive mechanisms (reward intensity, basic endowment) can have non-monotonic effects on group efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of reward as a moderator or alternative.

- source: Lee, J. H., Yamaguchi, R., Yokomizo, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2020). Preservation of the value of rice paddy fields: Investigating how to prevent farmers from abandoning the fields by means of evolutionary game theory. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation (maintenance effort) and thus group efficiency can be sustained when responsibility for collective action (maintenance) is local (among neighbors), but not when it is global (all players responsible for all facilities). Maintenance effort promotes cultivation effort, but not vice versa. Spatial structure and local interaction are key to sustaining group welfare. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in nonlinear public goods games with spatial structure and local cooperation, but not to games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that local responsibility and spatial structure can sustain cooperation and group efficiency in nonlinear public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative. However, it is highly informative for understanding the baseline efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of spatial structure, local vs. global responsibility, and ecological parameters. It can help predict when efficiency will be high or low in the absence of punishment, but not the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Joshi, J., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2020). Emergence of social inequality in the spatial harvesting of renewable public goods. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that when harvesting efficiency is low, all agents evolve to be sedentary and harvest at low rates, resulting in equitable but inefficient resource use. As harvesting efficiency increases, agents become mobile, and resource extraction approaches the maximum sustainable yield, achieving both efficiency and equity. Further increases in harvesting efficiency or reductions in dispersal cost lead to overexploitation and the emergence of social inequality, with a minority of mobile, overexploitative agents and a majority of frugal, sedentary agents. Rapid social learning (impatience) and local information (myopia) further aggravate inequality and inefficiency. The model identifies key moderators of efficiency and equity but does not include or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how efficiency in spatial public goods–like environments depends on ecological and behavioral parameters such as harvesting efficiency, dispersal cost, consumer density, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are absent from the model. Therefore, while the findings are useful for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions and for understanding the role of mobility and social learning, the paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to the introduction of punishment. It is best used for baseline efficiency estimation and for understanding moderators of overexploitation and inequality in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Yamamoto, T., Ito, H., Nii, M., Okabe, T., Morita, S., & Yoshimura, J. (2019). A single 'weight-lifting' game covers all kinds of games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper demonstrates mathematically that the weight-lifting game can represent all major two-player cooperation/defection games by adjusting three parameters: the incremental success probabilities from cooperation and the cost-to-benefit ratio. It provides a unified payoff matrix and phase diagrams showing how efficiency and cooperation depend on these parameters. The model is shown to encompass the standard public goods game as a special case when the success probability increases linearly with the number of cooperators. The paper does not consider punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how efficiency and group payoff outcomes in public-goods-like games depend on the underlying payoff structure, especially the cost/benefit ratio and the synergy of cooperation. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanism, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for baseline modeling of efficiency in the absence of punishment, or for understanding how the structure of the game (e.g., synergy, cost/benefit) sets the stage for potential intervention effects.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Evolution of Public Cooperation in a Risky Society with Heterogeneous Assets. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that asset heterogeneity (a large gap between rich and poor) hinders cooperation, regardless of the temptation to defect. Increasing the value of individual assets or the risk of collective loss promotes cooperation. High thresholds and low risk can destroy cooperation, especially when asset inequality is high. The proportion of poor in the population also affects cooperation: when the poor are a small minority, they tend to free ride; when they are a majority, more contribute. The model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative about how asset heterogeneity, risk, and threshold affect cooperation rates in threshold public goods games with collective risk. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide contextual insight into how structural features (like risk and heterogeneity) affect cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Friehe, T., & Tabbach, A. (2018). A comparison of simple action-based and outcome-based policies for emergency-like situations. *Mathematical Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis shows that action-based punishment/reward policies are superior to outcome-based policies in generalized volunteer's dilemma settings (where more than one volunteer is needed and costs are private). Action-based policies can uniquely induce the efficient (second-best) outcome and prevent the no-participation equilibrium, while outcome-based policies always allow for a no-participation equilibrium, which is stable and potentially focal. The required level of punishment/reward under action-based policies is lower than under outcome-based policies. Combined policies that include punishment for non-participation can also rule out the no-participation equilibrium. The analysis is grounded in social cost minimization and group welfare, not just participation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that punishment (when implemented as action-based or combined with action-contingency) will have a more positive effect on efficiency (group welfare, social cost minimization) than outcome-based punishment in public-goods-like environments where more than one participant is needed. The results suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (action-based vs. outcome-based) is a critical moderator: action-based punishment can uniquely induce efficient outcomes and prevent social traps, while outcome-based punishment may fail to improve efficiency if the no-participation equilibrium is selected. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings with binary (all-or-nothing) participation, private costs, and no coordination. However, the results are theoretical and based on a generalized volunteer's dilemma, which is adjacent but not identical to standard PGGs.

- source: Mihm, M., & Toth, R. (2020). Cooperative networks with robust private monitoring. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms can sustain efficient cooperation in networked repeated games, but the effectiveness of punishment—and thus the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes—depends critically on the monitoring structure. With perfect or partial monitoring (players can observe at least their neighbors' neighbors), punishment can be immediate and efficient networks are possible. With local (private) monitoring (players only observe their own ties), punishment is delayed and only highly clustered networks (with triadic closure) can robustly support cooperation. The paper provides formal incentive constraints (PIC, LIC, RIC) that characterize when efficient or near-efficient networks can be sustained. Efficiency is maximized when monitoring allows for rapid punishment, and is reduced when monitoring is local and punishment is delayed or less credible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the monitoring structure (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' and 'show_punishment_id'), network clustering, and the immediacy/effectiveness of punishment affect the efficiency of public-goods-like games. If punishment is possible and monitoring is sufficiently rich (players can observe others' actions beyond their own ties), then enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency or at least sustain cooperation. If monitoring is strictly local, only highly clustered (triadically closed) networks can sustain cooperation, and efficiency gains from punishment are limited. The paper also shows that the cost and effectiveness of punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech) interact with network structure to determine whether efficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with bilateral ties, but the logic extends to public goods games with networked or local interaction structures.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Lenaerts, T., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Mediating artificial intelligence developments through negative and positive incentives. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment (sanctions) and rewards can increase group welfare and promote safety compliance in a repeated innovation race game, but their effects depend on the underlying risk and incentive parameters. Punishment is generally more effective at suppressing unsafe (defective) behavior and increasing group welfare in the 'dilemma' region where individual and collective interests diverge, but if punishment is too strong or too efficient, it can lead to over-regulation and reduce innovation even when risk is low and unsafe behavior would be collectively optimal. Rewards can also promote safety and welfare, especially when highly cost-efficient, and do not suffer from the risk of over-regulation, but are generally less effective than punishment. The model provides explicit conditions for when each incentive is beneficial or harmful for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like innovation races is highly context-dependent. If the risk of disaster from unsafe behavior is high, punishment increases efficiency by promoting safe behavior. If the risk is low, strong punishment can reduce efficiency by suppressing innovation. The cost-effectiveness of punishment (punishment cost and impact) is critical: punishment is most beneficial when its cost to the punisher is low relative to its impact on the punished. Reward mechanisms can also increase efficiency, especially when highly cost-effective, and are less likely to reduce efficiency through over-regulation. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on the risk/benefit structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline efficiency of the control game. The findings are most directly applicable to innovation race or winner-take-all settings, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2020). Five rules for friendly rivalry in direct reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strategies combining partner and rival features (friendly rivals) exist in the repeated prisoner's dilemma, with the CAPRI strategy as a prime example. CAPRI achieves mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency) when both players use it, and punishes defection to prevent exploitation. Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI dominates other strategies in terms of abundance and average payoff across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios, error rates, and population sizes. The paper also shows that memory length (m >= 3) is crucial for such strategies. While the main results are for the two-player PD, the authors note that similar strategies exist for the three-person public goods game, suggesting potential generalizability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment strategies designed to be both efficient and defensible (like CAPRI) can substantially increase group efficiency and average payoff in repeated prisoner's dilemma settings, and by extension, in closely related public goods games. The findings suggest that, when punishment is implemented with sufficient memory and recovery mechanisms, it can robustly improve efficiency regardless of population size, error rate, or benefit-to-cost ratio. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that enabling well-designed punishment in repeated games will increase efficiency, especially when players can remember and respond to past actions. However, the results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated PDs, with only indirect evidence for multi-player PG games.

- source: Lancia, F., & Russo, A. (2019). Sustaining Cooperation Through Strategic Self-Interested Actions. *B E Journal of Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that organizations can sustain higher efficiency and cooperation by conditioning punishments (and rewards) not only on collective output but also on observable outside activities, even if those activities are not directly productive. Strategic distortion of privately beneficial effort (either increasing or decreasing it, depending on whether it is a complement or substitute to collective effort) reduces the short-run gain from defection, thus lowering the required severity and frequency of punishment. This mechanism increases the expected output and intertemporal utility of members compared to settings where only output is monitored. The result is robust to extensions with more generations and noisy monitoring of outside activities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated public-goods-like environments with imperfect monitoring, the ability to condition punishment on additional observable actions (beyond just output) increases efficiency by making cooperation easier to sustain. If a game design allows punishment to be based on richer or more informative signals (e.g., monitoring of side activities), the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be higher than in designs where only output is observable. The model also predicts that the efficiency gain from punishment is larger when monitoring is more informative and when organizations can strategically distort observable but privately beneficial actions. However, the model is not an empirical test and does not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., Wang, X. J., Yang, J. B., Wu, K. B., & Dai, Z. L. (2019). Withhold-judgment and punishment promote cooperation in indirect reciprocity under incomplete information. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of punishment in indirect reciprocity models with incomplete information generally increases cooperation rates, but its effect on efficiency (average payoff) depends on the social norm and parameter settings. In some cases, especially when punishment is frequent and costly, the net effect on social welfare is negative, even as cooperation rises. Social norms that allow observers to withhold judgment (not update reputation) in ambiguous cases are more robust, especially under private reputation and noisy/incomplete information, and can maintain both high cooperation and high average payoff. The effect of punishment is thus context-dependent: it can either improve or harm efficiency depending on the interplay of punishment cost, observation probability, and the social norm in use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is not universally positive: while punishment reliably increases cooperation, it can reduce efficiency if the cost of punishment is high or if punishment is overused. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related (donation game with indirect reciprocity and group-level outcomes). The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one must consider not only the presence of punishment but also its cost, the effectiveness of punishment, the information structure (public vs private reputation), and the social norm governing reputation updates. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not always a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment), as punishment can sometimes lower group welfare despite increasing cooperation.

- source: Bhaskar, V., & Thomas, C. (2019). Community Enforcement of Trust with Bounded Memory. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain efficient cooperation (trust/lending) in large societies with random matching and one-sided moral hazard depends critically on the information structure available to punishers (lenders). With perfect bounded memory, punishment unravels and efficiency collapses. With coarse information (binary flag for recent default), temporary exclusion can sustain high efficiency, with the exact level depending on the profitability of lending and the exclusion length. The model provides explicit formulas for borrower and lender payoffs under different regimes. When lending is very profitable, some inefficiency is unavoidable due to the need for randomization and endogenous adverse selection. The results generalize to other sequential-move games with one-sided moral hazard.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining efficiency depends on the information structure: coarse information (pooling recent defaulters) enables efficient punishment and high payoffs, while fine-grained information leads to unraveling and zero efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will only improve efficiency if the punishment mechanism is supported by an information structure that prevents cherry-picking of near-rehabilitated defectors. The model is not a PGG or direct PGG variant, but the logic applies to trust games and other one-sided moral hazard settings. The findings are most relevant for settings with random matching, temporary exclusion, and information constraints.

- source: Baker, S., & Choi, A. H. (2018). Reputation and Litigation: Why Costly Legal Sanctions Can Work Better than Reputational Sanctions. *Journal of Legal Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that legal sanctions (punishment) can be more efficient than reputational sanctions (boycotts) in deterring undesirable behavior, even when both are costly. This is because legal sanctions have an inframarginal effect: increasing damages not only deters more lawsuits at the margin but also increases the penalty for existing lawsuits, improving deterrence at lower cost. Reputational sanctions lack this inframarginal effect, so increasing their severity is less efficient. The efficiency advantage of legal sanctions is greatest when courts are accurate and when litigation outcomes are public. When court error is high or when consumers have independent, reliable information about quality, reputational sanctions become more important.
