The model predicts that the optimal regime combines both types of sanctions, but leans toward legal sanctions unless litigation is highly error-prone or costly. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide experimental or empirical estimates, but it offers clear comparative statics and policy implications for the efficiency of punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (legal sanctions) in repeated moral hazard environments can increase efficiency relative to relying solely on reputational sanctions, especially when punishment is not too costly or error-prone and when outcomes are public. The inframarginal effect of punishment means that its efficiency benefit can be greater than that of reputational sanctions, which only deter at the margin. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment should increase efficiency, particularly when the punishment mechanism is accurate and visible. However, if punishment is highly error-prone or costly, or if reputational information is highly reliable, the efficiency gain from punishment may be reduced or even reversed. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution, but the qualitative comparative statics are clear and relevant.

- source: Honjo, K., & Kubo, T. (2020). Social Dilemmas in Nature-Based Tourism Depend on Social Value Orientations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of outcomes in a two-player common-pool resource game depends critically on the social value orientations (SVOs) of the players. When both players are competitive, the Nash equilibrium is often Pareto-inefficient (i.e., group efficiency is low), and this inefficiency is robust to the introduction of fair punishment. In fact, punishment can increase the parameter space in which inefficiency occurs for competitive players. When at least one player is individualistic or prosocial, the Nash equilibrium is almost always Pareto efficient (i.e., high group efficiency). The model demonstrates that punishment does not always improve efficiency and can backfire in certain social contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the social preferences of the players. In environments where players are competitive (i.e., care about outperforming others), introducing fair punishment can worsen efficiency, increasing the likelihood of Pareto-inefficient outcomes. In contrast, with individualistic or prosocial players, efficiency is generally high even without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee improved efficiency and may even reduce it in competitive settings, especially in small, binary-choice, one-shot games. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, single-round, all-or-nothing, common-pool resource dilemmas, but the underlying logic may transfer to similar public goods environments.

- source: Jindani, S. (2020). Community enforcement using modal actions. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated random matching games where players are matched with multiple partners and can observe modal actions, any efficient (strictly individually rational) payoff can be supported as a strict, sequential equilibrium. This includes full cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma and similar games. The mechanism relies on community punishment: players who deviate (i.e., are non-modal) are sanctioned by others, and those who fail to sanction are also sanctioned. The equilibrium is robust to trembles and the presence of noisy players, and the efficiency loss due to noise is linear in the noise rate. However, the mechanism is not robust to observation errors unless these are rare relative to other noise. The informational burden is low: in the prisoner's dilemma, only one period of memory is needed to sustain cooperation. The results generalize to a wide class of games and show that community enforcement with punishment can achieve high efficiency under realistic informational constraints.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated random matching games with multiple partners per period and observable modal actions, enabling community punishment can raise efficiency to the full cooperative level, provided players are sufficiently patient and the population is large. The results are robust to some forms of noise, and the informational requirements are minimal. For prediction tasks, the presence of multiple matching, observable modal actions, and community punishment mechanisms should be expected to enable high efficiency, even in large groups. However, if observation errors are frequent or if players cannot observe modal actions, efficiency may not be sustained. The findings are most directly applicable to settings structurally similar to the model (random matching, multiple partners, observable actions, and community punishment), and less so to standard public goods games with fixed groups and no community enforcement.

- source: Quan, J., Pu, Z. J., & Wang, X. J. (2021). Comparison of social exclusion and punishment in promoting cooperation: Who should play the leading role?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both keeping and reversing social norms can promote cooperation and increase social welfare (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games with punishment. The effect is moderated by the benefit-cost ratio: keeping rules are more effective at low benefit-cost ratios, while reversing rules are more effective at high benefit-cost ratios. Public reputation scenarios consistently support higher cooperation and welfare than private reputation scenarios. Punishment actions can help sustain cooperation, but their cost can reduce average welfare. The specific social norm in use (among 16 studied) determines whether the population reaches a cooperative or non-cooperative stable state, especially in private reputation settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of social norms and the structure of reputation (public vs. private) are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games. The results suggest that, in environments similar to pairwise donation games with indirect reciprocity, enabling punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but the net effect on efficiency depends on the cost of punishment and the social norm in use. Public reputation and appropriate social norms (keeping or reversing, depending on benefit-cost ratio) are important for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to pairwise donation games, not standard public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for prediction.

- source: Berger, U., & De Silva, H. (2021). Evolution of deterrence with costly reputation information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that in a repeated two-player deterrence game (with roles of challenger and defender), the possibility of costly punishment (retaliation) can lead to higher defender payoffs and social welfare, but only when reputation information is minimal (e.g., only the last action is observable and costly to obtain). When reputation information is rich (e.g., challengers can observe a defender's empirical fighting frequency over many rounds), deterrence breaks down, punishment loses its deterrent effect, and defender payoffs collapse. Agent-based simulations confirm that defender payoffs decline as the amount of reputation information increases, with a sharp drop when information becomes too rich. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: positive under minimal information, negative or null under rich information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the information structure: punishment increases efficiency (defender payoffs) when reputation information is minimal and costly, but not when information is rich and easily available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension 'information about others' past actions' (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' or 'show_punishment_id') is a key moderator: enabling rich, low-cost information can undermine the efficiency benefits of punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player deterrence games, but the mechanisms may generalize to PGG variants where punishment and reputation interact.

- source: Yan, F., Chen, X. J., Qiu, Z. P., & Szolnoki, A. (2021). Cooperator driven oscillation in a time-delayed feedback-evolving game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment (probabilistic detection and fine for defectors) can stabilize cooperation and maintain the common resource at sustainable levels, but only if the product of detection probability and fine (pβ) is sufficiently large relative to the cost of cooperation or the reinvestment rate. When punishment is strong, the system converges to a stable, efficient state with high cooperation and resource sustainability. When punishment is weak, defectors dominate and the resource is depleted. If cooperator reinvestment is proportional to endowment and there is a time delay in environmental feedback, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations in cooperation and resource levels, especially when punishment is moderate and resource growth is not too fast or too slow. The critical threshold for punishment effectiveness and the impact of time delay are analytically characterized. The model provides explicit conditions under which punishment improves or fails to improve group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how institutional punishment (with known cost and effectiveness) affects group efficiency in a common-pool resource game with feedback dynamics. The results are most relevant for adjacent PGG-like environments with renewable resources and environmental feedback. The model gives explicit formulas for the stationary group payoff/resource level as a function of punishment parameters, player count, resource growth, and reinvestment structure. For prediction, the key insight is that increasing punishment effectiveness (higher pβ) generally increases efficiency up to a threshold, above which full cooperation and sustainability are achieved. However, if punishment is too weak, efficiency remains low. Time delay in feedback can destabilize the system and induce oscillations, but does not always reduce average efficiency. The findings are most transferable to settings with institutional punishment, renewable resources, and feedback between group action and group payoff.

- source: Perry, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2020). Foresight in a Game of Leadership. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a two-player leadership/inspection game, standard rational or learning protocols do not support the emergence of punishment or cooperation, leading to inefficient outcomes (no production, no inspection). However, if leaders use foresight (weighting future payoffs and anticipating subordinates' best responses), then punishment and production can become stable equilibria, resulting in higher payoffs for both leader and group (i.e., higher efficiency). Selective imitation can also support this outcome if subordinates use best response. The effect depends on the weight placed on future payoffs (the foresight parameter) and the cost-benefit structure of punishment and production.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated or multi-round games where leaders (punishers) value future payoffs and anticipate others' responses, punishment can increase group efficiency by enabling cooperation/production that would not occur otherwise. The effect is sensitive to the cost of punishment, the benefit of production, and the degree of foresight. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in games with forward-looking or strategic agents (or mechanisms that mimic foresight/selective imitation) can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of punishment is not too high relative to the benefit. However, in the absence of such mechanisms, punishment may not emerge and efficiency remains low.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). Two ways to overcome the three social dilemmas of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with private reputation assessment, punishment can increase group efficiency and maintain stable cooperation if assessment norms are designed to (1) detect free riders, (2) justify punishment of bad actors, and (3) generously assess cooperation toward badly reputed recipients. Two main solutions are identified: one based on recipient-focused justified punishment (with generosity toward bad recipients), and another based on doer-focused justified punishment (with high cooperation efficiency required). The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c), with more generous norms required at lower efficiency. The presence of punishment, when justified and properly assessed, leads to higher average payoffs and stable cooperative regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the assessment norm and the efficiency of cooperation (b/c). If the punishment system is designed so that justified punishment is recognized and not misinterpreted (i.e., the intention is correctly communicated), and if generosity is applied in assessing cooperation toward bad recipients, then enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The benefit-to-cost ratio (mpcr analog) is a key moderator: at higher efficiency, more norms can sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence and design of punishment, the assessment norm, and the efficiency parameter are crucial for forecasting the efficiency gain from punishment.

- source: Gámez, M., López, I., Rodríguez, C., Varga, Z., & Garay, J. (2018). Game-theoretical model for marketing cooperative in fisheries. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that effective punishment (penalty parameter β > 1) stabilizes full cooperation in a marketing cooperative, leading to the group achieving the maximum possible profit (i.e., efficiency). When punishment is limited (β = 1), cooperation is unstable and members tend to defect, reducing group profit. The model is dynamic, with payoffs and strategies evolving over time, but the key result is that sufficiently strong punishment ensures convergence to the efficient outcome. The findings are demonstrated both analytically and through simulation examples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling sufficiently strong punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, a marketing cooperative) will increase efficiency, moving the group toward the maximum possible collective payoff. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar: members choose how much to contribute to the group (by selling through the cooperative), and the group can punish defectors. The explicit analysis of payoff functions and the demonstration that efficiency is achieved only when punishment is strong enough (β > 1) are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where the punishment technology is effective and the cost/impact ratio is favorable. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Nakamaru, M., Shimura, H., Kitakaji, Y., & Ohnuma, S. (2018). The effect of sanctions on the evolution of cooperation in linear division of labor. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing sanctions—especially the producer responsibility system—promotes the evolution of cooperation and increases group-level efficiency in a linear division of labor game. The actor responsibility system only promotes cooperation if monitoring and detection are sufficiently effective, which is often not the case in real-world settings. The producer responsibility system, which sanctions the generator if the manifest is not returned, is robust to low monitoring probabilities and is more effective at promoting cooperation and reducing illegal dumping. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable and shows that the three-role model captures more realistic dynamics than simpler two-role models. The findings are supported by both analytical results and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a well-designed punishment system (especially one that does not rely on costly monitoring, like the producer responsibility system) will increase group efficiency in public-goods-like environments with linear division of labor. The model is adjacent to standard PGGs but is directly relevant for settings with sequential roles and externalities. The results suggest that, for games with similar structure, enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and low monitoring requirements) should be predicted to increase efficiency relative to control. The paper also highlights the importance of the effectiveness of monitoring and the structure of the sanction system as moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical estimates of effect sizes.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2018). Evolution of global cooperation and ethnocentrism in group-structured populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops an analytical model of a group-structured population where individuals can adopt one of four strategies (global cooperator, ethnocentrist, traitor, egoist) and interact via a simplified prisoner's dilemma. Without any promoting mechanism, global cooperation is never favored by selection, regardless of migration pattern or group number; ethnocentrism is promoted, especially by global migration. When leader punishment is introduced (leaders punish defectors), global cooperation can be favored and can become more abundant than ethnocentrism if the punishment fine is sufficiently high. Efficiency-oriented leader punishment is more effective than equality-oriented punishment. The number of groups, migration rate, and mutation rate all moderate these effects. The main outcomes are derived as conditions for strategy abundance, which are directly linked to group payoff and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in group-structured prisoner's dilemma-like environments, punishment mechanisms (especially leader/third-party punishment with sufficient fines) can transform a setting where global cooperation is not favored into one where it is. The effect is more pronounced for efficiency-oriented punishment. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the payoff-based selection conditions are directly interpretable as efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment, the cost/fine of punishment, and the structure of the population (number of groups, migration/mutation rates) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. In the absence of punishment, efficiency is low (global cooperation not favored); with strong leader punishment, efficiency can be high (global cooperation favored).

- source: Brandt, U. S., & Svendsen, G. T. (2019). How robust is the welfare state when facing open borders? An evolutionary game-theoretic model. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly punishment (by Willing Punishers) is essential for maintaining high average welfare in a society vulnerable to free riding. If the proportion of punishers falls below a threshold, defectors proliferate and average welfare collapses. Timely recognition of the threat and sufficient support (redistributive rewards) for punishers can reverse this trend and restore high welfare. The model identifies threshold effects: there is a minimum level of support for punishment required to sustain cooperation and high efficiency. Delays in shifting support to punishers or insufficient rewards for them lead to welfare loss or collapse. The model also shows cyclical dynamics, with periods of high and low welfare depending on the political narrative and group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The model suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only if punishment is sufficiently incentivized and timely. The effect is not automatic: if support for punishers is too low or delayed, efficiency gains are not realized and may even be reversed. The findings support the prediction that punishment will increase efficiency when the game design ensures adequate support for punishers and rapid response to defection. The model also highlights the importance of migration/inflow of defectors and the political/institutional context (ingroup favouritism) as moderators. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct mapping to specific experimental game parameters.

- source: Barron, D., & Guo, Y. N. (2021). THE USE AND MISUSE OF COORDINATED PUNISHMENTS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in relational/trust games with coordinated punishment, the possibility of extortion (i.e., agents threatening to trigger punishment unless paid, regardless of their effort) can completely undermine cooperation and efficiency. In the baseline model with extortion, equilibrium effort and surplus are zero. However, if institutions can tie the ability to trigger punishment (leverage) to actual effort—such as through public signals of effort or strong dyadic relationships—then positive effort and efficiency can be restored, though often at the cost of rents to agents or the need for strong relationships. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore not universally positive: it depends critically on whether misuse/extortion is possible and whether institutional features can prevent it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games is highly sensitive to the possibility of extortion and the institutional context. If punishment can be misused (e.g., via action-contingent threats), enabling punishment may reduce efficiency to zero. However, if the game design includes mechanisms that tie punishment power to actual cooperative behavior (e.g., public effort signals, strong dyadic relationships), punishment can support high efficiency. For prediction, this means that the presence or absence of extortion opportunities, and the presence of institutional safeguards, are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. Game design dimensions that affect the ability to make credible threats, the observability of effort, and the structure of relationships are especially important.

- source: Podobnik, B., Gabor, A. M., & Kirbis, I. S. (2019). Scale-free growth of human society based on cooperation and altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that the system's growth rate (interpreted as efficiency or collective payoff) increases monotonically with the level of cooperation, which itself increases with the level of punishment. There is a threshold: if punishment is too high, the system can collapse (negative growth), but within reasonable bounds, higher punishment leads to more cooperation and higher efficiency. Empirical data suggest that less corrupt (more cooperative) countries tend to have higher fines, supporting the model's prediction that higher punishment is associated with higher cooperation. Survey data indicate the perceived fine needed to induce compliance, but do not directly measure payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling or increasing punishment increases efficiency (system growth rate), up to a point. The model is not a direct PGG but is closely related, and the main mechanism—punishment increasing cooperation, which increases collective payoff—transfers to PGG settings. The paper does not provide empirical estimates of effect size, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment increases efficiency unless set excessively high. The model also highlights that the effect of punishment depends on the baseline level of cooperation and the distribution of punishment sensitivity in the population.

- source: dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Explaining the evolution of parochial punishment in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment can promote cooperation and increase group average payoff (efficiency) when it is targeted at cheats and used to enforce cooperation. However, the evolution of parochial punishment (harsher punishment of out-group cheats) depends on the ecological scenario. Parochial punishment evolves (and can support high between-group cooperation and group payoff) when groups interact collaboratively and conflict occurs with random groups, or when cooperation is difficult to enforce. In contrast, when conflict occurs with the same group as prior interactions, punishment becomes spiteful (targeting all out-groups, including cooperators), which undermines between-group cooperation and does not improve efficiency. The model shows that both collaborative intergroup relations and the structure of conflict are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent: it can be positive (by promoting cooperation) or negative (by reducing group payoff through excessive or misdirected punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games, especially in settings with group structure, third-party punishment, and group-level selection. It shows that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether punishment is targeted at cheats (promoting cooperation and group payoff) or is spiteful (reducing group payoff by punishing all out-groups). The presence and structure of intergroup conflict, the difficulty of enforcing cooperation, and the ability to form collaborative intergroup relationships are key moderators. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when it is used to enforce cooperation (especially when cooperation is hard to sustain), but can reduce efficiency if it is misdirected or used spitefully. The findings are most relevant for games with group structure, repeated interactions, and third-party punishment, but less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without these features.

- source: Wei, X., Xu, P., Du, S. T., Yan, G. H., & Pei, H. Y. (2021). Reputational preference-based payoff punishment promotes cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputational preference-based payoff punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas (PDG and SDG) significantly increases the fraction of cooperators. At low punishment intensity, both cooperators and defectors may have low or negative average payoffs due to the cost of punishment, but as punishment intensity increases, cooperators' average payoff and fitness rise, and defectors' payoffs drop to zero. At high punishment intensity, the system can reach full cooperation with high average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is more pronounced in the snowdrift game, where coexistence of cooperators and defectors persists over a wider parameter range. The study does not directly report group-level efficiency, but the increase in average payoffs for cooperators at high punishment intensity suggests a positive effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that introducing a costly, reputation-based punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas increases cooperation rates and, at sufficient punishment intensity, increases average payoffs for cooperators, leading to higher group efficiency. The effect is robust across both prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games, with stronger effects in the latter. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and shared cost) will increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, particularly in spatially structured games. However, the results are based on simulation, not empirical data, and do not use the public goods game directly, so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious but is likely informative.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Liang, J. L. (2020). Cooperation under institutional incentives with perfect and imperfect observation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Institutional punishment (and reward), funded by a tax on positive payoffs, can promote the evolutionary emergence of cooperation in a well-mixed prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of punishment increases with higher tax rates, greater benefit of cooperation, and stronger incentive effectiveness. There is a threshold tax rate above which cooperation is favored by natural selection. Imperfect monitoring (i.e., noise in identifying defectors) reduces the effectiveness of punishment and increases the required tax rate for cooperation to emerge. The model provides analytical results for average payoffs and fixation probabilities, but does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that institutional punishment (with costs funded by a tax on payoffs) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperation, especially when monitoring is perfect, the tax rate is high, and incentives are strong. While it does not report group efficiency directly, the increase in fixation probability of cooperation and average payoffs for cooperators suggest that enabling punishment in similar game designs would increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, particularly under high tax rates and effective monitoring. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a prisoner's dilemma structure, so predictions should be transferred with caution. The findings are most informative for settings with institutional, payoff-funded punishment and well-mixed populations.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Guo, J. L. (2019). A synergy of punishment and extortion in cooperation dilemmas driven by the leader. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a networked donation game, a single high-degree leader node using a combined punishment and extortion strategy can induce full cooperation in the population with probability close to 1, regardless of initial conditions or the presence of other strategies. This combined strategy yields the highest group and leader payoffs, and is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the set of available strategies. Pure punishment or pure extortion by the leader is less effective. The results suggest a strong synergy between punishment and extortion in promoting cooperation and maximizing group welfare in networked social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in networked prisoner's dilemma/donation games, the presence of a punishment mechanism—especially when combined with extortion and wielded by a central leader—can dramatically increase group payoff and the probability of full cooperation. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with network structure and leader influence. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially in combination with other mechanisms) can shift efficiency from low to near-maximal, even when baseline cooperation is low. However, the effect depends on the presence of influential nodes and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-networked settings.

- source: Takesue, H. (2018). Evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on the network with punishment and opportunistic partner switching. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both punishment and partner switching (opportunistic rewiring of social ties) can support the evolution of cooperation in a networked prisoner's dilemma. When partner switching is infrequent, strong punishment is necessary to suppress defectors. When partner switching is frequent, cooperation dominates even with weak or no punishment. The mechanism is that defectors sever ties with punishers and preferentially connect to cooperators, which increases cooperators' opportunities to be imitated and thus proliferate. Average payoffs by strategy are reported, showing that cooperators and punishers can outperform defectors, but the main outcome is the frequency of each strategy. The results suggest that social fluidity (partner switching) can substitute for punishment in maintaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report group efficiency or total welfare directly, it shows that both punishment and partner switching can increase the prevalence of cooperation and reduce defection, and that average payoffs for cooperators and punishers can exceed those of defectors. The findings suggest that in networked games with opportunities for partner switching, the marginal benefit of adding punishment to increase efficiency may be reduced if partner switching is already frequent. The results also highlight the importance of the punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the fluidity of social relationships (analogous to partner switching) as moderators of punishment's effect. However, because the main outcomes are strategy frequencies and not group efficiency, the evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators rather than for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Nakamura, M. (2019). Rare Third-Party Punishment Promotes Cooperation in Risk-Averse Social Learning Dynamics. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that risk-averse individuals in evolutionary social dilemma games can achieve stable, high levels of cooperation with only rare third-party punishment. Both exogenous (authoritative) and endogenous (peer) punishment models are analyzed. Theoretical results show that the required frequency of punishment to maintain cooperation is much lower for risk-averse individuals than for risk-neutral or risk-prone individuals. Among punishment variants, punishing all defectors or punishing one defector (as a warning) are both effective in promoting cooperation, while punishing only one-sided defectors is less effective. The models predict that, under risk aversion, group payoff and cooperation rates are higher with punishment than without, and that the cost of maintaining cooperation via punishment can be minimized.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in two-player social dilemma games with third-party punishment, enabling punishment (even rarely) can substantially increase cooperation and group payoff, especially when individuals are risk averse. The findings suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is robust to low punishment frequency and that the structure of punishment (punish all vs. punish one) can moderate the effect. However, the results are derived from theoretical models, not empirical data, and the games are not public goods games but two-player dilemmas. The paper is most informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in environments where risk aversion is salient and punishment is rare.

- source: Matsuzawa, R., & Tanimoto, J. (2018). Sanctions triggered by jealousy help promote the cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that jealousy-motivated sanctions (where agents punish neighbors with higher payoffs, regardless of strategy) can promote cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect depends on the network structure: in lattice networks, higher sanction efficiency (penalty per cost) promotes cooperation, while in scale-free networks, lower efficiency (higher cost per penalty) can be more effective. However, the paper notes that even when cooperation is achieved, the continued use of jealous sanctions (due to persistent payoff inequalities) can reduce total social payoff, potentially lowering efficiency. The main reported outcome is the cooperation fraction, not direct group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to the cost of sanctions, especially when punishment is motivated by jealousy and can be directed at cooperators as well as defectors. The effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to depend on network structure and the cost-effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment may increase cooperation but could reduce efficiency if sanctioning is costly and not well-targeted.

- source: Ågren, J. A., Davies, N. G., & Foster, K. R. (2019). Enforcement is central to the evolution of cooperation. *Nature Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enforcement mechanisms—defined as actions that reduce selfishness within cooperative alliances—are predicted by evolutionary models to evolve across a wide range of biological systems, from genomes to human societies. The models show that, in the absence of enforcement, selfishness undermines cooperation and reduces group fitness. When enforcement is possible, it evolves under many parameter regimes and leads to higher cooperation and improved collective outcomes. Empirical examples from animal societies, mutualisms, and cellular systems support the theoretical predictions. The effectiveness of enforcement depends on its cost and the structure of the system, but its presence is generally associated with higher group efficiency or fitness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment or enforcement in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency or collective payoff, relative to control conditions without enforcement. The models are not direct PGGs but are structurally analogous, and the mechanisms (punishment, policing, sanctions) map closely to punishment in PGGs. The effect is generally more positive, but the magnitude depends on the cost of enforcement and system details. The paper also highlights that enforcement is most critical when relatedness is low and that high enforcement costs can limit its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment will increase efficiency, especially when enforcement is not prohibitively costly.

- source: Schlaepfer, A. (2018). The emergence and selection of reputation systems that drive cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of initially non-cooperative individuals, a reputation system based on punishment is likely to emerge first and drive the initial evolution of cooperation. However, once cooperation is established, it is mainly sustained by a reputation system based on cooperative actions (social image). The model shows that cooperation rates are highest when both punishment-based and cooperation-based reputation systems are available. The emergence and stability of these systems depend on the costs of information acquisition (gossip) and the relevance of active gossip channels. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but focuses on cooperation rates and the use of reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-based reputation systems can help establish cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when information about others' behavior is costly to acquire. However, it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game (it uses a dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage). The findings suggest that punishment may be most important for the initial emergence of cooperation, but less so for its maintenance. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the role of punishment-based reputation in overcoming initial defection.

- source: Ye, W. X., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Evolutionary traveler's dilemma game based on particle swarm optimization. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that using a PSO-based learning mechanism in the traveler's dilemma game leads to higher levels of cooperation (higher average claims) compared to traditional Fermi dynamics, across different network types. The effect of the reward/punishment parameter (R) and the balance between self-cognition and social cognition (c1) are explored: social learning promotes cooperation when the reward/punishment is small (large strategy interval), while self-cognition becomes more important as the reward/punishment increases (small strategy interval). The main outcome is the normalized cooperation level, not efficiency or total group payoff. The results are robust across network types, but the study is simulation-based and does not report direct efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how learning mechanisms and the structure of reward/punishment in a traveler's dilemma game (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game) affect the prevalence of cooperative behavior (high claims). It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation depends on the size of the incentive and the learning dynamics. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes and studies a different game structure.

- source: Fang, X., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of trust in the N-player trust game with individual reward and punishment. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The introduction of individual reward and punishment in the N-player trust game can promote the stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, which is interpreted as a promotion of trust. The size of the attraction domain for this coexistence increases with both the overall incentive strength and the share of incentives devoted to reward (as opposed to punishment). Reward is found to be more effective than punishment in promoting trust. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that mechanisms increasing trust and trustworthiness can move the system toward more socially optimal states.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment (and especially reward) mechanisms can promote cooperation-like behavior in trust-based social dilemmas. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report quantitative efficiency outcomes for control vs. punishment-enabled conditions. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its findings may be cautiously extrapolated to similar environments, especially regarding the positive effect of incentive strength and the relative effectiveness of reward over punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that increasing the strength and effectiveness of incentives (especially reward) is likely to expand the basin of attraction for cooperative outcomes, but does not provide direct efficiency data.

  findings: The model shows that cooperation can emerge and be sustained in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games when agents have memory and low mutation rates, with punishment and reciprocity as key mechanisms. Increasing memory increases cooperation, while higher mutation rates reduce it. When agents can discriminate kin, cooperation is still sustained, but the underlying heuristics shift from reciprocity to kin-based discrimination. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperative actions and the distribution of strategies; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment strategy (punishers who pay a cost to fine defectors) in a spatial multigame environment (mix of PD and SD) increases the fraction of cooperation (cooperators and punishers), especially when the sucker's payoff is large or when more players play the Snowdrift game. Punishers form compact clusters that help protect against defectors. The average payoff is shown to recover as cooperation increases, but efficiency (as a ratio to full cooperation) is not directly analyzed. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not experiments or real human subjects.

The punishment mechanism is explicit and parameterized, but the results are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Hu, K. P., Tao, Y. W., Ma, Y. J., & Shi, L. (2021). Peer pressure induced punishment resolves social dilemma on interdependent networks. *Scientific Reports*.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a peer-pressure-like punishment mechanism—where players with inconsistent strategies across interdependent network layers have their payoffs reduced—can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment maximizes cooperation, while too little or too much punishment is less effective. The effect is robust to changes in network structure (lattice, small-world, random) and the degree of payoff interdependence between layers. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; the main focus is on cooperation rates and the spatial distribution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, especially when the punishment is moderate. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that moderate punishment is likely to be most effective for promoting cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. The design dimensions most relevant here are the punishment cost (modeled as a reduction in payoff for inconsistent pairs) and the degree of interdependence between players' payoffs. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

  findings: The introduction of conditional neutral punishment—where agents punish neighbors with the opposite strategy if their own payoff is below the neighborhood average—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the cost and effectiveness of punishment. Cooperative punishers form protective clusters that shield cooperators, while antisocial punishment (defectors punishing cooperators) is largely ineffective. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperation, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional neutral punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates typically—but not always—imply higher efficiency, especially in prisoner's dilemma-like settings. However, without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the magnitude and reliability of efficiency gains from punishment in this design cannot be directly inferred. The spatial structure, conditionality of punishment, and the absence of communication or reward mechanisms are important contextual features.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., & Fu, F. (2018). Strategy intervention for the evolution of fairness. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of fairness in the Ultimatum Game is promoted by the presence of spiteful strategies (which reject low or unequal offers, i.e., costly punishment) and inhibited by altruistic strategies (which accept any offer). The introduction of non-monotonic rejection (rejecting both low and high offers) further helps fairness overcome selfishness. In group-structured populations, larger population size increases the frequency of altruism and decreases the frequency of selfishness and spitefulness, but does not change the dominance of fairness. Migration inhibits the evolution of selfishness and fairness, but promotes altruism and, with sufficient migration, spitefulness. Mutation has non-monotonic effects, with intermediate mutation maximizing fairness and selfishness, and minimizing spitefulness. The main dependent variable is the frequency of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of costly punishment (rejection) in promoting fairness in strategic environments similar to the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it supports the idea that the availability of costly punishment can increase the prevalence of fairness-oriented behavior, especially when non-monotonic punishment is possible. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment mechanisms affect the evolutionary stability of fairness, not for predicting group efficiency outcomes.

When the sum of incentives and punishments exceeds the total additional cost (TAC) of cooperation, positive cooperation becomes stable. If incentives and punishments are too low, negative cooperation prevails. The model does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare, but focuses on the conditions for stable cooperation strategies.

The findings suggest that, in similar environments, increasing punishment and incentives above a threshold can shift the system toward more cooperation, which may imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly modeled or measured.

The strength of punishment, temptation to defect, and ecological benefits (from 'free space') all modulate which strategies persist. Moderate punishment can suppress defectors and promote cooperation, but high temptation to defect can still undermine cooperation. The focus is on the eco-evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total welfare.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes sociological theories of norms, focusing on why and how people enforce norms (including through punishment and reward), and the implications for social order and inequality. It distinguishes between consequentialist (cooperation-focused), relational (coordination-focused), and agentic (contextual/individual-focused) approaches. The review highlights that sanctions (punishment and reward) can increase norm compliance, but also that enforcement can reinforce inequalities and sometimes undermine collective welfare, depending on the structure of interests and relationships. The paper does not report new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or group payoff in experimental games.

It may inform the interpretation of why punishment can sometimes promote or undermine cooperation, but does not offer parameter-level guidance for the prediction task.

- source: Molho, C., & Wu, J. H. (2021). Direct punishment and indirect reputation-based tactics to intervene against offences. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that both direct punishment and indirect, reputation-based tactics (gossip, ostracism) are important for maintaining cooperation and enforcing norms. Direct punishment is more effective at recalibrating offenders' behavior but is costly and risky, while indirect tactics are less costly and reduce retaliation risk but are less effective at changing behavior. The choice of tactic depends on factors such as the self-relevance of the offense, relationship to the offender, power dynamics, and emotional responses (anger vs. disgust). The paper highlights that indirect tactics are more prevalent in real-world settings than direct punishment as operationalized in laboratory public goods games. It also notes that punishment can sometimes reduce efficiency, and that the ecological validity of lab findings is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers theoretical context suggesting that the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment depend on its cost, the risk of retaliation, and the availability of alternative (indirect) sanctioning mechanisms. It cautions that lab-based punishment games may overestimate the prevalence and impact of direct punishment compared to real-world settings, where indirect tactics are more common. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of communication, relationship context, and the type of punishment available may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, but it does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Cofnas, N. (2018). POWER IN CULTURAL EVOLUTION AND THE SPREAD OF PROSOCIAL NORMS. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper argues that mainstream cultural evolutionary models, which treat punishment as an individual-level, uncoordinated action, fail to capture the collective, power-based enforcement of prosocial norms observed in real-world societies, especially among hunter-gatherers. It contends that collective punishment and coordinated norm enforcement are central to the evolution of morality and prosociality, and that these processes are not well-modeled by standard public goods game frameworks. The paper reviews anthropological evidence for collective punishment and discusses how this shapes moral psychology, but does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes from experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for interpreting the role of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, emphasizing that real-world punishment is often collective and coordinated, not merely the sum of individual actions. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, rounds, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that models which ignore collective decision-making and power dynamics may misrepresent the real impact of punishment, but it does not provide data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Toelstede, B. (2020). The Absence of Policing and the Political Economy of Contribution and Defection. *Democracy & Security*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in large groups, the disappearance of institutional punishment leads to a delayed decline in public goods contribution and a rapid increase in defection, while the reappearance of punishment leads to a delayed recovery of contribution and a rapid decline in defection. The length of these delays depends on factors such as trust in the punisher, transparency, media behavior, and regime support. The paper highlights the asymmetry between small and large groups, and between peer and institutional punishment, in terms of the speed and nature of behavioral responses. It also discusses the role of blame assignment and political manipulation in shaping these dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers theoretical and qualitative insights into how the effect of punishment on contribution (and by extension, efficiency) may be moderated by group size, institutional structure, trust, and information transparency. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that in large, real-world groups, the positive effect of punishment on contribution (and thus efficiency) may be delayed or attenuated compared to small, transparent, lab-like settings. The paper is not suitable for direct quantitative prediction but may inform qualitative expectations about moderators.

- source: Phillips, T. (2018). The concepts of asymmetric and symmetric power can help resolve the puzzle of altruistic and cooperative behaviour. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness and function of punishment in promoting cooperation depend critically on the symmetry or asymmetry of power among individuals. In asymmetric power settings (e.g., dominance hierarchies), punishment is typically used by dominants to coerce subordinates, not to enforce reciprocity or group-beneficial cooperation. In symmetric power settings, punishment can be counteracted by retaliation, and cooperation may break down unless specific conditions are met (e.g., territoriality, exit options). The paper reviews evidence that punishment often reduces group payoffs in experimental public goods games, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is crucial for its effectiveness. However, it does not provide new quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments, especially regarding the role of power asymmetries. It suggests that punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency in settings with strong power asymmetries, as it is used for coercion rather than cooperation enforcement. In symmetric power settings, punishment may also fail to improve efficiency due to retaliation and the tragedy of the commons. The paper highlights the importance of considering power relations, cost-to-impact ratios, and the possibility of retaliation when predicting the efficiency effects of punishment. However, it does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes, so its value is mainly in informing the interpretation of empirical results and the design of experiments.

- source: Smith, D. (2020). Cultural group selection and human cooperation: a conceptual and empirical review. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the mechanisms often posited by CGS models to stabilize cooperation—such as conformist social learning and a norm psychology that motivates indiscriminate punishment—are not strongly supported by empirical evidence. Instead, individuals tend to use adaptive, context-sensitive strategies, and punishment is typically self-interested, aimed at increasing future cooperation or personal payoff rather than enforcing norms for their own sake. The review also notes that institutions facilitating cooperation often arise endogenously through rational design rather than through stochastic group-level processes. While punishment can stabilize cooperation, its effectiveness and the resulting group efficiency depend on the alignment of individual and group interests, the structure of institutions, and the presence of incentives. The review does not provide new quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants, but it synthesizes theoretical and empirical arguments relevant to the design and interpretation of such experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It suggests that the impact of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether punishment is self-interested (aligned with individual and group payoffs) or norm-based (potentially maladaptive). The review argues that, in practice, punishment is usually self-interested and embedded in institutional contexts that align individual incentives with group benefit, which tends to support higher efficiency. However, it cautions that models assuming indiscriminate or maladaptive punishment may not reflect real human behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that game designs with punishment mechanisms are more likely to increase efficiency when they include features that align individual and group interests (e.g., rewards for punishers, reputation systems, endogenous institution formation), and less likely to do so when punishment is costly, norm-based, and not incentivized. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers a framework for interpreting when and why punishment may or may not improve efficiency.

  findings: The paper reviews neuroscience evidence showing that costly punishment, cooperation, trust, and fairness are associated with activation in specific brain regions, such as the prefrontal cortex, dorsal striatum, anterior insula, and amygdala. It discusses how these neural mechanisms underlie pro-social behaviors observed in economic games, including the willingness to punish norm violators at a personal cost. However, the review does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, nor does it provide quantitative or qualitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in public goods or related games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it reviews behavioral and neural evidence on costly punishment and other pro-social behaviors, the outcomes discussed are primarily neural activation and behavioral propensities (e.g., willingness to punish, cooperate, or trust), not efficiency or group payoff. The review may be useful for understanding the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment, but it does not support quantitative or qualitative prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: Mulder, L. B. (2018). When sanctions convey moral norms. *European Journal of Law and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that sanctions can have an expressive function, conveying moral norms and motivating compliance for moral reasons rather than purely instrumental ones. It reviews evidence that (1) retributive sanctions are more likely to induce moral norms than compensatory ones, (2) punishments are more effective than rewards at conveying obligatory moral rules, and (3) severe (but not unreasonably severe) sanctions are more likely to induce moral disapproval and compliance, especially when implemented by trustworthy and fair authorities. However, the paper also notes that sanctions can backfire, especially among those already engaging in the undesired behavior, and that excessive severity or unfairness can undermine moral norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and psychological context for how punishment may affect compliance and norm internalization in public-goods-like environments, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It suggests that the design and framing of punishment (retributive vs. compensatory, punishment vs. reward, severity, authority trustworthiness, and fairness) can moderate the effectiveness of sanctions in changing behavior. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff data. Its main value is in highlighting psychological mechanisms and moderators that could influence the effectiveness of punishment in experimental designs.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence that altruistic punishment is a key mechanism for maintaining cooperation and is influenced by neural systems affected in PD, such as the dopaminergic reward system and the default mode network. It highlights that PD patients, especially those with impulse-control disorders and on dopaminergic medication, may show altered punishment behavior. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, and its main focus is on the mechanisms and behavioral expressions of punishment rather than on payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and mechanistic context for how punishment behavior may be altered in populations with neurodegenerative disorders like PD, especially in relation to impulsivity and dopamine. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. As such, it offers limited direct support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment from game design dimensions, but may be useful for understanding potential moderators (e.g., impulsivity, neurobiological state) in special populations.

- source: Zabyelina, Y. (2020). The Long Arm of Anti-corruption: Extraterritoriality and Anti-corruption Targeted Sanctions. *Global Policy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews two books: one theorizing extraterritorial anti-corruption enforcement as a collective action problem, and another analyzing the legal and human rights aspects of targeted anti-corruption sanctions. It highlights that extraterritorial enforcement and targeted sanctions can incentivize cooperation and deter corruption, but also notes the risks of politicization, lack of compensation for victims, and potential human rights concerns. The review does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related quantitative outcomes, and does not present experimental or empirical data on public goods games or similar environments.
