While it discusses the theoretical role of incentives and sanctions in overcoming collective action problems, it does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes in experimental or empirical settings. Its relevance is limited to high-level theoretical framing of punishment as a tool to address free-riding in public goods provision, but it does not inform quantitative prediction tasks based on game design dimensions.

- source: Hooper, P. L., Kaplan, H. S., & Jaeggi, A. V. (2021). Gains to cooperation drive the evolution of egalitarianism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that punishment of dominance (hawkishness) by withholding cooperation (defection in the PD game) can be evolutionarily stable and lead to egalitarian outcomes when the future benefits of cooperation outweigh the short-term gains from dominance. The efficiency of the group increases as leveller strategies proliferate, reducing the frequency of costly contests and increasing resource sharing. Simulations show that an ecological shift increasing the gains to cooperation leads to a transition from dominance/defection equilibria to egalitarian/cooperative equilibria with higher payoffs. Empirical analysis of forager societies finds that greater reliance on cooperative hunting predicts a higher probability of egalitarianism, consistent with the model's predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment (here, the ability to withhold cooperation from dominant individuals) increases group efficiency and resource equality, especially when the marginal benefit of cooperation is high and the probability of future interaction is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games but not an exact match; however, the logic and payoff structure are highly relevant. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that in repeated, high-mpcr, high-future-interaction environments, enabling punishment (even in the form of exclusion or shunning) will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the benefits of cooperation are high relative to the benefits of dominance or defection.

- source: Qian, X. H., Huang, M., Zhang, Q. Y., Yin, M. Q., & Wang, X. W. (2018). Mechanism design of incentive-based reverse auctions with loss-averse 3PLs under incomplete attributes. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that combining incentive-based contracts (cost-sharing) with contingent penalties (punishment for delivery failure) in reverse auctions increases the ex ante expected profit (efficiency) of the system and the principal, compared to standard reverse auctions without these mechanisms. The efficiency gains are moderated by the number of agents (more agents increase efficiency), the fraction of low-risk agents, and the degree of loss aversion (higher loss aversion reduces efficiency). The presence and magnitude of the penalty (punishment) is a key design variable, and its optimal value depends on delivery risk parameters. The model provides explicit formulas for optimal efficiency under different mechanism designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that introducing punishment (contingent penalty) in a competitive procurement game with cost uncertainty and risk can increase system efficiency, but the effect is moderated by agent risk aversion and the number of agents. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that, all else equal, adding a punishment mechanism to a reverse auction or similar environment will increase efficiency, especially when there are more agents and when agents are less loss-averse. However, the magnitude of the effect depends on the specific design of the punishment and incentive parameters. The findings are most directly applicable to procurement/auction settings but are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: Dubey, S. P., Kedar, G. D., & Ghate, S. H. (2021). A COMMUNICATION NETWORK ROUTING PROBLEM: MODELING AND OPTIMIZATION USING NON-COOPERATIVE GAME THEORY. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a non-cooperative network routing game with a punishment mechanism (increased cost for exceeding link capacity), the efficiency of outcomes (minimized total cost) depends on the structure of the cost function and the distribution of user demands. When the cost function is linear in the number of packets, all Nash equilibria are efficient (Pareto optimal). When the cost function is convex, only some Nash equilibria (about 50% of preferred focal Nash equilibria) are efficient, and this depends on the relative size of user demands. The punishment mechanism (cost increase) can deter greedy strategies and promote more efficient, balanced transmission strategies, but does not guarantee efficiency in all cases. The model is adjacent to public goods games in that it involves individual versus collective use of a shared resource, but differs in the details of the payoff and punishment structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment mechanisms implemented as increased costs for overuse can improve efficiency in resource-sharing games, but the effect depends on the cost function and the distribution of demands. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that punishment (costly overuse) can align Nash equilibria with efficient outcomes under certain conditions (e.g., linear cost), but not always (e.g., convex cost). The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games where the 'punishment' is a proportional cost rather than a direct deduction or fine. The results support the idea that the design of the punishment mechanism (cost structure) and the symmetry of player demands are important moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Rauwolf, P., & Bryson, J. J. (2018). Expectations of Fairness and Trust Co-Evolve in Environments of Partial Information. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of a one-shot trust game with partner choice and partial information, both trust (willingness to invest with unknown partners) and costly punishment (rejecting profitable but unfair offers) can co-evolve and are adaptive, maximizing average payoffs. The benefit of punishment for efficiency only arises when trust is also present, and only in environments where information about partners is partially but not fully available. When information is fully transparent or there is no partner choice, punishment does not increase payoffs. The effect is robust to changes in the number of partners, population size, investment multiplier, selection intensity, and mutation rates. The model predicts that as the value of investment increases, the advantage and prevalence of fairness norms and punishment also increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency benefit of punishment in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the presence of both partner choice and partial information. If a public-goods or trust game is designed with multiple potential partners and only partial information about others' behavior, enabling punishment (the ability to reject unfair offers) can increase group efficiency, but only if players are also willing to trust unknown partners. If either partner choice or trust is absent, punishment does not improve efficiency. The model also predicts that the efficiency benefit of punishment increases with the investment multiplier (analogous to MPCR in PGGs). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by information structure, partner choice, and the value of cooperation.

- source: He, J. J., Liu, J. S., Qiu, Z. P., Nanzayi, N. E., & Zhang, M. (2021). How Does the R&D Cooperation Path of Supply and Demand Network Enterprises Evolve?. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation in R&D networks depends on the balance of knowledge spillover, government reward, government penalty (punishment), cooperation cost, and cooperation risk. Increasing government penalty (punishment) and reward, and decreasing cost and risk, promote the stability of cooperation. The model shows that under certain parameter regimes, cooperation becomes evolutionarily stable, while in others, selfishness dominates. The effect of punishment (penalty) is to accelerate and stabilize cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing or increasing punishment (government penalty) in a social dilemma-like environment can promote cooperation, especially when combined with rewards and low costs/risks. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that, in similar networked cooperation settings, punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may translate to higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative direction of punishment effects and the importance of cost, risk, and reward as moderators.

- source: Geng, Y. N., Shen, C., Hu, K. P., & Shi, L. (2018). Impact of punishment on the evolution of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a punishment mechanism—where players with below-average payoffs are punished by a reduction in their fitness coefficient—substantially increases the stationary frequency of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model. Increasing the punishment rate and memory length both further promote cooperation, leading to full dominance of cooperation under many parameter settings. The mechanism's effect is attributed to increased heterogeneity in individual fitness, which supports network reciprocity. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, especially when punishment is more intense and lasts longer. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, and because the game is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG-like efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that, in similar spatial or networked environments, enabling punishment may increase cooperation, which could plausibly increase efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific punishment mechanism (punishing those with below-average payoffs) is also nonstandard compared to typical PGG punishment designs.

- source: Song, M., Ge, Y. H., & Liu, J. S. (2020). Knowledge Input or Not in the Dynamic Cooperation Behavior of Top Management Teams? From the Perspective of Knowledge Flow. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The model shows that increasing knowledge flow, government reward, and government penalty (punishment) promotes the selection of the cooperative (knowledge input) strategy by TMTs, while higher input costs hinder cooperation. Under some parameter regimes, mutual cooperation (input, input) is the stable outcome; in others, only one party inputs knowledge. The effect of government penalty (punishment) is to increase the likelihood of cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that the presence and magnitude of punishment (government penalty) can increase the likelihood of cooperative behavior in a two-player, repeated interaction setting with knowledge input as the cooperative act. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment in public goods games. The model's structure and findings may be useful for mechanism or moderator reasoning, especially regarding the effect of punishment cost and effectiveness, but the lack of payoff-based outcomes limits its direct predictive value for efficiency.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). A Review of Theoretical Studies on Indirect Reciprocity. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical advances in indirect reciprocity, showing that cooperation can be maintained in social dilemma settings when reputation mechanisms and justified punishment are present. It details how the refinement of assessment functions (e.g., leading eight norms) resolves issues like the scoring and punishment dilemmas, enabling stable cooperation under certain conditions. However, it notes that these models are not directly applicable to public goods games, as selective cooperation (the core of indirect reciprocity) is not possible in standard PGGs. The review also highlights the importance of the benefit-cost ratio, error rates, and the distinction between public and private reputation systems in determining the stability of cooperation. While punishment and reward mechanisms are discussed, the focus is on their role in norm stability and cooperation, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and reputation mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in social dilemmas, but it does not offer direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its models are adjacent to PGGs but differ in key structural ways (e.g., selective cooperation vs. public goods provision). The review is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and norm refinement can support cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on norm structure, reputation information, and error rates, but it does not allow for direct mapping from game design dimensions to efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Yang, Z. L., Shi, Y. Y., & Li, Y. C. (2018). Analysis of intellectual property cooperation behavior and its simulation under two types of scenarios using evolutionary game theory. *Computers & Industrial Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that under both market and administrative supervision mechanisms, certain policy levers (subsidies, liquidated damages, tax rates, penalties) can increase the probability and stability of cooperation among heterogeneous agents in intellectual property activities. However, the effect is non-monotonic: excessive penalties or taxes can reduce cooperation. The main findings are about the conditions under which the system converges to full cooperation, noncooperation, or mixed states, rather than about efficiency or total group payoff. The results are based on theoretical modeling and numerical simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for how punishment-like mechanisms (liquidated damages, administrative penalties) and incentives (subsidies, tax breaks) affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured, the results suggest that moderate punishment and incentives can stabilize cooperation, but excessive punishment or taxation can backfire. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the design and magnitude of punishment/reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Goetzmann, M. (2021). The Building Blocks of Social Trust: The Role of Customary Mechanisms and Property Relations for the Emergence of Social Trust in the Context of the Commons. *Philosophy of the Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that social trust in commons-like environments emerges from a complex system of property relations and mutual monitoring, where the ability to sanction is distributed among community members. This structure enables individuals to internalize the external effects of their actions, fostering cooperation and trust even in the absence of external authorities. The paper extends Ostrom's work by emphasizing the importance of property rights structures (patrimonial structures) and the legitimization of direct sanctions. However, it does not provide empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and mutual monitoring can support cooperation and trust in commons-like (public goods) environments, especially in the absence of external enforcement. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions affect the impact of punishment on efficiency. Its value for the prediction task is mainly in supporting the plausibility of mechanisms by which distributed sanctioning and property rights structures can facilitate cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Glover, J., & Kim, E. (2021). Optimal Team Composition: Diversity to Foster Implicit Team Incentives. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated team production, the efficiency of implicit team incentives (mutual monitoring and punishment for free-riding) depends critically on team composition and the expected duration of the relationship (discount factor). Diverse teams (with productive complementarity) always have a monotonic relationship between tenure and incentive efficiency: as tenure increases, the cost of providing incentives decreases, and collusion is not a concern. Specialized teams (with productive substitutability) can initially benefit from longer tenure (lower incentive costs), but as tenure increases further, collusion becomes a problem, raising the cost of incentives and reducing efficiency. The model provides explicit formulas and parameter thresholds for when each team type is optimal. The main efficiency outcome is the principal's expected payoff, which is maximized by choosing the team composition that best balances productive efficiency and incentive efficiency, given the risk of collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms (mutual monitoring, implicit threats) in repeated team production environments, especially as a function of team composition (diversity vs. specialization), the discount factor (tenure), and the production technology (complementarity vs. substitutability). While not a public goods game per se, the model is adjacent and highly relevant for settings where group payoff depends on repeated cooperation and the threat of future punishment. The results show that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether collusion is possible and on the structure of the team. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment (mutual monitoring) generally increases efficiency in diverse teams and in specialized teams with short tenure, but may reduce efficiency in specialized teams with long tenure due to collusion. The explicit formulas and parameter thresholds can inform quantitative predictions if the relevant parameters are known.

- source: Andersson, L. (2020). Cooperation between Emotional Players. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoners' dilemma games, the presence of emotional players—who value others' payoffs positively (friendly) or negatively (hostile) depending on the history of play—can alter the conditions under which cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) is sustainable. With Grim Trigger strategies, emotional states (both friendly and hostile) lower the required discount factor for cooperation, making efficient outcomes more attainable. With mutual minmax strategies, emotions can either facilitate or obstruct cooperation: friendly states increase the cost of deviating, but hostile states can make it harder to return to cooperation after punishment, sometimes requiring greater patience than in the standard model. The model also shows that a rational player can manipulate an emotional player into a friendly state to achieve higher payoffs. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with no empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how emotional dynamics—modeled as state-dependent preferences—can affect the efficiency of repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the repeated prisoners' dilemma is closely related. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, emotional transitions that make punishment more or less credible) on efficiency depends on the interplay between strategy, emotional responsiveness, and patience (discount factor). For prediction tasks, the paper implies that enabling mechanisms that make punishment more credible (e.g., emotional hostility after defection) can increase efficiency under some strategies, but may also backfire if they make return to cooperation difficult. The results are most directly relevant for settings where player preferences or states can change in response to observed actions, and less so for standard PGGs with fixed payoffs and explicit punishment stages.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2021). Cooperation evolves by the payoff-difference-based probabilistic reward. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic reward mechanism, where the probability of rewarding a cooperator increases with the payoff difference between the player and the cooperator, leads to higher average payoffs and more cooperation in most cases. The effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, though the strength and range of the effect depend on the network structure and the reward parameter t. The paper does not directly study punishment in this model, but situates its findings in the context of prior work on punishment and reward in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a well-designed reward mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially under certain network structures and parameter ranges. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare punishment and reward within the same experimental or simulation framework. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by analogy and through its discussion of prior punishment studies.

- source: Deb, J., & González-Díaz, J. (2019). Enforcing social norms: Trust-building and community enforcement. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that, in large enough populations and with sufficiently patient players, efficient (cooperative) outcomes can be sustained in equilibrium in repeated anonymous random matching games, even when players only observe their own transactions and have no information about others' actions or identities. The mechanism involves a trust-building phase followed by community enforcement (contagion-based punishment). The set of achievable equilibrium payoffs includes those arbitrarily close to the efficient frontier, as long as the stage game has a strict Nash equilibrium and a one-sided incentive profile. The paper provides detailed conditions and mathematical proofs for when and how this is possible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated games with random matching, minimal information, and community enforcement (contagion-based punishment), the introduction of a punishment mechanism (in the form of Nash reversion triggered by observed deviations) can enable the group to achieve efficiency levels arbitrarily close to full cooperation, provided the population is large enough and players are sufficiently patient. The results are not specific to the public goods game but apply to a broad class of two-player games with certain properties. The findings suggest that, in environments with similar structure (random matching, minimal information, repeated play, and the possibility of community-wide punishment), enabling punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, even when direct monitoring is not possible. However, the results are theoretical and depend on equilibrium reasoning, not empirical observation.

- source: Raub, W., Buskens, V., & Frey, V. (2019). Strategic tie formation for long-term exchange relations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when actors can invest in strategic tie formation (i.e., commit to repeated interaction at a cost), and when the continuation probability and payoff structure are favorable, they will rationally choose to form ties and cooperate, resulting in higher group efficiency (payoff) than in the absence of such ties, where defection prevails. The efficiency gain from tie formation is explicitly quantified as the difference in equilibrium payoffs between mutual cooperation and mutual defection, net of tie formation costs. The model also generalizes to other social dilemma games (e.g., Trust Game) and discusses the role of institutional design in facilitating or hindering efficient outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling mechanisms for repeated interaction and voluntary commitment (analogous to endogenous punishment or sanctioning institutions) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, compared to control conditions without such mechanisms. While the model is not a direct PGG or experimental study, its formal results on equilibrium payoffs, tie formation, and cooperation are highly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in repeated, two-player social dilemmas with commitment options. The explicit mapping of efficiency gains to game parameters (payoff structure, continuation probability, cost of commitment) can inform predictions about the impact of punishment-like or commitment-enabling design features in related experimental games.

- source: Kayser, K., & Lampert, A. (2021). Survivor's dilemma: The evolution of cooperation in volatile environments. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in the survivor's dilemma, cooperation can be evolutionarily stable if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and/or environmental volatility is low. In these cases, mutual cooperation leads to higher expected lifespan (efficiency) for all. However, if the benefit-to-cost ratio is low or volatility is high, defection dominates and efficiency is lower. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, indirect punishment occurs because defectors lose partners and thus reduce their own survival prospects. The findings are presented for both 2- and 3-player games, with detailed analysis of parameter regions where cooperation or defection is stable. The results are directly about efficiency (expected lifespan) and its dependence on game parameters, but do not address the effect of enabling or disabling explicit punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence about how efficiency (expected lifespan) in repeated social dilemmas depends on group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and environmental volatility. It does not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment, as no explicit punishment mechanism is present. However, it does show that indirect forms of 'punishment' (loss of cooperative partners) can sustain cooperation and efficiency under certain conditions. For prediction tasks involving explicit punishment in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation depend strongly on the benefit-to-cost ratio and the likelihood of future interaction (volatility), and that even without explicit punishment, cooperation can sometimes be stable. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment.

- source: Forges, F., & Horst, U. (2018). Sender-receiver games with cooperation. *Journal of Mathematical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in sender-receiver games with the possibility of credible punishment (a uniform punishment decision), there always exists an equilibrium (CTE) where both players cooperate and communicate, leading to expected payoffs for both that are at least as high as in the baseline game without communication, and often strictly higher. The existence of credible punishment is key: without it, such efficient equilibria may not exist. The paper provides examples where the introduction of a UPD enables partially revealing communication and joint decisions that strictly improve welfare for both players. The findings are theoretical and general, but the examples show that the efficiency gain from enabling credible punishment can be substantial.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling credible punishment (via a uniform punishment decision) in games with communication and cooperation can increase group efficiency/welfare relative to a no-punishment baseline. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the logic is adjacent: credible punishment for non-cooperation enables more efficient equilibria. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that, in environments where a credible punishment mechanism is available and can be used as a threat, enabling punishment should not decrease and may increase efficiency, sometimes strictly. The magnitude and details depend on the structure of the game and the nature of the punishment, but the direction is clear. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Iwamura, Y., Nagashima, K., & Tanimoto, J. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics of a 3-strategy game: Cooperator, defector and costly cooperative loner strategic types. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a costly cooperative loner (CL) as a third strategy in a prisoner's dilemma game does not prevent the collapse to all-defection, nor does it improve social average payoff (efficiency) compared to the standard two-strategy game. The CL strategy is always outcompeted and goes extinct, after which defectors dominate. This is in contrast to models with costly punishment, where punishment can sometimes sustain cooperation and higher efficiency. The paper provides a detailed analysis of the payoff matrix and evolutionary trajectories, showing that the addition of CL does not alter the efficiency outcome: the system always ends in the least efficient (all-defector) state.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a negative case: simply adding a third strategy that is not an active punisher (but pays a cost to avoid defectors) does not improve efficiency in a prisoner's dilemma environment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'costly loner' or similar non-punishing third strategy is not sufficient to increase efficiency. The results reinforce that only certain types of third strategies (e.g., active costly punishment or rewarding) can shift efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and may not generalize to finite or structured populations. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment per se, but its comparison to punishment models supports the view that punishment (with sufficient impact and cost structure) is necessary for efficiency gains in these settings.

- source: Auerswald, H., Schmidt, C., Thum, M., & Torsvik, G. (2018). Teams in a public goods experiment with punishment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The evolutionary IPD with choice and refusal of partners (IPD/CR) produces higher average fitness (group efficiency) than random partner matching, with most runs evolving to near full cooperation. The average fitness clusters around a few regions, with the highest corresponding to mutual cooperation. However, if the refusal payoff is high or players are highly intolerant of defection, populations can become trapped in low-efficiency wallflower states. Sensitivity analyses show that the refusal payoff, minimum tolerance level, and memory weight are key moderators: high refusal payoffs or high intolerance increase the risk of social isolation and lower efficiency. Increasing population size reduces the risk of wallflower ecologies. The findings are robust across different strategy representations and parameter sweeps.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling partner selection (choice and refusal) in repeated social dilemmas can increase group efficiency compared to random matching, especially when refusal payoffs and intolerance are not too high. For prediction tasks, the presence of a partner selection mechanism (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be expected to increase efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit structure of refusal does not incentivize social isolation. The results are most directly relevant to settings where agents can avoid defectors, but less so to standard public goods games with centralized punishment. The findings suggest that game design dimensions such as the cost of refusal, tolerance thresholds, and population size are critical moderators of the efficiency effect.

- source: Lee, S., Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2021). Local stability of cooperation in a continuous model of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity depends on the sensitivity of the assessment and behavioral rules (social norms). It derives explicit conditions (in terms of derivatives of the assessment and behavioral functions) under which a cooperative norm is stable against local mutants, and shows that the benefit-to-cost ratio required for stability increases if reputation is insensitive to observed behavior or if cooperation is insensitive to recipient reputation. The analysis is supported by numerical simulations, which confirm the theoretical predictions about payoff differences between resident and mutant norms. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard PGGs, but reputation loss functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the expected payoff (efficiency) of the group under different norms and parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence about how the structure of social norms (in terms of sensitivity to behavior and reputation) affects the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity. While not a direct PGG or explicit punishment game, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is enforced by reputation-based indirect sanctions rather than direct punishment. The findings suggest that the efficiency of cooperation depends on the responsiveness of the reputation system and the behavioral rule, and that certain parameter regimes (high benefit-to-cost ratio, high sensitivity) are required for stable, efficient cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where indirect reciprocity is the main enforcement mechanism, and less so to direct punishment or reward treatments in PGGs.

- source: Bear, A., & Rand, D. G. (2019). Can Strategic Ignorance Explain the Evolution of Love?. *Topics in Cognitive Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a partner-choice game where both agents can choose to search for better partners (look) or commit (not look), and can allow or disallow their partner to look, the only robust equilibrium is mutual looking and allowing. Commitment (strategic ignorance) does not evolve except when the cost of being abandoned is extremely high. The model directly analyzes the efficiency (expected payoff) of different equilibria and shows that, under most parameterizations, the efficiency of the 'punishment' (commitment-demanding) equilibrium is not higher and is not evolutionarily stable. The findings suggest that, in this class of games, enabling punishment-like mechanisms (demanding commitment) does not increase group efficiency and is not selected for, except in rare, extreme cases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical counterpoint to the idea that punishment or commitment mechanisms generally increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. In the context of partner-choice games with bilateral temptation and the ability to condition on partner behavior, the model predicts that enabling punishment (demanding commitment or non-looking) does not robustly increase efficiency and is not evolutionarily stable. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in games with similar structure (bilateral temptation, partner choice, visible actions), enabling punishment or commitment mechanisms is unlikely to increase efficiency unless there are very large costs to being abandoned. The paper is most informative for adjacent settings rather than standard public goods games, but its payoff-based analysis is directly relevant to efficiency predictions.

- source: Ille, S. (2021). The evolution of sectarianism. *Communications in Nonlinear Science and Numerical Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of coercive (punishment-like) strategies in a repeated bargaining game can lead to either efficient egalitarian outcomes or inefficient sectarian contracts, depending on the relative costs and benefits of coercion and the structure of social learning. When the cost of coercion is low and potential gains from inegalitarian contracts are high, societies are more likely to settle into inefficient, conflictual, or sectarian equilibria. When both groups have similar coercive capacities and the cost of coercion is high, egalitarian and efficient outcomes are more likely. The model also shows that collective memory, identity politics, and initial asymmetries can tip the balance toward persistent conflict or sectarianism, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (coercion) on efficiency is highly context-dependent. If punishment is symmetric and costly, it can support efficient cooperation (egalitarian contracts). If punishment is asymmetric or the gains from inegalitarian outcomes are high, punishment can entrench inefficient, conflictual, or exploitative equilibria. The model suggests that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the symmetry of player power, and the structure of social learning critically moderate the efficiency impact of punishment. However, the model is not a direct PGG or public goods game, but rather a repeated bargaining/coordination game with endogenous punishment-like coercion. Thus, its findings are most relevant for adjacent settings where punishment can be used to enforce either efficient or inefficient group outcomes.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhu, Y. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2020). Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems on Multiple Community Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Systems Man Cybernetics-systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the interaction network (community structure and interaction probabilities) and the payoff matrix jointly determine which strategies dominate and what equilibrium payoffs are achieved. In dominating games (like the prisoner's dilemma), the system converges to full defection or cooperation, mirroring well-mixed results. In coexistence games (like the snowdrift game), the system can reach an interior equilibrium with mixed strategies, provided certain conditions on the interaction matrix are met. In coordination games (like the stag-hunt), multiple stable equilibria are possible, and the final state depends on initial conditions and network structure. The paper provides sufficient conditions for the stability of equilibria and shows that higher payoff strategies tend to dominate. However, it does not analyze the effect of punishment, reward, or other institutional interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and payoff matrices affect equilibrium efficiency and welfare in multiagent evolutionary games. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it model public goods games directly. Its results are most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in structured populations without institutional interventions. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from punishment or reward mechanisms, but it does clarify how efficiency depends on game type and network structure in the absence of such mechanisms.

- source: Szabó, G., & Bunth, G. (2018). Social dilemmas in multistrategy evolutionary potential games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in symmetric potential games, efficiency (measured as average payoff or contentment) is maximized when the symmetric part of the payoff matrix dominates, aligning individual and collective interests. The introduction of antisymmetric (deluding) components can create social dilemmas, leading to inefficient Nash equilibria and lower average payoffs. The likelihood of inefficiency increases with the number of strategies. However, if the antisymmetric component supports the same equilibrium as the symmetric part, it can help maintain efficiency, especially under noise. The paper provides explicit mappings of parameter regions where efficiency is high or low, but does not study punishment or public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the structure of payoff matrices in multi-strategy evolutionary games affects efficiency, especially the role of antisymmetric (deluding) components in creating or resolving social dilemmas. While it does not study public goods games or punishment directly, it offers a framework for predicting when efficiency will be high or low based on the alignment of individual and collective incentives. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that efficiency is sensitive to the presence of payoff components that create conflicts between individual and group interests, and that the probability of inefficiency increases with the number of available strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or specific public goods game design dimensions.

- source: Han, T. A., & Tran-Thanh, L. (2018). Cost-effective external interference for promoting the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: This work provides analytical and numerical results showing that an external decision-maker can promote high levels of cooperation in a population of self-interested agents by using a cost-efficient, state-dependent rewarding scheme. Rather than always rewarding cooperators, it is more cost-effective to reward only when the number of cooperators is below a threshold. The optimal threshold depends on the per-individual reward cost, the intensity of selection, and the desired cooperation level. For a wide range of parameters, this targeted approach achieves the same or higher cooperation at a lower total cost than always-on institutional incentives. The model does not directly analyze group payoff or efficiency in the public goods sense, but the results are closely related to maximizing cooperation frequency while minimizing resource expenditure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for predicting the effect of reward-based interventions (not punishment) on cooperation and cost-efficiency in Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. It suggests that targeted, conditional incentives can be more cost-effective than unconditional ones, especially under weak selection. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not analyze punishment, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency with punishment are not possible. The findings support the idea that the design of incentive schemes (e.g., when and whom to reward) strongly moderates the cost-effectiveness of cooperation promotion, and that always-on incentives may be suboptimal. For downstream prediction, the results are most informative for settings with external, state-dependent rewards, and less so for punishment or standard PGGs.

- source: Fulker, Z., Forber, P., Smead, R., & Riedl, C. (2021). Spite is contagious in dynamic networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic networks where agents can choose interaction partners and update their strategies by imitating higher-payoff individuals, spiteful (costly harmful) behavior can reliably invade and fixate in the population. This occurs even when the overall network does not exhibit strong anti-correlation, due to local and transient anti-correlated interactions. The spread of spite is facilitated by reinforcement learning and imitation based on average payoff per interaction. When spiteful behavior dominates, average payoffs are lower than in populations of only 'Social' agents, indicating a reduction in group efficiency. The findings challenge classic conditions for the evolution of costly harm and suggest that dynamic partner choice is a powerful mechanism for the emergence of antisocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is primarily theoretical and does not directly study public goods games or experimental punishment interventions. However, it provides indirect evidence that dynamic partner choice and imitation based on payoffs can enable the spread of costly harmful behaviors, which reduce group efficiency. In environments where agents can choose partners and update strategies based on payoffs, the introduction of punishment-like costly harm may decrease efficiency, especially if such behaviors can spread by imitation. The findings suggest caution in predicting positive efficiency effects from punishment in dynamic or endogenous network settings, as costly harm can become self-reinforcing and reduce welfare. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not include a cooperative contribution stage, so direct quantitative prediction is limited, but the qualitative mechanism is relevant for understanding the risks of antisocial punishment or spite in flexible social environments.

- source: Wu, J. H., Számadó, S., Barclay, P., Beersma, B., Cruz, T. D. D., Lo Iacono, S., Nieper, A. S., Peters, K., Przepiorka, W., Tiokhin, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Honesty and dishonesty in gossip strategies: a fitness interdependence analysis. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the honesty of gossip is determined by the gossiper's fitness interdependence with the receiver and target, and by the marginal cost/benefit structure of the underlying game between receiver and target. A simple matching rule is proposed: gossipers are honest when their fitness interdependence aligns with the effect of honest gossip, and dishonest when it mismatches. The model is applied to four game types, including a punishment game, but the focus is on the strategic choice of gossip honesty rather than on direct efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests that honest gossip can support cooperation, but that dishonest gossip can undermine reputation-based cooperation, especially when fitness interdependence is negative or mixed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information transmission (gossip) can act as a low-cost reputational punishment mechanism in social dilemmas, including punishment games. However, it does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze the standard public goods game dimensions. Its main contribution is to clarify when gossip will be honest or dishonest, which can indirectly affect cooperation and efficiency, but the paper does not quantify these effects. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the importance of information accuracy and fitness interdependence in the effectiveness of reputational sanctions.

- source: Guo, H., Chu, C., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2018). Reputation-based coevolution of link weights promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based coevolution of link weights in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game promotes cooperation. When link weights are allowed to vary (parameterized by delta), higher heterogeneity in link weights leads to higher levels of cooperation. Punishment is implemented as a reduction in link weight to the lowest-reputation neighbor, and reward as an increase to the highest-reputation neighbor. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The effect of punishment and reward is to facilitate the formation of cooperative clusters, which resist invasion by defectors. The effect is stronger for larger delta (greater possible link weight variation).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemma settings. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game), and the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of network structure, reputation, and coevolving relationships in promoting cooperation, but do not provide quantitative guidance for efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, X. P., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2019). Reputation-based adaptive adjustment of link weight among individuals promotes the cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to adaptively increase or decrease based on reputation (interpreted as a form of social reward/punishment) leads to a substantial increase in the frequency of cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. The effect is stronger as the range of possible link weights (controlled by ε) increases, which increases heterogeneity and supports the formation of cooperative clusters. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that dynamic, reputation-based social reward and punishment mechanisms (via link weight adjustment) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a simulation of spatial PDG/SDG rather than a public goods game, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited and should be considered only as suggestive, not directly predictive.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2020). Self-regulation versus social influence for promoting cooperation on networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that strong self-regulation (relative to social influence from neighbors) can lead to full cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games, while weak self-regulation leads to defection. For intermediate self-regulation, complex behaviors such as partial cooperation, reciprocity, and anti-reciprocity emerge. Network topology matters: scale-free networks foster higher average cooperation than Erdös-Rényi or regular networks, especially when self-regulation is moderate. The model suggests that social diversity and heterogeneity in network structure can promote cooperation, even when the 'cost' of self-regulation is not high. However, the study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with self-regulation (an internal, not externally imposed, mechanism) and analyzes cooperation levels, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no manipulation of punishment, punishment cost, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how intrinsic motivation or network structure can affect cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Wang, Z. R., Deng, Z. H., Wang, H. B., & Qu, Y. (2021). Moderate irrational sentiment-driven fitness can promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that time-varying reputation mechanisms driven by environmental feedback can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma game. The effectiveness of different reputation update rules depends on the temptation to defect (b): some mechanisms are more effective under low social dilemma intensity, while others are better under high intensity. Increasing the maximum possible reputation reward/punishment (ΔR_max) and the environmental sensitivity parameter (β) can further enhance cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. No direct analysis of group payoff, welfare, or efficiency is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The reputation mechanism is analogous to a form of social sanctioning, but it is not implemented as a costly punishment with explicit payoff consequences. Therefore, while the findings suggest that adaptive, environment-dependent sanctioning can promote cooperation, they do not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The results may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Zhang, N., Zhang, X. X., Lei, M., & Yang, Y. J. (2020). Multiagent Collaborative Governance for Targeted Poverty Alleviation from the Perspective of Stakeholders. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing support from local governments and social organizations to poverty groups can promote the willingness of poverty groups to cooperate, but excessive support from higher-level government does not necessarily lead to more cooperation or better outcomes. (2) Moderate increases in punishment (negative returns) for noncooperation by social organizations and poverty groups can encourage cooperative behavior. (3) Increasing the basic economic benefits for social organizations and poverty groups significantly increases their willingness to cooperate. The model suggests that a combination of incentives and moderate sanctions is more effective than top-down subsidies alone. However, the main outcomes are the equilibrium rates of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but it does analyze how punishment (modeled as negative returns for noncooperation) and other design parameters affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. The findings suggest that moderate punishment can increase cooperation rates, which in standard public goods games is often associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated here. The model is more complex than standard PGGs, involving three types of agents and context-specific payoffs. Thus, the paper is best used for qualitative, indirect support: it reinforces that punishment and incentives can promote cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency effects for PGG-like environments.

  findings: The study finds that introducing environment-based preference selection (parameter \alpha) and environmental heterogeneity (parameter u) into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. The combination of these mechanisms is more effective than either alone. The effect is robust across a range of parameters and network sizes. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are thus relevant for understanding cooperation dynamics but not directly for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings on cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma games with preference selection and heterogeneity may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

The link weight adjustment mechanism is adjacent to punishment/reward but does not map directly to standard punishment in PGGs. Thus, while the findings may suggest that adaptive network structure and memory can promote cooperation, they do not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

The key driver is the link weight boundary control parameter (\delta), which, when increased, allows for greater heterogeneity in social ties and promotes fairer strategies. The adjustment amplitude (\Delta) has a smaller or context-dependent effect.   findings: The study finds that the leading eight social norms for indirect reciprocity can maintain high levels of cooperation in networked populations, even when reputation and strategy updating are local rather than global. However, the ability to sustain cooperation depends on the accuracy of reputation transmission, the speed of evolutionary updating, and the specific norm used. - source: Danku, Z., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Knowing the past improves cooperation in the future. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in spatial social dilemma games (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, stag-hunt) update their strategies based on a combination of current and past payoffs, cooperation is strongly promoted compared to the standard model where only current payoffs matter. This effect is robust to the details of how the past is incorporated (weighted average or single past payoff with decaying probability). The mechanism works by slowing down the invasion of defectors into cooperative clusters, allowing cooperation to persist and spread. The effect is most pronounced when the memory window is sufficiently long or the selected past payoff is not too far in the past. The results are shown for the stationary fraction of cooperators, not for efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or closely related environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are about how memory of past payoffs (a strategy-neutral mechanism) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. While the results may be conceptually relevant for understanding how information and history affect cooperation, they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very weak, indirect context for the role of history or memory in cooperation.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Environmental-based defensive promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that allowing cooperators to take defensive actions (at a cost) in response to environmental cues (number of other cooperators) can prevent the collapse to full defection and promote higher levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the defensive cost, defensive benefit, defensive loss, and the threshold for defensive awareness. When defensive measures are sufficiently beneficial and not too costly, and when cooperators are sufficiently vigilant (high threshold and probability), the system can reach full cooperation or stable coexistence. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce exploitation (analogous to defensive or sanctioning actions) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and reports cooperation fraction rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The results suggest that the effectiveness of defensive/sanctioning mechanisms depends on their cost, benefit, and the conditions under which they are triggered, which may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in related models. However, quantitative predictions for efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Effects of defensive cooperation strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a defensive cooperation strategy (where cooperators pay a cost to reduce losses against defectors) can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in both well-mixed and structured populations, compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of this strategy depends on the balance between the defensive cost and defensive benefit. When the defensive benefit exceeds the cost, defensive cooperators can suppress defectors and allow cooperation to persist. However, excessive defensive benefit can lead to second-order free-riding by unconditional cooperators. The model shows cyclic dominance among the three strategies in structured populations. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its model is a theoretical extension of the prisoner's dilemma with a defensive cooperation strategy, which is conceptually adjacent to punishment but not equivalent. The outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not payoff-based. Therefore, while the paper suggests that self-interested defensive strategies can promote cooperation under certain conditions, it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or group payoff that can be directly used for the downstream prediction task. Its relevance is limited to mechanism discussion and indirect contextualization.

- source: Li, W. J., Jiang, L. L., & Perc, M. (2021). A limited mobility of minorities facilitates cooperation in social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model, allowing individuals to move away from defectors with low probability (limited mobility) increases the frequency of cooperation by enabling the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters. High mobility, however, undermines cooperation by disrupting network reciprocity. The authors interpret mobility away from defectors as a form of punishment, but the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The paper claims that limited mobility of minorities can promote cooperation more effectively and with less public resource cost than explicit punishment or reward, but this is not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The analogy between mobility/alienation and punishment is discussed, but there is no explicit manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how indirect forms of sanctioning (like alienation) can promote cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for the prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.

- source: Newton, P. K., & Ma, Y. (2021). Maximizing cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma evolutionary game via optimal control. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that by optimally controlling the off-diagonal elements of the prisoner's dilemma payoff matrix (interpreted as dynamically changing incentives and penalties), it is possible to minimize the defector population (maximize cooperation) over a fixed time horizon. The optimal control is bang-bang and depends on the initial state. The reduction in defectors is most efficient for small initial defector populations. The method cycles through a sequence of different games (PD, Leader, Deadlock, etc.) to achieve the optimal outcome. The approach is adaptive over multiple cycles, recalculating the optimal schedule based on the current state. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not based on experimental or empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical study of optimal control in a two-population evolutionary prisoner's dilemma, focusing on minimizing defection via dynamic changes to the payoff matrix. There is no explicit punishment mechanism, no group payoff or efficiency outcome, and the setting is not a public goods game. The findings may be conceptually relevant for understanding how dynamic incentives can shape cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Wang, Q., He, N. R., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Replicator dynamics for public goods game with resource allocation in large populations. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
