  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the coevolutionary dynamics of cooperation and environmental state are more diverse than in well-mixed populations. Depending on payoff parameters and the number of neighbors, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations, bistability (with both depleted and replete environmental states as attractors), coexistence of oscillation and dominance, or stable coexistence of cooperation and defection. The number of neighbors (degree) can either expand or shrink the basin of attraction for favorable environmental states, depending on the ratio of enhancement/degradation rates or payoff parameter ratios. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or model costly punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation and defection in a structured population with environmental feedback, but the 'punishment' parameter is the matrix payoff for mutual defection, not a sanctioning institution. The findings may inform how structural parameters (e.g., number of neighbors) and payoff ratios affect the maintenance of cooperation and environmental quality, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. L. (2018). The dynamic of general compliance with the OECD anti-bribery convention: two interpretative approaches. *Crime Law and Social Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that the enforcement of the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention is best understood through a combination of rational-choice and institutional approaches. It highlights the collective action problem inherent in international anti-bribery efforts, likening it to a public goods game with free-rider issues. The rational-choice approach explains 'under-enforcement' as a result of insufficient incentives and ineffective centralized monitoring or sanctioning. The institutional approach emphasizes the role of domestic institutional contexts and 'unconscious enforcement' by agencies like the US SEC, whose evolving missions and regulatory environments lead to increased enforcement over time. The paper suggests that decentralized monitoring involving private sector whistleblowers and inter-country competition may be more effective than centralized mechanisms. However, it does not provide empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical context for understanding how punishment, monitoring, and institutional design might affect cooperation and compliance in public-goods-game-like environments, especially at the international treaty level. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or monitoring mechanisms. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in experimental public goods games is limited to its discussion of collective action, monitoring, and sanctioning as mechanisms, rather than direct evidence. It may inform mechanism design or the interpretation of monitoring and punishment variables, but does not support direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2021). The complexity of human cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews the literature on indirect reciprocity, emphasizing how the complexity of social norms and strategies affects the evolution and maintenance of cooperation. It synthesizes theoretical and computational results showing that: (1) more complex social norms can, in principle, sustain higher cooperation, but are harder for individuals to implement; (2) the costliness of reputation spreading, the observability of interactions, and empathy are key moderators of cooperation under indirect reciprocity; (3) when complexity costs are introduced, simpler norms may outperform more complex ones in sustaining cooperation. The paper provides a proof-of-concept simulation showing that cooperation rates under different social norms decline as the cost of implementing complex strategies increases. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or direct welfare outcomes, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report or synthesize results on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, nor does it manipulate punishment or sanctions. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the complexity of social norms and the cognitive costs of implementing strategies can moderate cooperation rates in reputation-based systems. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides only indirect, contextual background, not quantitative or directly transferable evidence.

- source: Jagers, S. C., Harring, N., Löfgren, Sjöstedt, M., Alpizar, F., Brülde, B., Langlet, D., Nilsson, A., Almroth, B. C., Dupont, S., & Steffen, W. (2020). On the preconditions for large-scale collective action. *Ambio*.

  findings: The paper argues that as collective action problems become larger in scale (more actors, greater spatial/temporal distance, more complexity), spontaneous cooperation becomes less likely due to increased stressors such as anonymity, lack of accountability, heterogeneity, and uncertainty. Third-party interventions (which may include sanctions, incentives, or other mechanisms) become increasingly necessary to generate and sustain collective action. The framework is intended to guide future empirical research and policy design, but does not itself provide new empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers theoretical context: it suggests that in larger-scale settings, informal mechanisms (like peer punishment) are less effective, and formal third-party interventions (which may include sanctions or rewards) become more important for sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may diminish as group size, spatial/temporal distance, and complexity increase, unless punishment is institutionalized or externally enforced. The paper does not provide parameter-level guidance for the specific game design dimensions required for quantitative prediction.

- source: Isoni, A., & Sugden, R. (2019). Reciprocity and the Paradox of Trust in psychological game theory. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that models of reciprocal kindness, as formalized in psychological game theory (e.g., Rabin 1993, Charness & Rabin 2002, Falk & Fischbacher 2006), cannot explain mutually beneficial trust and trustworthiness in Trust Games—this is termed the 'Paradox of Trust.' The paper argues that trust and trustworthiness are better explained by a different form of reciprocity, namely reciprocal cooperation, where individuals are motivated to play their part in mutually beneficial practices conditional on others doing the same. The paper sketches how this approach can be applied to public goods games, but does not provide empirical results or efficiency data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative results that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it is conceptually relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of cooperation and the limitations of certain reciprocity models. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that models focusing solely on rewarding/punishing intentions (reciprocal kindness) may not capture the mechanisms that lead to efficient cooperation in trust or public goods games. Instead, models of reciprocal cooperation may be more appropriate for predicting when mutually beneficial outcomes (i.e., high efficiency) can arise. The paper does not address specific game design dimensions or provide data on how punishment affects efficiency.

- source: Gao, H. Y., Wang, J., Zhang, F., Li, X. P., & Xia, C. Y. (2021). Cooperation dynamics based on reputation in the mixed population with two species of strategists. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-based imitation mechanism in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game with both node-type and link-type strategists increases the frequency of cooperation, especially when the population is a balanced mix of both types. Link-type strategists can selectively cooperate or defect with neighbors, effectively punishing defectors and protecting cooperative clusters. The strength of the reputation effect and the reputation adjustment factor both positively influence cooperation rates. However, all reported outcomes are in terms of cooperation frequency and network structure, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it models a form of punishment (link-type strategists defecting against defectors) and shows that this increases cooperation rates, it does not report on efficiency, welfare, or group payoff. The model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment mechanism is endogenous to strategy choice rather than an explicit costly punishment option. Thus, the paper may provide some indirect support for the idea that targeted sanctions can promote cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., & Perc, M. (2021). Mathematical foundations of moral preferences. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that a wide range of unselfish behaviors in one-shot, anonymous economic games are better explained by a 'moral preference hypothesis'—that is, people have preferences for following their own personal norms about what is right or wrong, beyond the monetary consequences of their actions. This framework organizes and explains empirical deviations from standard social preference models in games such as the dictator game, ultimatum game (altruistic punishment), Prisoner's Dilemma (cooperation), trust game (trustworthiness), sender-receiver game (truth-telling), and trade-off games (equality vs. efficiency). The review also discusses the practical implications of targeting personal norms (rather than social or injunctive norms) in interventions to promote pro-sociality, and proposes a formal utility function incorporating personal norms. However, the paper does not provide new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions in multi-player, repeated, or public goods games. Its main contribution is theoretical: it suggests that personal moral norms are a key driver of unselfish behavior (including punishment) in one-shot, anonymous games, and that interventions targeting personal norms may be more effective than those targeting social norms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic context about why people might punish or cooperate, but not quantitative or design-specific evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Cordes, C., Elsner, W., Graebner, C., Heinrich, T., Henkel, J., Schwardt, H., Schwesinger, G., & Su, T. Y. (2021). The collapse of cooperation: the endogeneity of institutional break-up and its asymmetry with emergence. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in groups and societies is fragile and subject to collapse due to endogenous factors such as group growth, increasing complexity, cognitive limits, and the ceremonialization of institutions. Collapse is often sudden and asymmetric with respect to emergence, meaning that the conditions under which cooperation breaks down are not simply the reverse of those under which it emerges. Theoretical mechanisms include the role of group size (with a cognitive upper limit for informal cooperation), the importance of network structure (with clustered and heterogeneous networks supporting more robust cooperation), and the increasing costs of monitoring and punishment as cooperation declines. The paper also notes that high punishment cultures may be associated with lower cooperation and socio-economic thriving. However, all findings are theoretical or based on secondary synthesis; no new empirical or experimental data are presented.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group size, network structure, and the costs of monitoring and punishment may affect the emergence and collapse of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that as groups grow or as network structures become less favorable, cooperation may collapse suddenly and may not be easily restored. The paper also highlights that high punishment costs and cultures may undermine cooperation. However, because the paper is theoretical and does not report empirical data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in specific public goods game designs. Its main value is in highlighting potential moderators and mechanisms that could inform the interpretation of empirical results from other studies.

It highlights that enforcement can take the form of sanctions or rewards, but these are discussed as evolutionary strategies rather than as manipulable variables in economic games. It may inform the general expectation that enforcement can increase cooperation, but it does not specify under what game design conditions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR) this effect is positive, negative, or neutral for efficiency. For prediction tasks requiring quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Gold, N. (2020). HOW SHOULD WE RECONCILE SELF-REGARDING AND PRO-SOCIAL MOTIVATIONS? A RENAISSANCE OF DAS ADAM SMITH PROBLEM. *Social Philosophy and Policy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper proposes that motivation crowding (the phenomenon where extrinsic incentives undermine intrinsic or pro-social motivations) can be explained by changes in the framing of a situation. When a task or interaction is framed in terms of monetary gain or punishment, individuals may shift from pro-social or intrinsic motivations to self-regarding ones, reducing cooperation or helpfulness. The essay reviews evidence from behavioral economics and psychology (e.g., dictator games, prisoner's dilemma, effects of fines and rewards) to support this mechanism. It recommends that institutional designers consider how incentives and frames interact, as poorly designed incentives can backfire by crowding out desirable motivations. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance on the importance of framing and motivation crowding for predicting the effects of punishment or incentives in social dilemmas. It suggests that introducing punishment or monetary incentives can sometimes reduce cooperation or pro-social behavior, especially if these incentives shift the frame from normative/moral to gain/self-interest. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze game design dimensions, it cannot be used directly to predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design features. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for negative or counterintuitive effects of punishment/reward mechanisms, especially when they alter the perceived frame of the game.

- source: Morris, A., & Cushman, F. (2018). A COMMON FRAMEWORK FOR THEORIES OF NORM COMPLIANCE. *Social Philosophy & Policy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper synthesizes and formalizes four major theories of norm compliance—planning (folk theory), habit, internalization, and choice set construction—within a reinforcement learning framework. It argues that norm compliance can arise from explicit planning over expected rewards (including punishment/reward), from habits formed by past reinforcement, from internalized intrinsic rewards for norm compliance, or from the exclusion of norm-violating actions from consideration. The paper reviews evidence from economic games (including public goods and ultimatum games) to illustrate the persistence of norm compliance even in the absence of external enforcement, but does not report new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. The framework highlights that the mechanisms by which punishment or sanctions affect behavior may differ (e.g., via planning, habit, or internalization), and that these mechanisms have different implications for the persistence and malleability of norm compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it is useful for understanding the possible cognitive mechanisms by which punishment, reward, and norm salience may influence cooperation and compliance. The framework suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether compliance is driven by planning (sensitive to incentives), habit (sensitive to past reinforcement), or internalization (less sensitive to external incentives). For prediction tasks, this implies that the design dimensions that affect the salience, frequency, and perceived legitimacy of punishment or reward may interact with these underlying mechanisms, but the paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence for such effects.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2021). Cooperation in large-scale human societies-What, if anything, makes it unique, and how did it evolve?. *Evolutionary Anthropology*.

  findings: The paper argues that the key to sustaining cooperation in large-scale societies is the development of new institutional rules (social interaction mechanisms) that make prosocial behavior payoff-compatible for self-interested individuals, rather than a shift to decision-making mechanisms that are insensitive to payoff (e.g., strong conformity or prestige bias). It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that, in both small- and large-scale societies, humans are generally sensitive to material payoffs in social dilemmas, and that institutions such as monitoring, sanctioning, and reputation systems evolve to support cooperation. The paper highlights the importance of distinguishing between cooperative (payoff-increasing for all) and altruistic (payoff-decreasing for the actor) prosocial behaviors, and suggests that in large-scale societies, cooperation among non-relatives is typically cooperative, not altruistic, due to institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that the effect of punishment and institutional rules on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on whether those rules make cooperation payoff-compatible for self-interested agents. It suggests that, in predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, one should focus on the design of the social interaction mechanism (e.g., the structure and effectiveness of punishment, monitoring, and reward systems) and the degree to which these mechanisms align individual incentives with group welfare. However, the paper does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative relationships between game design dimensions and efficiency outcomes. Its value is in framing the importance of institutional design and payoff-sensitivity, rather than in providing parameter-specific predictions.

- source: Heath, J., & Rioux, C. (2018). Recent trends in evolutionary ethics: greenbeards!. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that many recent philosophical and scientific models that attempt to explain large-scale human cooperation by introducing commitment or punishment mechanisms (such as strong reciprocity) tacitly rely on greenbeard mechanisms, which are considered implausible in the human case due to their vulnerability to exploitation by 'falsebeards.' The authors argue that these models do not provide a satisfactory solution to the problem of human ultrasociality and that the evolution of cooperation remains an open question. The paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative outcomes relevant to efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance to the prediction task is limited to theoretical critique: it cautions that models relying on punishment to sustain cooperation may be structurally vulnerable unless they avoid greenbeard-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks based on game design dimensions and control efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, high-level theoretical context and does not inform parameter-level predictions.

  findings: The paper finds that when both competition (conflict) and transfer (recruitment) rates among three political parties are weak, coexistence of all parties is robust. As either competition or transfer increases, the system transitions to single-party or two-party dominance, with the possibility of bistable or multistable outcomes depending on initial conditions. The maintenance of coexistence is sensitive to the balance between competition and transfer. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not report any payoff or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shimura, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2018). Large group size promotes the evolution of cooperation in the mutual-aid game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in the mutual-aid game (an 'all for one' group helping structure), larger group size and more repeated interactions (rounds) both promote the evolution and stability of cooperation, as measured by the expected payoff of conditional cooperators relative to defectors. This is in contrast to the standard public goods game, where larger groups typically undermine cooperation. The effect is robust to implementation and perception errors, and is strongest under certain reputation assessment rules. The paper does not include punishment or costly sanctioning mechanisms; all incentives are mediated by reputation and conditional cooperation. The findings are directly about efficiency (payoff) outcomes, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in mutual-aid games with indirect reciprocity and reputation-based conditional cooperation (but no explicit punishment), increasing group size and number of rounds both increase the likelihood that cooperation (and thus high efficiency) will evolve and persist. The results are not directly transferable to standard public goods games with punishment, but they suggest that the structure of the game (e.g., 'all for one' vs 'all for all') and the presence of reputation-based mechanisms can strongly moderate the effect of group size and repetition on efficiency. Since punishment is not present, the paper does not inform the effect of enabling punishment per se, but it does provide a baseline for efficiency in a related class of games without punishment.

- source: Roberts, G. (2020). Honest signaling of cooperative intentions. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive

  findings: The paper finds that costly signaling of cooperative intent can evolve as an honest signal when the long-term benefits of repeated cooperation outweigh the costs of signaling. In such environments, agents who signal are more likely to be chosen as partners, and those who are chosen are more likely to cooperate, leading to higher payoffs for both signalers and choosers. The model predicts that efficiency (total payoff) is maximized when signaling costs are moderate and the number of repeated interactions is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and is based on dyadic rather than group interactions, but the findings are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can support efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms allowing individuals to signal cooperative intent and choose partners based on such signals can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the model is closely related and suggests that, in environments where reputation-building is possible and partner choice is allowed, efficiency will be higher than in environments without these mechanisms. The absence of punishment in the model means it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of costly signaling and partner choice as alternative or complementary mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling reputation-building and partner choice (analogous to some forms of communication or signaling in PGGs) can increase efficiency, especially when the cost of signaling is not prohibitive and the benefits of cooperation are high.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2021). Consensus towards Partially Cooperative Strategies in Self-Regulated Evolutionary Games on Networks. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in Stag-Hunt and Chicken games on networks, consensus on partial cooperation can be globally stable and more rewarding (in terms of average payoff) than full cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma. The stability of full cooperation, full defection, or partial cooperation depends on the self-regulation parameter relative to network degree. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, it focuses on endogenous self-regulation. Payoff at equilibrium is analytically and numerically compared across game types and equilibria.

However, because the model does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment in public goods games. The results are most relevant for understanding how internalized social preferences (modeled as self-regulation) affect efficiency, rather than the effect of exogenous punishment mechanisms.

- source: Shen, C., Du, C. P., Mu, C. J., Shia, L., & Wang, Z. (2020). Exit Option Induced by Win-Stay-Lose-Leave Rule Provides Another Route to Solve the Social Dilemma in Structured Populations. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to exit the game (become inactive) according to a win-stay-lose-leave rule, with an associated exit cost, fundamentally alters the dynamics of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. When the exit cost is intermediate, inactive players form a buffer at the boundaries of cooperative clusters, protecting cooperators and enabling the expansion of cooperation, which leads to high average payoffs for cooperators and, by implication, higher group welfare. When exit is free (zero cost), too many players become inactive, breaking network reciprocity and causing cooperation and average payoffs to collapse. When exit is very costly, only a few players become inactive, and cooperation can survive but not dominate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcomes are cooperation rate and average payoffs for different strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option and no punishment or reward. However, it provides indirect evidence about how allowing players to opt out (with varying costs) can affect cooperation and average payoffs. The findings suggest that intermediate costs for exit can maximize cooperation and average payoffs, while free exit is detrimental. For the prediction task, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of exit/opt-out mechanisms rather than punishment. It does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or analyze any of the standard public goods game punishment dimensions.

- source: Sun, H. J., & Bigoni, M. (2018). A fine rule from a brutish world? An experiment on endogenous punishment institution and trust. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that influence-based preferential selection and the coevolution of game type and strategy can significantly increase cooperation rates and the average payoff of cooperators, especially in the Snowdrift game regime. Higher influence growth factor and higher 'sucker's payoff' (sigma) both lead to higher average payoffs for cooperators and greater resource accumulation. The introduction of a resource protection mechanism further increases cooperation and makes resource distribution more equitable. While explicit punishment is not modeled, the results suggest that mechanisms which promote cooperation (such as influence-based selection) also improve group-level payoffs and resource efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for predicting efficiency in public-goods-like environments where cooperation is promoted by mechanisms other than explicit punishment, such as preferential selection based on influence and resource coevolution. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness of mechanisms that promote cooperation (e.g., higher influence growth, more favorable game parameters) will increase both cooperation rates and average payoffs, analogous to increased efficiency. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the direct transferability to punishment-enabled PGGs is limited. The results are most informative for adjacent mechanisms (e.g., reputation, influence, resource redistribution) rather than direct punishment.

Since the paper does not include punishment or sanctions, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding the role of game structure and timing in cooperation and efficiency.

- source: Johnson, T., & Smirnov, O. (2018). Inequality as information: Wealth homophily facilitates the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive

  findings: The paper finds that in a well-mixed population playing repeated one-shot prisoners' dilemma games, a strategy that uses wealth homophily (COEQUALS) can successfully invade and dominate a population of defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs (efficiency). The success of COEQUALS is robust to noise, population size, number of rounds, and the presence of certain mimics. The key moderator is the ability to observe the wealth of a sufficient portion of the population: when COEQUALS agents can view at least 10-20% of the population's wealth, the strategy spreads widely and average payoffs increase. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings suggest that visible inequality can serve as an informational mechanism to support cooperation and efficiency, even in the absence of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma environments where agents can condition their cooperation on visible wealth (past payoffs), but it does not address punishment or sanctions. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling positive assortment (e.g., wealth homophily) can substitute for punishment in supporting high efficiency. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is less directly relevant, but it does highlight the importance of informational cues and partner selection. The results also indicate that efficiency is robust to variations in player count, number of rounds, and noise, but is sensitive to the ability to observe others' payoffs. If a public goods game allows for similar informational mechanisms, efficiency may be high even without punishment.

- source: Vorlaufer, T. (2019). Effects of double-anonymity on pro- and anti-social behavior: Experimental evidence from a lab in the field. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that generalized reciprocity (helping if helped) can evolve and persist in structured populations, even without active assortment or reputation mechanisms, provided that social interactions are limited to local neighborhoods. The expected payoff for reciprocators can exceed that of non-reciprocators when reciprocators are sufficiently clustered, leading to the dominance of cooperative strategies in the stationary distribution. The model is adjacent to public goods games but does not include group-level public goods, punishment, or sanctioning. Efficiency is analyzed in terms of expected payoffs for different strategies, and the results show that network structure and the independence of spontaneous helping and reciprocation are key moderators of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, as it does not model punishment or group-level public goods. However, it provides theoretical evidence that local interaction structure and the independence of helping and reciprocation can strongly affect the evolution of cooperation and the resulting payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that network structure and the possibility of reciprocation (even without punishment) can be important moderators of efficiency, but the absence of punishment in the model limits its direct applicability to the target prediction task.

- source: Wang, L., Ye, S. Q., Cheong, K. H., Bao, W., & Xie, N. G. (2018). The role of emotions in spatial prisoner's dilemma game with voluntary participation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial voluntary prisoner's dilemma game with emotional profile imitation, the average payoff (efficiency) is high when the temptation to defect (T) is low (T=1.0, average payoff 0.92, cooperation rate ~95%), and low when T is high (T=1.25, average payoff 0.3, cooperation rate ~1%). The system exhibits order-chaos-order transitions as T increases, with bifurcations in strategy and emotional profile distributions. The presence of loners and the structure of emotional imitation modulate the system's efficiency, but no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The findings are relevant for understanding efficiency in voluntary participation social dilemmas, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods or similar games. It is not directly useful for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present or analyzed. However, it does provide informative context on how efficiency varies with temptation and emotional imitation in voluntary participation dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2019). Nonconformity of cooperators promotes the emergence of pure altruism in tag-based multi-agent networked systems. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in tag-based multi-agent systems, the combination of spatial structure (small-world networks) and the presence of nonconforming (free) altruists, together with conformity-biased transmission of other strategies, is necessary for the stable dominance of pure altruism (unconditional cooperation). Conformity alone, or spatial structure alone, is insufficient to produce this outcome. The model shows that even a small fraction (>1%) of nonconforming altruists can drive the system toward near-complete altruism, resulting in higher mean fitness and group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are robust across a range of network parameters and conversion sensitivities. The results are supported by both agent-based simulations and mathematical analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about the role of conformity, spatial structure, and unconditional cooperation in evolutionary games with tag-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does provide a strong theoretical and computational baseline for understanding how other mechanisms (conformity, spatial structure, nonconformity) can promote efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment. If a model or prediction task involves tag-based cooperation, conformity, or spatial structure, the findings here suggest that these factors can be powerful moderators of efficiency, but the effect of punishment would need to be assessed from other sources.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Competition and partnership between conformity and payoff-based imitations in social dilemmas. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the coevolution of payoff-based and conformity-based learning protocols in spatial social dilemma games can dramatically increase the parameter space where full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is achieved, especially in the snowdrift and stag-hunt game regions. In the snowdrift game, the coevolutionary model allows for full cooperation at higher temptation and sucker's payoff values than the classic payoff-driven model. In the stag-hunt game, alliances between different types of cooperators can reverse the direction of evolution, leading to full cooperation in regions where defection would otherwise dominate. In the prisoner's dilemma, the coevolutionary model does not improve cooperation. The main outcome is that diversity in learning protocols can enhance collective payoff and efficiency, but this is achieved without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about how alternative mechanisms (specifically, the coevolution of learning protocols) can affect efficiency and cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (such as conformity-based imitation) can also expand the region of high efficiency, but it does not inform the effect of punishment per se. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the broader context of cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Li, C., Xu, H. D., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Synergistic effects of self-optimization and imitation rules on the evolution of cooperation in the investor sharing game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the proposed dynamic strategy updating rule can substantially increase the density of cooperators in the investor sharing game, especially at low aspiration levels. The self-optimization rule increases the probability that defectors with high investment become cooperators, which then promotes further imitation of cooperation. Lower market monopoly (parameter alpha) and more random network structure also boost cooperation, but the effect of network randomness is stronger. Average payoffs for cooperators can exceed those for defectors at high cooperation rates, but group efficiency is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on cooperation rates and average payoffs under different strategy updating rules and aspiration levels may be of indirect interest for understanding how endogenous learning rules affect cooperation, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report group efficiency as defined for the prediction task.

- source: Li, B., Zhao, X. W., & Xia, H. X. (2019). Promotion of cooperation by Hybrid Migration mechanisms in the Spatial Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that mixing success-driven migration with mean-payoff-driven or escaping-defector-driven migration (hybrid migration mechanisms) can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma game, especially under certain population densities and mixture levels. In some cases, these mechanisms also lead to higher average payoffs for individuals. The effect is sensitive to the mixture parameter and population density. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled, and the main focus is on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the main outcomes are cooperation rates and, to a lesser extent, average payoff in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma context. The findings may be of limited indirect value for understanding how migration or mobility affects cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Li, Q. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Han, J. D., & Zhang, J. L. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics in division of labor games on cycle networks. *European Journal of Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in division of labor games on cycle networks, the update rule (BD, DB, or mixed) has a major impact on the speed at which efficient strategies (interpreted as high-quality task allocation) spread through the population. Under strong selection, BD updating leads to fixation times that scale linearly with population size, while DB updating leads to quadratic or cubic scaling, meaning much slower convergence. The mixed rule interpolates between these extremes. The fixation probability and time depend on the payoff matrix parameters, but the presence or absence of punishment is not considered. The results are relevant for understanding how structural and dynamic features of the game affect the emergence of efficient collective behavior, but do not directly address efficiency as a payoff ratio or the effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. However, it does offer indirect insight into how update rules and network structure can affect the speed and likelihood of efficient collective behavior emerging in games with a cooperative dilemma structure. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment and for considering the role of update mechanisms and network topology.

  findings: The paper finds that incorporating a preference for social norm compliance into a one-shot prisoner's dilemma can generate cooperative behavior as an equilibrium outcome, even without repeated interaction, communication, or punishment. The likelihood of cooperation depends on the strength of the disutility from norm violation. However, the non-cooperative outcome is always an equilibrium as well. The model is presented as a theoretical explanation for observed cooperation in experiments, but does not itself report empirical results or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.

It does not include punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and is focused on a two-player, one-shot prisoner's dilemma. Its main contribution is to show that social norm preferences can support cooperation in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as a reminder that cooperation can arise from norm compliance alone, and that the absence of punishment does not necessarily imply zero cooperation. It does not inform how punishment affects efficiency or how game design dimensions moderate that effect.

When only pro-social reward is allowed, defectors tend to dominate; when only anti-social reward is allowed, cooperators can dominate. The model does not include punishment or negative sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The results are based on simulations and theoretical analysis of cooperation rates, not on empirical or experimental data.

The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) affect cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is adjacent to, but not the same as, a public goods game, and the outcome measures are not payoff-based.

  findings: The paper finds that when players in a spatial prisoner's dilemma use averaged payoff information from their neighborhood (or from random samples of the population) to inform strategy updating, the stationary level of cooperation increases compared to the traditional model where only the immediate neighbor's payoff is considered. The effect is stronger as the weight given to averaged information increases, and as the size of the neighborhood or sample increases (with diminishing returns). The mechanism is robust to both regular and random interaction topologies. However, the study does not involve punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.

- source: Takesue, H. (2019). Roles of mutation rate and co-existence of multiple strategy updating rules in evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that introducing aspiration-based updating into a population of imitators generally reduces cooperation, especially when mutation is present. Mutation itself also reduces cooperation, particularly in populations dominated by imitators. However, when mutation rates are high and aspiration levels are low, the coexistence of both updating rules can lead to higher cooperation than would be expected from the weighted average of homogeneous populations. The paper does not analyze efficiency, payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency outcomes. Its findings about mutation and updating rule heterogeneity may be contextually relevant for understanding the robustness of cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted heavily in the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

However, the paper does not include punishment, reward, or direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Tan, S. L. (2019). Proximity inheritance explains the evolution of cooperation under natural selection and mutation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that proximity inheritance in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model leads to the emergence and maintenance of high levels of cooperation, even in the presence of mutation. Cooperation is favored when offspring are placed within the neighborhood of their parents, and the effect is robust to variations in temptation of defection, selection intensity, and mutation rate. The model demonstrates that dynamic clustering of cooperators can arise, and that cooperation collapses if proximity inheritance is removed (i.e., if offspring are placed randomly or at the site of the dead individual). However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma rather than a public goods game. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how spatial structure and inheritance of proximity can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, S. L., Hong, L. J., Geng, Y. N., & Shen, C. (2020). Popularity-driven fitness calculation promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that integrating popularity into the fitness function (via a tunable parameter alpha) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can significantly promote cooperation. When alpha is positive, popular individuals (those whose neighbors share their strategy) gain higher fitness, leading to the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters and, in some cases, full cooperation. When alpha is negative, isolated cooperators can persist but do not dominate. The results are supported by both numerical simulations and mean-field theoretical analysis. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, nor does it include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that mechanisms which enhance the fitness of popular strategies can promote cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment. The game studied is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome of interest is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that making network edge weights depend on recent cooperation (reputation) strongly increases the fraction of cooperators in the population, compared to a standard unweighted network. The effect is strongest when only the most recent action is used for reputation (short memory). Longer memory reduces the benefit for cooperation. The mechanism is that cooperators in clusters gain higher edge weights and thus higher payoffs, helping them resist defectors. However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. - source: Loch-Temzelides, T. (2021). Walrasian equilibrium behavior in nature. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the trading behavior of mycorrhizal fungi in exchanging phosphorus for carbon with plant roots is consistent with Walrasian equilibrium, as revealed by the satisfaction of GARP in the observed data. This implies that the resulting allocations are Pareto efficient. The study provides a proof-of-concept that biological markets can achieve efficient outcomes as predicted by general equilibrium theory, even in the absence of human participants or explicit institutional mechanisms. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and revealed preference tests using data from a prior biological experiment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not study public goods, punishment, or sanctions. Its findings are relevant to the efficiency of biological market exchanges and the applicability of Walrasian equilibrium, but not to the design dimensions or efficiency outcomes of PGGs with or without punishment. It provides no evidence on how punishment, player count, or other PGG design features affect efficiency.

- source: Capraro, V., Perc, M., & Vilone, D. (2020). Lying on networks: The role of structure and topology in promoting honesty. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that network structure has a nontrivial effect on the evolution of honesty and lying in the sender-receiver game. For black lies and altruistic white lies, honesty is more likely to evolve in small-world networks and, to a lesser extent, in the one-dimensional ring, compared to well-mixed populations. For Pareto white lies, lying is more likely to evolve in small-world networks. For spiteful lies, honesty always prevails regardless of network. The outcomes are reported in terms of the densities of liars and believers, not in terms of payoffs or efficiency. The sender-receiver game is fundamentally different from public goods games and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
